TL;DR: A 3D CT-to-PET translation framework using latent Brownian Bridge Diffusion (BBDM) with VAE and contrastive learning achieves improved synthesis of PET images from CT scans, offering a promising approach for virtual imaging with better lesion preservation than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PET imaging provides crucial functional/metabolic information for cancer diagnosis and treatment planning, but its widespread use is limited by high radiation exposure, elevated costs, and restricted availability. CT-to-PET translation using deep learning offers a promising solution to synthesize PET-like information from readily available CT images, though accurately modeling the large cross-modal gap remains challenging.

Method: The method consists of two stages: (1) A Variational Autoencoder (VAE) is trained on paired CT-PET patches with contrastive learning to improve latent alignment between anatomical (CT) and metabolic (PET) representations; (2) A Latent Brownian Bridge Diffusion Model (BBDM) is trained in the latent space to translate CT latent representations into PET counterparts, followed by decoding and stitching to reconstruct 3D PET volumes.

Result: Quantitative evaluation on two publicly available datasets using image fidelity and lesion-level PET-specific metrics demonstrates improved performance compared with competing methods. The approach shows enhanced PET signal fidelity, better preservation of clinically relevant uptake patterns, and improved performance in preserving small-lesion metabolic activation.

Conclusion: The proposed BBDM-based CT-to-PET translation framework demonstrates improved performance over competing methods, with better PET signal fidelity, preservation of clinically relevant uptake patterns, and enhanced small-lesion metabolic activation preservation. This approach paves the way for virtual imaging applications in cancer diagnosis and treatment planning.

Abstract: Computed tomography (CT) and positron emission tomography (PET) provide complementary anatomical and functional information for cancer diagnosis and treatment planning. However, the widespread use of PET is limited by high radiation exposure, elevated costs, and restricted availability. To address these limitations, deep learning-based CT-to-PET translation has emerged as a promising approach for synthesizing PET-like information directly from CT images, although accurately modeling the large cross-modal gap remains challenging. In this work, we propose a 3D CT-to-PET translation framework based on latent Brownian Bridge Diffusion (BBDM). The method consists of two stages. First, a Variational Autoencoder (VAE) is trained on paired CT-PET patches, integrating contrastive learning to improve latent alignment between anatomical and metabolic representations. Second, a BBDM is trained in the latent space to translate CT latent representations into their corresponding PET counterparts. The translated PET latents are then decoded and stitched to reconstruct the final 3D PET volume. We evaluate the proposed approach on two publicly available datasets. Quantitative results based on image fidelity and lesion-level PET-specific metrics demonstrate improved performance compared with competing methods. In particular, the proposed approach improves PET signal fidelity, better preserves clinically relevant uptake patterns, and shows improved performance in preserving small-lesion metabolic activation, paving the way for virtual imaging applications.

</details>


### [24] [VideoTok4D: A 4D-Aware Video Tokenizer for Compact World Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12874)
*Xinyi Chen,Hanxin Zhu,Xijun Wang,Xingrui Wang,Sen Liang,Xin Li,Zhibo Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VideoTok4D is a novel 4D-aware video tokenizer that addresses the limitation of current approaches treating videos as 2D image sequences. It uses spatiotemporal disentanglement to separate static and dynamic tokens, a track-aware dynamic attention mechanism for motion consistency, and Co4DGen (a diffusion prior) for efficient 4D scene generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video tokenization paradigms remain within the 2D visual domain, treating videos as image sequences rather than observations of an underlying dynamic 3D world. This observation-centric bias limits the capacity to compactly represent real-world 4D scenes.

Method: Three key designs: 1) Spatiotemporal disentanglement strategy that factorizes videos into static and dynamic tokens for holistic world modeling; 2) Track-aware dynamic attention mechanism that aggregates trajectory-aligned cues to promote cross-view motion consistency; 3) Co4DGen, a diffusion prior learned over the resulting token space for efficient 4D scene generation.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance while requiring up to 4 orders of magnitude less storage than dense 4D representations. The compact token space substantially shortens diffusion sequences, enabling efficient generation.

Conclusion: VideoTok4D successfully enables compact 4D world representation by disentangling static and dynamic components, with the diffusion prior enabling efficient 4D scene generation.

Abstract: Video tokenizers have emerged as a cornerstone of modern video modeling, underpinning progress in compression, reconstruction and generation by mapping high-dimensional visual signals into compact latent spaces. However, despite this progress, current tokenization paradigms largely remain within the 2D visual domain, treating videos as image sequences rather than observations of an underlying dynamic 3D world. Consequently, the learned tokens inherit this observation-centric bias, limiting their capacity to compactly represent real-world 4D scenes. To mitigate this issue, we propose VideoTok4D, a novel 4D-aware video tokenizer for compact world representation. Specifically, our approach comprises three key designs: 1) a spatiotemporal disentanglement strategy that factorizes videos into static and dynamic tokens for holistic world modeling; 2) a track-aware dynamic attention mechanism that aggregates trajectory-aligned cues to promote cross-view motion consistency; and 3) Co4DGen, a diffusion prior learned over the resulting VideoTok4D token space for efficient 4D scene generation. Extensive experiments have demonstrated that our proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance while requiring up to 4 orders of magnitude less storage than dense 4D representations. Moreover, the compact token space substantially shortens diffusion sequences, enabling efficient generation.

</details>


### [25] [Learning Sign Language Recognition under Label Noise: A Study of Noise-Robust Losses for Isolated and Continuous Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12885)
*Akihisa Shitara,Yoichi Ochiai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates robust loss functions (SCE, GCE) for sign language recognition to handle noisy labels in both isolated (ISLR) and continuous (CSLR) settings. The study tests these losses on ASL Citizen and PHOENIX-2014 datasets with multiple backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard cross-entropy loss treats single labels as ground truth in ISLR, while frame-level auxiliary classifiers in CSLR rely on pseudo-labels that are inherently noisy. Both scenarios suffer from label noise: stylistic variations in ISLR blur annotations, and CSLR lacks temporal boundaries forcing unreliable pseudo-labels. The authors seek robust alternatives to cross-entropy that can handle such noise.

Method: The researchers applied symmetric cross-entropy (SCE) and generalized cross-entropy (GCE) - robust alternatives to cross-entropy from image classification - to the single-label classifier preceding the connectionist temporal classification. They conducted experiments on ASL Citizen dataset with injected symmetric noise using three backbones (ST-GCN with three seeds). For CSLR, they used PHOENIX-2014 dataset with frame-level auxiliary classifiers at lambda_aux = 25.

Result: On ASL Citizen with symmetric noise: robust losses cost at most 2.5 percentage points when labels are clean, but beat CE by 2.9-10.0 pt in all six conditions at noise rate 0.2. GCE showed greater gains but its optimal q parameter did not transfer across backbones, while one SCE setting worked across all nine conditions. Both SCE and GCE varied 2-11 times more than CE across runs, indicating instability. On PHOENIX-2014 (CSLR): no gain was observed due to systematic assignment bias in frame-level targets. With lambda_aux=25, pseudo-label CE auxiliary raised word error rate above baseline on VAC, CorrNet and SlowFastSign, but GCE/SCE improved on CE by 1.7-3.2 pt. After matching initial gradient magnitudes, the gap narrowed to 0.4-0.9 pt.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that robust losses can improve performance under noisy label conditions in ISLR, with SCE offering more consistent transfer across backbones than GCE. However, the improvements in CSLR could not be separated from the effect of weight (lambda_aux), as lowering the CE weight alone already beat the baseline. The CSLR results represent a diagnosis of a single configuration rather than a general improvement. Key limitations include testing only symmetric noise and limited multi-seed evaluation.

Abstract: In sign language recognition, the isolated (ISLR) classification loss treats a single label as ground truth, as does the frame-level auxiliary classifier over pseudo-labels we add to continuous (CSLR) methods, which lack one. Stylistic variation blurs ISLR annotation and the lack of temporal boundaries in CSLR forces pseudo-labels; both are noisy. We therefore apply symmetric and generalized cross entropy (SCE, GCE), robust alternatives to cross entropy (CE) from image classification, not to connectionist temporal classification but to the preceding single-label classifier. On ASL Citizen with injected symmetric noise on three backbones (three seeds for ST-GCN), robust losses cost at most 2.5 pt when labels are clean and beat CE by 2.9-10.0 pt in all six conditions at noise rate 0.2, one of which only after q was re-selected on dev. GCE gains more, but its optimal q does not transfer across backbones, whereas one SCE setting works in all nine conditions; both vary 2-11 times more than CE across runs, so a favorable point estimate does not establish stability. For CSLR (PHOENIX-2014) we report no gain; our frame-level targets carry a systematic assignment bias, making that study a diagnosis of a single configuration. At lambda_aux = 25 the pseudo-label CE auxiliary raises word error rate above the no-auxiliary baseline on VAC, CorrNet and SlowFastSign, and GCE/SCE improve on CE by 1.7-3.2 pt (three of six conditions return below that baseline). However, the three losses differ by more than an order of magnitude in effective gradient at a common lambda_aux: matching the initial gradient shrinks the gap to 0.4-0.9 pt, and lowering the CE weight alone already beats that baseline, so neither the degradation nor the improvement can be separated from the effect of the weight. We use only symmetric noise; multi-seed evaluation covers only ST-GCN and VAC isolated.

</details>


### [26] [Beyond Accuracy: Uncertainty-Guided Boundary Refinement for Reliable Biomedical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12892)
*Anima Kujur*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RABR-Net is a two-stage framework for trustworthy biomedical image segmentation that combines a UNet++ EfficientNet-B4 base segmenter with a gated residual refiner. The method uses multiple uncertainty measures (predictive entropy, TTA variance, margin uncertainty, probability gradients, soft boundary cues) to create a boundary-aware reliability representation that guides selective correction of uncertain boundary pixels while preserving confident regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep segmentation models may remain uncertain or overconfident near ambiguous boundary regions even when achieving strong Dice scores. In blood-smear microscopy, cytoplasm and nucleus contours are clinically important but current methods lack reliability awareness at boundaries. The goal is to improve boundary delineation while maintaining overlap accuracy.

Method: Two-stage framework: (1) Strong UNet++ EfficientNet-B4 base segmenter produces initial class probabilities and logits. (2) Multiple uncertainty measures (predictive entropy, test-time augmentation variance, margin uncertainty, probability gradients, soft boundary cues) are combined into a boundary-aware reliability representation. This representation guides a gated residual refiner that selectively corrects uncertain boundary pixels while preserving confident regions.

Result: Improvements on held-out test set: Dice from 0.9602 to 0.9614, Boundary Dice from 0.3448 to 0.3611, HD95 from 3.0354 to 2.8274. Statistical analysis confirms significant improvements in Dice, Boundary Dice, and HD95. Qualitative results show learned gate concentrates around uncertain cytoplasm and nucleus boundaries, with correction maps confirming localized boundary refinement.

Conclusion: RABR-Net provides an interpretable and reliability-focused strategy for boundary-sensitive biomedical image segmentation. The framework successfully improves boundary delineation while maintaining overlap accuracy. Calibration does not automatically improve after refinement, but the method offers interpretable uncertainty-aware boundary refinement.

Abstract: Accurate biomedical image segmentation requires not only high global overlap but also reliable delineation of clinically meaningful boundaries. In blood-smear microscopy, cytoplasm and nucleus contours provide the structural basis for downstream morphology analysis; however, deep segmentation models may remain uncertain or overconfident near ambiguous boundary regions even when achieving strong Dice scores. This work proposes a Reliability-Aware Boundary Refinement Network (RABR-Net), a two-stage framework for trustworthy image segmentation. A strong UNet++ EfficientNet-B4 base segmenter first produces initial class probabilities and logits. Predictive entropy, test-time augmentation variance, margin uncertainty, probability gradients, and soft boundary cues are then combined into a boundary-aware reliability representation. This representation guides a gated residual refiner that selectively corrects uncertain boundary pixels while preserving confident regions of the base prediction. The framework is evaluated using overlap accuracy, class-wise Dice, Boundary Dice, HD95/ASSD, calibration, risk--coverage analysis, robustness under image perturbations, qualitative correction maps, and paired statistical testing. On the held-out test set, the proposed method improves Dice from 0.9602 to 0.9614, Boundary Dice from 0.3448 to 0.3611, and HD95 from 3.0354 to 2.8274 compared with the cached base prediction. Statistical analysis confirms significant improvements in Dice, Boundary Dice, and HD95. Qualitative results show that the learned gate concentrates around uncertain cytoplasm and nucleus boundaries, and correction maps confirm localized boundary refinement. Although calibration does not automatically improve after refinement, the proposed framework provides an interpretable and reliability-focused strategy for boundary-sensitive biomedical image segmentation.

</details>


### [27] [UniPart: Towards Zero-shot Language-Grounded 3D Part Segmentation for Embodied Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12898)
*Xinqiang Yu,Zekun qi,Jiawei He,Wenyao Zhang,Xuchuan Chen,Guaocai Yao,Li Yi,Zhaoxiang Zhang,He Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UniPart is a text-conditioned 3D part segmentation method that enables free-form text phrases to select functional parts on point clouds, using a cross-modal 3D Transformer conditioned on CLIP embeddings, trained on a large-scale dataset (LangPart-1M) and fine-tuned on manually labeled data (LangPart-4K).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-grained robotic manipulation requires understanding object parts, not just whole objects. Existing 3D foundation models are either generalized but object-aware, or part-aware but limited to closed-set taxonomies, which weakens zero-shot transfer capability.

Method: UniPart uses a feed-forward cross-modal 3D Transformer that conditions on CLIP text embeddings. Training data includes LangPart-1M with 160K+ Objaverse assets and 8M text-to-part pairs generated via multi-view consistent part generation, plus LangPart-4K manually labeled subset for fine-tuning and evaluation.

Result: UniPart achieves strong zero-shot results on open-vocabulary part benchmarks and successfully transfers to language-conditioned part grasping in real-world robotic manipulation.

Conclusion: The proposed UniPart enables open-vocabulary text-conditioned 3D part segmentation with zero-shot transfer capability, bridging the gap between 3D vision and language for robotic manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Fine-grained robotic manipulation depends on understanding parts, not only whole objects. Existing 3D foundation models tend to be either generalized but object-aware, or part-aware but limited to closed-set taxonomies, which weakens zero-shot transfer. We study text-conditioned 3D part segmentation, where a free-form phrase selects a functional part on point cloud. We introduce UniPart, a feed-forward cross-modal 3D Transformer that conditions CLIP text embedding. To scale supervision, we build LangPart-1M with 160K+ Objaverse assets and 8M text to part pairs using multi-view consistent part generation. We further manually label a high-quality subset, LangPart-4K, for fine-tuning and evaluation. UniPart achieves strong zero-shot results on open-vocabulary part benchmarks and transfers to language-conditioned part grasping in real world.

</details>


### [28] [Parallel Training Using a CNN-DNN Architecture for Accelerated Development of Diagnostic Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12902)
*Janine Weber-Hamacher,Astha Jaiswal,Philipp Fervers,Dorotya Móré,Athanasios Giannakis,Ricarda Fischbach,Andreas Michael Bucher,Rahil Shahzad,Jonathan Kottlors,Thorsten Persigehl,Axel Klawonn*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study develops a hybrid CNN-DNN model using image decomposition and parallel training to assist radiologists in COVID-19 diagnosis from CT scans. Using 300 CT scans from 3 German centers, the approach achieved up to 76.67% test accuracy with DenseNet121 and 31x faster training time with 3D CNN.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for efficient training of large deep learning models to cope with large or dynamically growing disease data, particularly in pandemic situations like COVID-19, where rapid model development is crucial.

Method: A hybrid CNN-DNN network based on image decomposition and localization that supports parallel training. 156 models with three architectures (including DenseNet121 and 3D CNN) were trained using 4×4×1 and 4×4×2 subdomains.

Result: DenseNet121 with 4×4×1 subdomains achieved 88.78% training, 76.67% validation, 76.03% test accuracy. 3D CNN achieved 87.72% training, 76.82% validation, 74.86% test accuracy. The strongest training time reduction was 31x with 3D CNN and 4×4×2 subdomains.

Conclusion: The parallel training approach improves both efficiency and performance, enabling rapid model development for pandemic preparedness.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence has shown promise in assisting radiologists in imaging-based diagnosis across a wide range of diseases. Efficient training of large deep learning models is essential to cope with extremely large data sets or dynamically growing disease data, like in a pandemic like situation. In this retrospective study, we collected 300 CT scans from COVID-19 and non-COVID-19 pneumonia patients from three different centers in Germany. We investigated a hybrid CNN-DNN network model based on image decomposition and localization that naturally supports parallel and efficient training of deep learning models. In total, 156 models with three different architectures were trained to capture features at different levels resulting in 12 patient-level COVID-19 diagnosis models. Diagnostic performance as well as time saving were measured.
  The highest accuracy was obtained from DenseNet121 and 3D CNN models with a parallel CNN-DNN approach, resulting in $88.78\%$ training, $76.67\%$ validation and $76.03\%$ test accuracy for the DenseNet121 with $4\times4\times1$ subdomains and $87.72\%$ training, $76.82\%$ validation and $74.86\%$ test accuracy, respectively, for the 3D CNN with $4\times4\times1$ subdomains. The strongest reduction in parallel training time by a factor of $31$ was observed for the 3D CNN model and $4\times4\times2$ subdomains.
  Our parallel training approach improves efficiency as well as performance enabling rapid model development, among others crucial for pandemic preparedness.

</details>


### [29] [PA-CDM: Position-Aware Character Detection Matching for Evaluating Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12917)
*Shiliang Luo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses evaluation blind spots in handwritten mathematical expression recognition (HMER) by proposing PA-CDM, a position-aware metric that combines character detection matching with position-forest encoding, along with a benchmark dataset (StructPerturb v2.0) and a cross-metric consistency protocol. PA-CDM achieves the highest correlation (rho=0.9535) with human judgments among seven automatic metrics, approaching LLM-judge performance (rho=0.9613) at minimal cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current HMER evaluation metrics have critical blind spots: exact-match rates and string-similarity metrics ignore error locations; render-based CDM is position-blind; tree-edit metrics penalize rewrites outside parser normalization. These lead to inconsistent scores (e.g., CDM scores 0.8595 vs 0.6253 for position-aware scoring on fraction-operand swaps; tree-edit scores 0.8552 vs render-based 1.0 for structural errors).

Method: The authors propose: (1) PA-CDM coupling character detection matching with position-forest encoding and divergence-level weighting; (2) StructPerturb v2.0 benchmark with 1,340 controlled perturbation pairs across 15 type-intensity cells; (3) cross-metric consistency protocol combining sensitivity matrix, human study, and LLM-judge calibration. Evaluation involves six annotators judging 990 samples.

Result: PA-CDM attains the highest correlation with human judgments among seven automatic metrics (Spearman rho=0.9535, n=990). An LLM judge shows slightly higher correlation (rho=0.9613) but is costly, nondeterministic, and API-dependent. PA-CDM approximates LLM performance at zero marginal cost with deterministic, diagnosable behavior.

Conclusion: PA-CDM successfully addresses position-blindness in render-based CDM and normalization-coverage issues in tree-edit metrics, achieving near-LLM accuracy in human correlation at minimal computational cost. The benchmark StructPerturb v2.0 and cross-metric protocol provide standardized evaluation frameworks for HMER metric development.

Abstract: Handwritten mathematical expression recognition (HMER) is conventionally scored by exact-match rates and string-similarity metrics that are blind to where an error occurs: two predictions with identical token-error counts receive identical scores whether they misplace a subscript or swap the operands of a fraction. Render-based character detection matching (CDM) aligns glyphs robustly but remains position-blind---on controlled fraction-operand swaps it scores 0.8595 where position-aware scoring yields 0.6253. Tree-edit metrics exhibit a complementary blind spot: rewrites outside the parser's normalization coverage are penalized as structural errors (0.8552 where render-based metrics score 1.0). We propose PA-CDM, a position-aware metric that couples character detection matching with position-forest encoding and divergence-level weighting; StructPerturb v2.0, a frozen benchmark of 1,340 controlled perturbation pairs across 15 type--intensity cells; and a cross-metric consistency protocol combining a sensitivity matrix, a human study, and LLM-judge calibration. In a six-annotator study, PA-CDM attains the highest correlation with human judgments among seven automatic metrics (Spearman rho=0.9535, n=990). A frontier LLM judge correlates slightly higher (rho=0.9613) but is costly, nondeterministic, and API-dependent; PA-CDM approaches it at zero marginal cost with deterministic, diagnosable behavior.

</details>


### [30] [Input Resolution Matters: Real-Time Object Detection Latency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12920)
*Qingyang Zhang,Fumio Machida,Laura Carnevali*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a resolution-aware latency modeling approach for object detection pipelines, treating total latency as the convolution of preprocessing, inference, and postprocessing stage distributions. The model is validated using YOLOv11n on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX with COCO2017 images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop a theoretically grounded and lightweight method for studying resolution-dependent latency distributions in object detection pipelines. Existing approaches likely use fixed parameters that don't account for how image resolution affects each processing stage, which can lead to inaccurate latency predictions.

Method: The authors model total latency as the convolution of preprocessing, inference, and postprocessing distributions under an independence approximation. Each stage is modeled using parametric distributions (Exponential, Erlang, Normal, Gamma) with parameters expressed as functions of source-image resolution. The probability density of total latency is obtained through convolution of stage-wise densities, and the CDF provides end-to-end detection time distribution.

Result: The results show that resolution-aware parameterization improves distributional approximation compared to fixed-parameter baselines, particularly for more flexible Normal and Gamma models. However, the quality of fit remains distribution-dependent. The approach was evaluated using Kolmogorov-Smirnov, Anderson-Darling, and Cramér-von Mises statistics.

Conclusion: The contribution is a theoretically grounded and lightweight formulation for studying resolution-dependent latency distributions in measured object detection pipelines. The approach successfully captures resolution effects and provides accurate latency predictions, though performance varies depending on the chosen distribution model.

Abstract: We model total latency as the convolution of preprocessing, inference, and postprocessing distributions under a simplifying independence approximation, with selected stage parameters expressed as functions of source-image resolution. Under this assumption, the probability density of the total latency is the convolution of the stage-wise densities, and its cumulative distribution function (CDF) provides the distribution of end-to-end detection time. Each stage is modeled by a parametric distribution (e.g., Exponential, Erlang, Normal, Gamma), with parameters expressed as functions of the source-image resolution. Experiments with YOLOv11n on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX using COCO2017 images across multiple resolutions assess the proposed models against fixed-parameter baselines using Kolmogorov Smirnov, Anderson Darling, and Cramér von Mises statistics. The results indicate that resolution-aware parameterization can improve distributional approximation in the measured setting, particularly for the more flexible Normal and Gamma models, while the quality of fit remains distribution dependent. Our contribution is a theoretically grounded and lightweight formulation for studying resolution-dependent latency distributions in a measured object detection pipeline.

</details>


### [31] [DementiaCare-Bench: A Modality-Validated Video Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12929)
*Afrouz Sheikholeslami,Yuankai Qi,Xuyun Zhang,Luping Zhou,Amin Beheshti,Quan Z. Sheng,Ming-Hsuan Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DementiaCare-Bench is a benchmark with 56 caregiver training videos (94 clips, 9 BPSD categories) and 2023 questions to evaluate video-language models (VLMs) for dementia care support. The benchmark measures visual demand rather than assuming it, revealing that current VLMs achieve 85% overall but drop 17 points on questions requiring ordered video frames. A lightweight LoRA fine-tune (DemCare-VLM) improves video dependence from -3.3 to +4.5 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The behavioral and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD) require understanding their triggers to provide appropriate care responses. No existing benchmark evaluates whether video-language models can support caregivers by understanding what happens before BPSD behaviors occur. This gap hinders development of AI tools to assist dementia care.

Method: The researchers created DementiaCare-Bench with 56 professionally produced caregiver training videos segmented into 94 clips across nine BPSD categories. A multi-agent pipeline generated 2023 questions that ground clinical claims in verbatim transcript spans. Each question was probed under four visual conditions and labeled by the minimum visual requirement needed to answer it, measuring actual visual demand rather than assuming it. Twelve VLMs were evaluated, and a lightweight LoRA fine-tune (DemCare-VLM) was developed.

Result: Measurement contradicted intent: 77.7% of questions were written to require ordered frames, but only 34.8% actually do. The best VLMs reach 85% overall accuracy, but this is carried by questions answerable from clinical knowledge alone; accuracy drops 17 points on average on questions requiring ordered clips. A leading open model scores at chance (50%) on judging caregiver response appropriateness. The DemCare-VLM fine-tune moves video dependence from -3.3 to +4.5 points, showing that the benchmark-exposed limitations can be repaired.

Conclusion: DementiaCare-Bench reveals a significant gap between VLMs' claimed capabilities and their actual visual understanding needed for dementia care. While current models perform well on knowledge-based questions, they struggle with visual reasoning tasks critical for understanding BPSD triggers. However, this deficiency can be addressed through lightweight fine-tuning (DemCare-VLM), demonstrating that the benchmark exposes a solvable problem rather than an insurmountable one.

Abstract: Dementia affects an estimated 57 million people worldwide, and for most families the hardest part of care is not memory loss but the behavioral and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD): agitation, wandering, resistance to care, sundowning. Understanding these symptoms requires more than recognizing the behavior itself; it also requires knowing what happened beforehand. The same behavior may call for a different response depending on its trigger. Video-language models (VLMs) could potentially support caregivers, yet no existing benchmark evaluates this capability. To fill this gap, we present DementiaCare-Bench: 56 professionally produced caregiver training videos segmented into 94 clips across nine BPSD categories, with 2023 questions generated by a multi-agent pipeline that grounds every clinical claim in a verbatim transcript span. Each question is then probed under four visual conditions and labelled by the least it requires, so its visual demand is measured rather than assumed. Measurement contradicts intent: we wrote 77.7% of the questions to require ordered frames, and 34.8% do. Across 12 current VLMs the pattern is uniform. The best reach 85% overall, but that average is carried by questions a language model can answer from clinical knowledge alone; accuracy falls by 17 points on average on questions that require the ordered clip, and a leading open model scores at chance on judging whether a caregiver's response was appropriate. A lightweight LoRA fine-tune, DemCare-VLM, moves video dependence from -3.3 to +4.5 points, so what the benchmark exposes can be repaired and not only measured.

</details>


### [32] [Fast and Faithful: Principled Conditional Flow Matching for Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12953)
*Shirin Shoushtari,Edward P. Chandler,Xiao Shi,Ulugbek S. Kamilov*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a principled parametrization of measurement-conditional velocity fields in flow matching for solving imaging inverse problems. The key insight is expressing the conditional velocity in terms of the posterior mean, which is characterized as the unique minimizer of a variational objective with explicit data-consistency term. This yields operator-dependent data-consistency updates trained end-to-end, achieving state-of-the-art PSNR with 50× fewer function evaluations than prior flow-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current flow matching approaches for inverse problems incorporate measurements either through conditioning (concatenation) or inference-guided updates, but neither explicitly enforces the forward model within the learned conditional velocity field. This limits data-consistency and computational efficiency.

Method: The authors propose: (1) Expressing conditional velocity v(x_t,t,y) via posterior mean E[x_1 | x_t,y] under linear interpolation; (2) Characterizing the posterior mean as unique minimizer of a variational objective with explicit data-consistency term; (3) Proving the velocity field defines a probability flow from source to measurement-conditioned posterior; (4) Splitting the variational objective to obtain operator-dependent data-consistency updates trained end-to-end under flow-matching objective.

Result: The method achieves state-of-the-art PSNR with 50× fewer function evaluations than the strongest flow baseline. Test-time control over distortion-perception trade-off is achievable by varying sampling steps without retraining.

Conclusion: The proposed principled parametrization successfully integrates forward model enforcement into the conditional velocity field, providing theoretical guarantees and superior empirical performance in terms of reconstruction quality and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Flow matching approaches to imaging inverse problems commonly incorporate measurements in two ways. Conditioning-based approaches supply measurement-derived information as a network input, often through concatenation, while inference-guided approaches combine an unconditional velocity field with a separate data-consistency update. In these common formulations, the forward model is not explicitly enforced within the learned conditional velocity field. We propose a principled parametrization of the measurement-conditional velocity field to solve inverse problems. Under linear interpolation, we express the conditional velocity $v(x_t,t,y)$ in terms of the posterior mean $E[x_1 | x_t,y]$, and characterize that mean as the unique minimizer of a variational objective whose data-consistency term is explicit. We further prove that the velocity field defines a probability flow from the source distribution to the measurement-conditioned posterior. Splitting the variational objective yields a conditional velocity parameterization with operator-dependent data-consistency updates, which we train end-to-end under the flow-matching objective, with no additional guidance at inference. Our method achieves state-of-the-art PSNR with $50\times$ fewer function evaluations than the strongest flow baseline. Varying the sampling steps provides test-time control over the distortion-perception trade-off without retraining.

</details>


### [33] [Generative Retrieval for Unsupervised Text-Based Person Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12965)
*Mang Ye,Yucheng Ji,Yang Bai,Min Cao,Siyuan Chai,Bo Du,Min Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces GTR+, an unsupervised text-based person search (TBPS) framework that operates without manually annotated image-text pairs. It employs a two-stage generation-then-retrieval approach with a three-tier description generation framework and an adaptive confidence-weighted retrieval learning mechanism. The authors also contribute LargeFine-Person, a large-scale TBPS dataset for unsupervised pre-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing TBPS methods rely heavily on supervised learning with manually annotated image-text pairs, which are expensive and time-consuming to obtain. This work explores unsupervised TBPS using only unlabeled images, which is more practical and scalable for real-world applications.

Method: The proposed GTR+ framework consists of two stages: (1) Generation Stage: A three-tier description generation framework - base tier uses automated Q&A for basic visual attributes, intermediate tier applies inter-sample contrastive mechanism for fine-grained details, and advanced tier employs stylized expansion for textual diversity. (2) Retrieval Stage: An adaptive confidence-weighted retrieval learning framework that models image-text pairs as clean or noisy using a Gaussian Mixture Model, calibrated by real-time image-text similarity and static text generation probability, yielding adaptive sample weights during training.

Result: Experiments on multiple TBPS benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of both GTR+ and the proposed LargeFine-Person dataset. The method successfully performs unsupervised TBPS without manually annotated pairs.

Conclusion: This work shows that unsupervised TBPS is feasible through the proposed GTR+ framework. The three-tier description generation produces diverse and fine-grained textual descriptions, while the adaptive confidence-weighted retrieval learning effectively handles noisy pseudo texts. The LargeFine-Person dataset provides a valuable benchmark for unsupervised TBPS research.

Abstract: Text-based person search (TBPS) aims to retrieve images of a target person from a large image gallery based on a given natural language description. Most existing methods rely on supervised learning with manually annotated image-text pairs. In this paper, we explore unsupervised TBPS, with only unlabeled images. We propose GTR+, a two-stage generation-then-retrieval framework. In the generation stage, we introduce a tiered description generation framework designed to produce fine-grained and stylistically diverse textual descriptions through a three-tier sequential process. The base tier leverages an automated question-and-answer mechanism to generate basic visual attribute descriptions; the intermediate tier enhances fine-grained detail using an inter-sample contrastive mechanism; the advanced tier further enriches textual diversity via a stylized expansion mechanism. In the retrieval stage, to mitigate the impact of noisy pseudo texts, we develop an adaptive confidence-weighted retrieval learning framework. We model image-text pairs as clean or noisy using a Gaussian Mixture Model, calibrated by real-time image-text similarity and static text generation probability from the prior stage, yielding adaptive sample weights during training. Beyond that, we also contribute LargeFine-Person, a large-scale TBPS dataset with high-quality, fine-grained, and diverse textual annotations, enabling a practical and generalizable TBPS pre-training benchmark under unsupervised setting. Experiments on multiple TBPS benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of both GTR+ and LargeFine-Person. Code is available at: https://github.com/Flame-Chasers/GTR.

</details>


### [34] [Investigating Temporal Motion Features for Pose-to-Text Indian Sign Language Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12993)
*Manav Dhamecha,Praveen Kumar Chandaliya,Pruthwik Mishra*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates pretrained T5 model scale and explicit motion features for pose-to-text Indian Sign Language Translation (SLT) in the WSLP 2026 Shared Task. Using a lightweight pose encoder to project pose sequences into T5's embedding space, the study compares T5-small, T5-base, T5-large, and a motion-augmented variant (T5-small + Motion). The motion-augmented model achieved the largest improvement in BLEU score, while the system ranked 5th on the official leaderboard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to investigate how pretrained language model scale (small, base, large) and explicit motion features (frame-to-frame pose differences) affect pose-to-text Indian Sign Language Translation performance, addressing the challenge of converting sign language poses into readable English text.

Method: The method uses a lightweight pose encoder to project pose sequences into the embedding space of pretrained T5 models. The complete model is fine-tuned to generate English text. For the motion-augmented variant (T5-small + Motion), explicit frame-to-frame pose differences are added to the input representation. The study uses a test set of 5,334 examples and a validation set of 5,257 examples from the WSLP 2026 Shared Task.

Result: T5-small achieved the best BLEU and ROUGE scores among spatial-only models, while T5-large obtained the highest chrF score. The motion-augmented T5-small + Motion showed the largest single improvement, substantially improving BLEU over the spatial-only baseline and becoming the strongest model on this metric. The submitted system ranked 5th on the official WSLP 2026 SLT testing leaderboard.

Conclusion: The study concludes that motion features provide significant improvements for SLT, with the motion-augmented T5-small model outperforming all other variants on BLEU. Larger T5 models (T5-large) provide benefits on certain metrics (chrF), but the addition of motion features to a smaller model can be more effective than simply scaling up the model.

Abstract: We investigate the effect of pretrained T5 model scale and explicit motion features on pose-to-text Indian Sign Language Translation (SLT) for the WSLP 2026 Shared Task. Pose sequences are projected into the embedding space of T5 through a lightweight pose encoder, with the complete model fine-tuned to generate English text. The shared task data used for this work consists of a test set with 5,334 examples and a validation set with 5,257 examples. We compare T5-small, T5-base, and T5-large, and additionally introduce a motion-augmented variant, T5-small + Motion, that adds explicit frame-to-frame pose differences to the input representation. T5-small achieves the best BLEU and ROUGE scores among the spatial-only models, while T5-large obtains the highest chrF score. Augmenting T5-small with motion features yields the largest single improvement observed in our study, substantially improving BLEU over the spatial-only baseline and making it the strongest model overall on this metric. Our submitted system ranked 5th on the official WSLP 2026 SLT testing leaderboard. The source code and trained models are publicly available on GitHub and HuggingFace.

</details>


### [35] [SV-Cine: Diagnosis-Conditioned Segmentation of Single Ventricle Physiology via Generative Data Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12997)
*Lila Cunge,Yuehong Liu,Hang Xu,Thomas Coudert,Pierangelo Renella,J Paul Finn,William Hsu,Kim-Lien Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a cardiac MRI segmentation framework (SV-Cine) for Single Ventricle Physiology (SVP), a rare congenital heart disease. The framework combines synthetic data generation via SDF4CHD and generative modeling with a diagnosis-conditioned adaptation of the foundation model CineMA using Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM) layers. SV-Cine achieved Dice scores of 0.89 for LV and 0.72 for RV, outperforming nnU-Net by 0.39 points on RV segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single Ventricle Physiology is a rare congenital heart disease with morphological diversity that challenges conventional image segmentation approaches. The scarcity of clinical data and atypical anatomic configurations make developing robust segmentation methods particularly difficult. Existing methods fail to handle the anatomical variations across SVP subtypes.

Method: The framework comprises two main components: (1) A data augmentation pipeline generating synthetic 3D cardiac meshes using SDF4CHD and corresponding synthetic cardiac MRI through generative modeling; (2) SV-Cine, a diagnosis-conditioned adaptation of the foundation model CineMA that incorporates patient-level diagnostic information through Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM) layers, enabling diagnosis-aware feature adaptation during segmentation.

Result: SV-Cine achieved median Dice scores of 0.89 (IQR: 0.80-0.91) for left ventricle and 0.72 (IQR: 0.54-0.84) for right ventricle, outperforming the strongest baseline nnU-Net by 0.39 Dice points on right ventricle segmentation. The median ejection fraction error was 5.55 percentage points (IQR: 3.41-7.69) for the dominant ventricle. External cohort showed lower performance for LV and myocardium segmentation but comparable RV Dice scores.

Conclusion: A pretrained foundation model can be effectively adapted for highly specialized downstream tasks through usage of diagnosis priors while leveraging anatomical knowledge learned from large-scale MRI datasets during pretraining. The approach demonstrates the potential of combining synthetic data augmentation with diagnosis-conditioned adaptation for rare cardiac conditions.

Abstract: Single Ventricle Physiology (SVP) is a rare subtype of congenital heart disease characterized by the presence of a single functional cardiac ventricle with atypical anatomic configurations that challenge conventional image segmentation approaches. The scarcity of clinical data and the morphological diversity across SVP subtypes make the development of robust segmentation methods particularly difficult. To address these limitations, we propose a cardiac MRI segmentation framework focused on ventricular chambers and myocardium segmentation tailored for SVP. First, we introduce a data augmentation pipeline that generates synthetic 3D cardiac meshes using SDF4CHD and corresponding synthetic cardiac MRI through generative modeling. Second, we introduce SV-Cine, a diagnosis-conditioned adaptation of the foundation model CineMA that incorporates patient-level diagnostic information through Feature-wise Linear Modulation layers, enabling diagnosis-aware feature adaptation during segmentation. We evaluated the framework on an internal cohort with varying SVP subtypes. SV-Cine achieved median Dice scores of 0.89 (IQR: 0.80--0.91) for the left ventricle and 0.72 (IQR: 0.54--0.84) for the right ventricle, outperforming the strongest baseline, nnU-Net, by 0.39 Dice points on right ventricle segmentation. It also yields a median ejection fraction error of 5.55 percentage points (IQR: 3.41--7.69) for the dominant ventricle. Compared with the internal cohort, LV and myocardium segmentation performance was lower for the external cohort; whereas RV Dice scores were comparable for both cohorts. Our findings suggest that a pretrained foundation model can be adapted for highly specialized downstream tasks through usage of diagnosis priors while leveraging anatomic knowledge learned from large-scale MRI datasets during pretraining.

</details>


### [36] [Physics-Aware Video Generation via Agentic Planning and Graph-Guided Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13006)
*Minh-Loi Nguyen,Xuan-Vu Le,Thanh-Toan Do,Tam V. Nguyen,Minh-Triet Tran,Trung-Nghia Le*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PhysPlan is a training-free guidance framework that enhances video diffusion models with physical awareness by using a VLM-based cognitive simulator to decompose multimodal inputs into kinematic trajectories and 3D depth geometries, combined with object-centric gradient routing to isolate kinematic modifications while locking passive backgrounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video diffusion models lack intrinsic understanding of physical laws and produce visually appealing but causally illogical sequences with structural hallucinations and physically implausible dynamics. Existing training-free methods rely on global gradient updates and rigid scheduling heuristics that corrupt passive backgrounds and fail to model complex dynamic state changes.

Method: PhysPlan introduces: (1) A VLM operating as an iterative cognitive simulator that decomposes multimodal inputs into a Chain-of-Visual-Thought to create multimodal representation of kinematic trajectories and 3D depth geometries; (2) Object-Centric Gradient Routing to isolate kinematic modifications and completely lock the passive environment; (3) Kinetic Intensity Profiling to dynamically parameterize framework hyperparameters for varying severity of physical deformations.

Result: PhysPlan significantly outperforms both foundational and controllable VDM baselines on PhyGenBench and Physics-IQ benchmarks.

Conclusion: PhysPlan offers a promising approach for improving physical understanding of video generation by shifting from stochastic visual interpolation to agentic physics simulation through training-free test-time optimization.

Abstract: Video diffusion models (VDMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in synthesizing high-fidelity, photorealistic video content. However, they fundamentally lack an intrinsic understanding of physical laws and frequently produce visually appealing but causally illogical sequences characterized by structural hallucinations and physically implausible dynamics. Injecting physical awareness via training-free test-time optimization is a promising alternative, yet existing methods rely on global gradient updates and rigid scheduling heuristics that inadvertently corrupt passive backgrounds and fail to model complex dynamic state changes. To address this, we propose PhysPlan, a novel training-free guidance framework that shifts the paradigm from stochastic visual interpolation to agentic physics simulation. First, a VLM operates as an iterative cognitive simulator, decomposing multimodal inputs into a Chain-of-Visual-Thought to create a multimodal representation of kinematic trajectories and 3D depth geometries. Second, these signals drives an object-centric test-time optimization. Unlike prior training-free methods that rely on global gradients and rigid scheduling heuristics, PhysPlan introduces Object-Centric Gradient Routing to isolate kinematic modifications and completely lock the passive environment. Furthermore, our Kinetic Intensity Profiling dynamically parameterizes framework hyperparameters to accommodate the varying severity of physical deformations. Extensive evaluations on the PhyGenBench and Physics-IQ benchmarks demonstrate that PhysPlan significantly outperforms both foundational and controllable VDM baselines, offering a promising approach for improving the physical understanding of video generation.

</details>


### [37] [Pixel Decodability Is Not a Compression Signal: Causally Evaluating Importance Proxies for Visual KV-Cache Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13012)
*Chenyu Zhou,Qiliang Jiang,Shuning Wu,Xu Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether vision-language models retain task-relevant visual information in their visual key-value (KV) cache. The authors discover that pixel-decodable visual content retained in the KV cache is "task-inert" - the amount retained does not predict whether the computation actually uses that information to answer questions. Using a learned pixel-inversion decoder and causal ablation methods, they find retention is decoupled from attention and causal utilization. Encoder-free models retain 2.7x more task-irrelevant content than encoder-based models. Importantly, pixel-decodable reconstructability is not a useful signal for KV compression, performing no better than random at super-patch granularity and worse than attention-based methods at token granularity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to understand what information vision-language models retain in their visual KV cache and whether this retained information is actually used for task completion. They investigate whether pixel-decodable content (visual information that can be reconstructed from the cache) serves any functional purpose or is merely architectural "spillage."

Method: The authors employ several methods: (1) A learned pixel-inversion decoder to measure how much pixel-level visual information is retained in the KV cache; (2) Single-super-patch KV ablation to measure causal use - dropping specific cache entries and measuring the drop in gold-answer log-probability; (3) A preregistered, sign-calibrated, held-out experimental design to relate retention to causal use within images. They compare encoder-free vs encoder-based model architectures.

Result: Key findings: (1) Retention is decoupled from attention and, in a well-powered null hypothesis test, from causal utilization; (2) Attention is the only signal that weakly but significantly tracks causal utilization (positive control); (3) Encoder-free models retain 2.7x more pixel-decodable content than encoder-based models; (4) At super-patch granularity, pixel-decodable retention ranks KV eviction no better than random; (5) At token granularity, it acquires only a weak inverse-importance signal at larger budgets, but is dominated by attention magnitude at every budget.

Conclusion: Pixel-decodable reconstructability is not a competitive KV-compression signal at any granularity tested. The retained visual content is task-inert - it represents informational "spillage" rather than functional computation. The authors characterize this as an "informational axis" of the visual KV cache that is orthogonal to the functional axis. This represents a controlled negative result for using reconstructability as a compression heuristic.

Abstract: Vision-language models retain a substantial amount of pixel-decodable visual content in their visual key-value cache. We show, in our setting, that this retention is task-inert: across our preregistered tests, how much a unit retains never positively tracks whether the computation that answers the question causally relies on it. We measure retention with a learned pixel-inversion decoder and causal use with single-super-patch KV ablation, the teacher-forced drop in gold-answer log-probability, and relate the two within images under a preregistered, sign-calibrated, held-out design. Retention is decoupled from attention and, in a well-powered null, from causal utilization. Utilization is not inert to every proxy: attention weakly but significantly tracks it, the only signal we find that does and the design's positive control. We characterize pixel-decodable retention as an informational axis of the visual KV cache, orthogonal to the functional one. How much task-inert content a cache holds differs by architecture in our model pair: the encoder-free model retains 2.7 times more than the encoder-based one. The engineering consequence is a controlled negative result. At super-patch granularity, deconfounded pixel-decodable retention ranks KV eviction no better than random; at token granularity it acquires only a weak inverse-importance signal at larger budgets, dominated at every budget by attention magnitude. In our setting, pixel-decodable reconstructability is not a competitive KV-compression signal at any granularity we test.

</details>


### [38] [TileNet: Tile-Based CNN-SVM Architecture for Autonomous Unmanned Aerial Systems Inspection of Flat Roofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13013)
*Samuel Dunthorne,Hashim A. Hashim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a UAS-based deep learning framework for autonomous real-time detection of flat roof defects using dual-altitude aerial passes and a lightweight CNN-SVM classifier. The system achieved 94.4% test accuracy, outperforming GoogLeNet (89.2%) and AlexNet (79.8%), enabling rapid, safe, and cost-effective roof inspections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flat roofs significantly impact building envelope performance, affecting energy consumption and carbon emissions. Timely detection of roof defects is crucial for reducing heating/cooling losses, preventing moisture-driven degradation, and supporting climate-change mitigation goals. Traditional inspection methods are time-consuming, costly, and pose safety risks to human inspectors.

Method: The framework uses a multi-resolution flight strategy with dual-altitude aerial passes to capture both small fine-scale defects and larger structural issues. It integrates a tile-based architecture with a lightweight CNN-SVM classifier (5 convolutional layers, 4 dense layers with linear SVM head) designed for low-latency onboard inference on embedded UAS hardware. The model was trained on 43,383 images, validated on 3,869, and tested on 2,540 tiled and augmented images. Evaluation used DJI Matrice 350 RTK drone for real UAS imagery collection.

Result: The proposed CNN-SVM model achieved a mean test accuracy of 94.4% with 95% confidence interval of ±0.4% over three seeds, outperforming GoogLeNet (89.2%) and AlexNet (79.8%). The system enables rapid, repeatable, and safe roof inspections while reducing human risk, lowering operational costs, and supporting sustainable building maintenance.

Conclusion: The proposed UAS-based deep learning framework successfully detects flat roof defects in real-time with high accuracy. The lightweight CNN-SVM architecture meets embedded hardware constraints while achieving superior performance compared to deeper models like GoogLeNet. The system offers a practical solution for automated roof inspections, contributing to energy efficiency, building sustainability, and climate-change mitigation efforts.

Abstract: Flat roofs are among the most influential components of the building envelope, governing both structural performance and thermal efficiency, and thereby contributing directly to household energy consumption, carbon emissions, and long-term environmental sustainability. Timely detection of roof defects is essential for reducing heating and cooling losses, preventing moisture-driven degradation such as mold growth, and supporting national climate-change mitigation goals. This paper presents a real-time, Unmanned Aerial System (UAS)-based deep learning framework that autonomously detects defects using live imagery captured during dual-altitude aerial passes. The multi-resolution flight strategy is designed to aid the identification of both small, fine-scale defects and larger structural issues, enabling more comprehensive assessments. To meet the strict computational and power constraints of embedded UAS hardware, the proposed framework integrates a tile-based architecture with a lightweight Convolution Neural Network-Support Vector Machine (CNN-SVM) classifier designed for low-latency onboard inference. The final model-comprising five convolutional layers and four dense layers, the last a linear SVM head, achieved a mean test accuracy of $94.4\%$ ($95\%$ confidence interval $\pm0.4\%$ over three seeds) on a photo-level split ($43,383$ training, $3,869$ validation, and $2,540$ test tiled and augmented images), outperforming GoogLeNet ($89.2\%$) and AlexNet ($79.8\%$). Experimental evaluations using real UAS imagery collected by onsite visits with DJI Matrice 350 RTK drone demonstrate that the system supports rapid, repeatable, and safe roof inspections while reducing human risk, lowering operational costs, and enabling more sustainable building maintenance.

</details>


### [39] [Label-Guided Knowledge Distillation for 3D-CNNs in Action Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13024)
*Yanjiang Shi,Peng Zhao,Nan Qi,Guiqin Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: As a key model compression technique, knowledge distillation aims to transfer knowledge from a high-capacity teacher model to a lightweight student model for enhancing the latter's performance. In this work, we reviewed the feature knowledge distillation for 3D-CNNs and observed that most feature distillation methods in video analysis are simple adaptations of those used in image analysis, often neglecting the differences of video features in the temporal dimension. To address this issue, we proposed Label-Guided Knowledge Distillation (LGKD) to guide the distillation of student model features using ground truth labels. Our method entails two components: sample-wise distillation and class-wise distillation, enabling the student model to learn feature representation of the teacher model at two levels. Sample-wise distillation utilizes label information and the teacher's probability distribution to guide the learning of features that significantly impact temporal accuracy while mitigating noise. Meanwhile, class-wise feature distillation employs a prototype network to further capture the relational knowledge among samples within the same category, enhancing the student's ability to learn higher-dimensional semantic information and improving model generalization. To demonstrate the effectiveness and superiority of our method, we conducted comprehensive experiments on two benchmark action recognition datasets, UCF101 and HMDB51, achieving competitive results.

</details>


### [40] [Unified CT and MRI Pancreas Segmentation for Label-Efficient Cross-Modality Subregion Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13043)
*Ziliang Hong,Hongyi Pan,Halil Ertugrul Aktas,Andrea Bejar,Elif Keles,Frank H. Miller,Michael B. Wallace,Rajesh N. Keswani,Gorkem Durak,Ulas Bagci*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A unified 3D pancreas segmentation framework using domain-adversarial learning to handle CT-MRI domain shift. The method trains a shared nnU-Net encoder-decoder with a latent domain discriminator to learn anatomical representations across 4,604 heterogeneous scans, enabling both cross-modality whole pancreas segmentation and label-efficient transfer to subregion segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical image segmentation models trained on a single modality suffer significant performance drops when applied to unseen domains due to large differences in appearance and intensity distributions between imaging modalities (CT vs MRI).

Method: Domain-adversarial learning with a shared nnU-Net encoder-decoder architecture trained on 4,604 heterogeneous CT and MRI scans. A latent domain discriminator encourages CT-MRI feature alignment. The learned encoder is transferred to pancreatic head-body-tail segmentation using limited MRI-only subregion annotations.

Result: Whole pancreas segmentation: 87.31% average Dice on in-distribution test set; 84.20%-88.09% Dice across external OOD datasets. Subregion segmentation: 80.53% on MRI, 83.05% on CT without using CT subregion annotations.

Conclusion: A unified anatomical representation can effectively support both cross-modality pancreas segmentation and label-efficient downstream transfer to subregion segmentation tasks.

Abstract: Robust medical image segmentation across imaging modalities is challenging because of large differences in appearance and intensity distributions. Models trained on a single modality often show substantial performance drops when applied to unseen domains. In this work, we develop a unified 3D pancreas segmentation framework that applies domain-adversarial learning to 4,604 heterogeneous CT and MRI scans to learn anatomical representations. A shared nnU-Net encoder-decoder is trained for whole-pancreas segmentation, with a latent domain discriminator encouraging CT-MRI feature alignment. The learned encoder is subsequently transferred to pancreatic head-body-tail segmentation using limited MRI-only subregion annotations. An average Dice score of 87.31% on the in-distribution test set and Dice scores ranging from 84.20% to 88.09% across external OOD datasets were achieved in whole pancreas segmentation. Dice scores of 80.53% on MRI and 83.05% on CT were achieved for downstream subregion segmentation, without using CT subregion annotations. These results demonstrate that a unified anatomical representation can support both cross-modality pancreas segmentation and label-efficient downstream transfer.

</details>


### [41] [USPLIT-VQA: U-Shaped Split Learning for Visual Question Answering with Contribution-Aware Weighted Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12168)
*Md Khalid Syfullah,Alvi Ataur Khalil*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: USPLIT-VQA is a privacy-preserving Visual Question Answering framework using U-shaped split learning, where clients retain initial layers and classification head while the server hosts intermediate layers. It includes CAWA, a gradient-similarity-based mechanism to mitigate malicious updates, achieving up to 5.8X memory reduction and 10.8X communication reduction while maintaining reasonable accuracy across four VQA datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VQA systems require joint interpretation of images and text, but privacy concerns arise from centralized training (requiring all data access) and federated learning (requiring each client to host the full model). The need is for a solution that preserves data privacy while reducing computational and communication overhead on client devices.

Method: The paper proposes USPLIT-VQA, a U-shaped split learning framework where clients retain initial layers and classification head while the server hosts computationally heavy intermediate layers, keeping raw inputs and labels on client devices. Additionally, Contribution-Aware Weighted Aggregation (CAWA) is introduced as a gradient-similarity-based client scoring mechanism to reduce the influence of malicious updates.

Result: Experiments on four VQA datasets (VQA-RAD, SLAKE, PathVQA, VizWiz) with two backbones show: accuracy gains over Federated Learning for Custom model but reduced accuracy for BiomedCLIP under the evaluated fixed split; client memory reductions of up to 5.8X and communication reductions of up to 10.8X. With one malicious client, CAWA reduces attacker's influence by over 98%, though limitations appear at higher corruption levels. Reconstruction experiments show lower inversion quality under evaluated attacks.

Conclusion: USPLIT-VQA effectively balances privacy preservation with computational efficiency in VQA tasks, offering significant memory and communication savings. CAWA provides robust defense against malicious updates at low corruption levels but shows limitations under higher corruption. The framework demonstrates the feasibility of privacy-preserving VQA through split learning, though accuracy trade-offs exist depending on the model backbone and split configuration.

Abstract: Visual Question Answering (VQA) systems, jointly interpreting images and natural language queries, hold significant promise across many domains, yet the privacy-sensitive nature of user data creates a fundamental barrier. Centralized training requires access to all data, while federated learning requires each client to host the full model. We propose USPLIT-VQA, a U-shaped split learning framework for privacy-preserving VQA in which each client retains the initial layers and the classification head while the server hosts the computationally heavy intermediate layers, keeping raw inputs and labels on the client device. We further introduce Contribution-Aware Weighted Aggregation (CAWA), a gradientsimilarity-based client scoring mechanism designed to reduce the influence of malicious updates. Experiments on four VQA datasets (VQA-RAD, SLAKE, PathVQA, and VizWiz) with two backbones show accuracy gains over Federated Learning for the Custom model and reduced accuracy for BiomedCLIP under the evaluated fixed split, alongside client memory reductions of up to 5.8X and communication reductions of up to 10.8X. With one malicious client, CAWA reduces the attacker's influence by over 98%, while experiments at higher corruption levels identify its limitations. Reconstruction experiments further show lower inversion quality under the evaluated attacks.

</details>


### [42] [QuPAINT: Physics-Aware Multimodal Reasoning for Quantum Material Characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12202)
*Sankalp Pandey,Xuan-Bac Nguyen,Hoang-Quan Nguyen,Tim Faltermeier,Nicholas Borys,Hugh Churchill,Khoa Luu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: QuPAINT is a physics-aware multimodal AI framework for characterizing 2D quantum materials from optical microscopy images. It uses synthetic data generation (Synthia), a multimodal instruction dataset (QMat-Instruct), and physics-informed attention (PIA) to handle domain shifts across materials, substrates, and imaging conditions. The system achieves state-of-the-art performance on QF-Bench, the largest real-world benchmark for this task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterizing 2D quantum materials by optical microscopy requires localizing exfoliated flakes and determining layer thickness from subtle optical contrast. However, models face synthetic-to-real domain shifts and variations across materials, substrates, laboratories, and imaging conditions, making accurate flake detection challenging.

Method: The framework has three main components: (1) Synthia generates diverse synthetic microscopy images preserving layer-dependent optical behavior; (2) QMat-Instruct is a multimodal instruction dataset with image-specific reasoning traces from verified annotations; (3) Physics-Informed Attention (PIA) injects substrate-relative optical priors into visual representations for grounded multimodal reasoning. Evaluation uses QF-Bench, spanning diverse microscopy and substrate conditions.

Result: QuPAINT-8B substantially outperforms prior methods and establishes state-of-the-art performance for both general and monolayer flake detection. Image-grounded supervision improves strict spatial grounding and confidence calibration while preserving robust general flake detection on unseen materials.

Conclusion: The physics-aware multimodal approach successfully addresses domain shift challenges in 2D quantum material characterization. The combination of synthetic data generation, instruction tuning, and physics-informed attention enables reliable transfer to unseen materials and substrates, representing a significant advancement for automated 2D material discovery and device fabrication.

Abstract: Characterizing two-dimensional (2D) quantum materials by optical microscopy requires localizing exfoliated flakes and determining their layer thickness from subtle optical contrast and interference color to select suitable flakes for device fabrication. However, models face synthetic-to-real domain shifts and variation across materials, substrates, laboratories, and imaging conditions. We present QuPAINT, a physics-aware multimodal framework for transferable quantum flake characterization. The Synthetic Materials Framework (Synthia) generates diverse synthetic microscopy images while preserving layer-dependent optical behavior. Using these images, we construct QMat-Instruct, a multimodal instruction dataset with image-specific reasoning traces generated from verified annotations and constrained to observable optical cues. QuPAINT integrates these signals through Physics-Informed Attention (PIA), which injects substrate-relative optical priors into the visual representation to support grounded multimodal reasoning. For evaluation, we introduce QF-Bench, to our knowledge, the largest real-world benchmark for this problem, spanning diverse microscopy and substrate conditions. Using its verified annotations, we study counting, visual grounding, reasoning quality, confidence calibration, and transfer to an unseen material. QuPAINT-8B substantially outperforms prior methods and establishes state-of-the-art performance for both general and monolayer flake detection. Additional experiments show that image-grounded supervision improves strict spatial grounding and confidence calibration while preserving robust general flake detection on the unseen material.

</details>


### [43] [Bridging Vision Foundation Model Priors with CLIP for Spatial-aware Few-shot Anomaly Detection in Medical Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12454)
*Juzheng Miao,Yuchen Yuan,Cheng Chen,Pheng-Ann Heng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Spatial-FAD is a spatial-aware few-shot medical anomaly detection framework that combines Vision Foundation Models (DINO) with CLIP semantics to improve lesion localization through structural affinity priors, sliding-window aggregation, and prototype-enhanced support memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CLIP-based few-shot medical AD lacks explicit spatial supervision for precise lesion localization, while Vision Foundation Models like DINO capture fine-grained anatomical structures better via self-distillation. This work aims to leverage the complementarity of both approaches.

Method: 1) VFM-enhanced adapter injecting structural affinity prior from DINO into CLIP features; 2) Sliding-window aggregation strategy for high-resolution, spatially dense embeddings; 3) Prototype-enhanced support memory scheme storing compact prototypes for normal/abnormal patterns, fusing patch-to-prototype and image-text similarities.

Result: On Liver CT, Retinal OCT, and Brain MRI datasets, Spatial-FAD significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods. In 4-shot scenario: +11.4% average Dice score improvement and +1.8% AUC improvement.

Conclusion: The proposed Spatial-FAD effectively combines VFM spatial priors with CLIP semantics, achieving superior lesion localization performance in few-shot medical anomaly detection through structural guidance, high-resolution embeddings, and prototype-based memory augmentation.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models such as CLIP enable effective few-shot medical anomaly detection (AD) via strong image-text semantic alignment. However, their globally contrastive pretraining lacks explicit spatial supervision, limiting precise lesion localization. In contrast, Vision Foundation Models (VFMs) such as DINO learn spatially coherent patch representations via self-distillation and local-to-global consistency, better capturing fine-grained anatomical structures. Leveraging this complementarity, we propose Spatial-FAD, a spatial-aware few-shot medical AD framework that improves lesion localization by combining VFM spatial priors with CLIP semantics. Specifically, we introduce a VFM-enhanced adapter that injects a structural affinity prior derived from DINO into CLIP features. This structure-guided refinement encourages visual embeddings to better adhere to lesion boundaries while maintaining semantic alignment. To address the loss of spatial detail from patchification and the limited input resolution of CLIP, we adopt a sliding-window aggregation strategy. This generates high-resolution, spatially dense embeddings to further enhance localization granularity. Moreover, we introduce a prototype-enhanced support memory scheme to efficiently exploit the few-shot support set. This module stores compact prototypes for normal and abnormal patterns, reducing memory costs while boosting performance by fusing patch-to-prototype and image-text similarities. Extensive experiments on three benchmark datasets, including Liver CT, Retinal OCT, and Brain MRI, demonstrate that Spatial-FAD significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods, especially in lesion segmentation. Notably, in the 4-shot scenario, our method achieves an average improvement of over 11.4% in Dice score and 1.8% in AUC. Code is available at: https://github.com/JuzhengMiao/Spatial-FAD.

</details>


### [44] [LettuceVisSim: A Simulator That Generates Lettuce Image Time-series for Vision-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12505)
*Ziye Zhu,Bert van 't Ooster,Congcong Sun,Eldert van Henten,Sjoerd Boersma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LettuceVisSim is a lettuce growth simulator that generates labeled time series of crop images to enable vision-based reinforcement learning in controlled environment agriculture (CEA). It combines a process-based model for shoot dry weight dynamics, a canopy layout algorithm, and a Unity rendering engine.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-based reinforcement learning holds strong potential for decision-making in CEA, but its development is hindered by the scarcity of labeled crop images.

Method: The simulator contains three core components: (1) a process-based model (PBM) for shoot dry weight dynamics, (2) a canopy layout algorithm for deriving canopy layout representations from shoot dry weight, and (3) a Unity rendering engine for image generation. Five validation experiments were conducted.

Result: The PBM reproduced shoot dry weight under dynamic plant-density management with R²=0.84. A piecewise cubic regression mapped shoot dry weight to potential projected area with R²=0.94. The canopy layout representation was validated using 12 experimental datasets, achieving R²=0.84 when driven by measured shoot dry weight and R²=0.40 (0.76 excluding one outlier) when driven by PBM-simulated values. The Unity rendering engine converted canopy layout representations into RGB and segmentation images in less than 10ms. A demonstration showed that a lighting-control policy can be learned and applied by observing only generated crop images.

Conclusion: LettuceVisSim provides a validated approach for generating labeled crop images, offering a proof of concept for vision-based reinforcement learning in CEA. The simulator addresses the data scarcity problem and enables further research in automated crop management.

Abstract: Vision-based reinforcement learning holds strong potential for decision-making in controlled environment agriculture (CEA). However, its development is hindered by the scarcity of labelled crop images. To address this gap, LettuceVisSim, a lettuce growth simulator that generates labelled time series of crop images, was developed and validated. The simulator contains a process-based model (PBM) for shoot dry weight dynamics, a canopy layout algorithm for deriving canopy layout representations from shoot dry weight, and a Unity rendering engine for image generation. Five findings support the simulator. First, the PBM reproduced shoot dry weight under dynamic plant-density management with $\mathrm{R}^{2}=0.84$. Second, a piecewise cubic regression mapped shoot dry weight to potential projected area with $\mathrm{R}^{2}=0.94$. Third, the canopy layout representation was validated using 12 experimental datasets each having different dynamic environmental and spacing conditions. It reproduced the ground coverage ratio dynamics observed in measured images, achieving $\mathrm{R}^{2}=0.84$ when driven by measured shoot dry weight and $\mathrm{R}^{2}=0.40$ (0.76 excluding one outlier) when driven by PBM-simulated values. Fourth, the Unity rendering engine converted canopy layout representations into RGB and segmentation images at less than 10~ms.
