Fifth, a demonstration showed that a lighting-control policy can be learned and applied by observing only crop images that were generated with LettuceVisSim, providing a proof of concept of vision-based reinforcement learning in CEA using LettuceVisSim.

</details>


### [45] [Earth-Agent-Pro: Towards Real-World Full-Chain Earth Observation with Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12533)
*Zhutao Lv,Chenhao Dang,Yi Feng,Yanpei Gong,Xiaolei Wang,Junyan Ye,Conghui He,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Earth-Agent-Pro is a Plan-and-Execute framework for open-world Earth observation tasks that translates high-level scientific questions into executable workflows. It uses expert-authored skills, workflow-centered structured memory for runtime repair, and two LLM adapters (sequence-level SFT for planning, GRPO for execution). The benchmark Earth-Bench-Pro contains 248 task cores as 744 questions across three regimes, with 248 Open-World Execution questions. Achieves 66.13% LLM-as-Judge accuracy with GPT-5, exceeding ReAct by 20.95 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Earth observation agents start from supplied observations and benchmarks provide prepared inputs, leaving full-chain open-world EO execution largely untested. There is a need for agents that can translate high-level scientific questions into executable workflows, acquire observations, prepare data, perform domain computations, and derive conclusions from runtime evidence.

Method: Execution-adaptive Plan-and-Execute framework using expert-authored skills to constrain planning and runtime tool use. Key components: (1) Workflow-centered structured memory that records planned steps, accepted evidence, and dependencies, enabling repair of only affected workflow suffix when runtime evidence invalidates a step. (2) Two separate LLM adapters: sequence-level supervised fine-tuning for planner workflow composition, and node-level group relative policy optimization (GRPO) with locally verifiable rewards for executor tool-argument grounding.

Result: With shared GPT-5 backbone: 66.13% LLM-as-Judge accuracy, exceeding ReAct by 20.95 points in this metric and 24.44 points in Tools-In-Order. Joint adapter tuning raises Qwen3.5-9B LLM-as-Judge accuracy from 38.31% to 50.00%, an 11.69-point gain over untuned configuration. Planning-only evaluation and execution with reference workflow show adapters improve workflow composition and argument grounding respectively.

Conclusion: The framework successfully enables open-world EO execution by combining planning and execution adapters with workflow-centered memory for runtime repair. The benchmark Earth-Bench-Pro provides comprehensive evaluation across RGB imagery, spectral observations, and remote sensing products. Both GPT-5 and Qwen3.5-9B show significant improvements over baseline methods, validating the effectiveness of the joint adapter tuning approach.

Abstract: Real-world Earth observation (EO) agents must translate high-level scientific questions into executable workflows to acquire observations, prepare data, perform domain computations, and derive conclusions from runtime evidence. Existing EO agents typically start from supplied observations, while benchmarks typically provide prepared inputs or candidate answers, leaving full-chain open-world EO execution largely untested. We present Earth-Agent-Pro, an execution-adaptive Plan-and-Execute framework using expert-authored skills to constrain planning and runtime tool use. Workflow-centered structured memory records planned steps, accepted evidence, and their dependencies, enabling repair of only the affected workflow suffix when runtime evidence invalidates a step. Separate large language model adapters use sequence-level supervised fine-tuning for planner workflow composition and node-level group relative policy optimization with locally verifiable rewards for executor tool-argument grounding. Earth-Bench-Pro instantiates 248 expert-curated task cores as 744 questions under three matched regimes. Its 248 Open-World Execution questions span RGB imagery, spectral observations, and remote sensing products, pairing high-level requests with runtime data requirements, executable trajectories, and open-ended answers grounded in execution evidence. With a shared GPT-5 backbone, Earth-Agent-Pro achieves 66.13% LLM-as-Judge accuracy, exceeding ReAct by 20.95 points in this metric and 24.44 points in Tools-In-Order. Joint adapter tuning raises Qwen3.5-9B LLM-as-Judge accuracy from 38.31% to 50.00%, an 11.69-point gain over the untuned configuration. Planning-only evaluation and execution with the reference workflow show that the adapters improve workflow composition and argument grounding, respectively. Code and datasets will be released soon.

</details>


### [46] [ExpertHTR: Unified Handwritten Text Recognition with Multi-Task Learning and Sparse Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12705)
*Dang Hoai Nam,Nguyen Duy Hieu,Quang Huu Hieu,Vo Nguyen Le Duy*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ExpertHTR is a unified vision-language framework for handwritten text recognition that addresses the challenge of heterogeneous datasets through complementary supervision and a sparse Mixture-of-Experts architecture. It uses a common Page-Region-Line representation to organize structural annotations from different datasets and constructs four related training tasks without requiring additional manual labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handwritten text recognition resources are often small and distributed across collections that differ in language, script, document structure, and annotation format, making joint page-level training difficult.

Method: The framework uses a Page-Region-Line representation to organize heterogeneous structural annotations and constructs four related training tasks: complete transcription, physical-line coverage, text localization, and localized recognition. It builds on a jointly trained dense model and introduces a sparse Mixture-of-Experts architecture with an always-active shared branch and conditionally routed full-MLP experts. Sparsegen allows the number of active routed experts to vary with the hidden representation, while routing regularization reduces persistent concentration on a small subset of experts.

Result: Experiments on seven heterogeneous handwriting benchmarks show that complementary supervision consistently improves training with page transcription alone, while joint multi-source training provides further gains on most datasets. The proposed sparse expert model further improves the dense baseline on six of the seven sources. The final unified model substantially outperforms general-purpose OCR and vision-language systems on most benchmarks and achieves state-of-the-art performance on the IAM paragraph-level benchmark, while specialized HTR systems remain stronger on several challenging collections.

Conclusion: ExpertHTR successfully addresses the challenge of heterogeneous handwritten text recognition datasets through complementary supervision and conditional model capacity. The sparse Mixture-of-Experts architecture provides additional gains over the dense baseline. However, specialized HTR systems still outperform the unified model on some challenging collections, suggesting a trade-off between generality and specialization.

Abstract: Handwritten text recognition resources are often small and distributed across collections that differ in language, script, document structure, and annotation format, making joint page-level training difficult. We propose ExpertHTR, a unified vision-language framework that addresses this problem through complementary supervision and conditional model capacity. Structural annotations from heterogeneous datasets are first organized through a common Page-Region-Line representation and used to construct four related training tasks for complete transcription, physical-line coverage, text localization, and localized recognition, without requiring additional manual labels. Building on a jointly trained dense model, ExpertHTR introduces a sparse Mixture-of-Experts architecture with an always-active shared branch and conditionally routed full-MLP experts. Sparsegen allows the number of active routed experts to vary with the hidden representation, while routing regularization reduces persistent concentration on a small subset of experts. Experiments on seven heterogeneous handwriting benchmarks show that complementary supervision consistently improves training with page transcription alone, while joint multi-source training provides further gains on most datasets. The proposed sparse expert model further improves the dense baseline on six of the seven sources. The final unified model also substantially outperforms the evaluated general-purpose OCR and vision-language systems on most benchmarks and achieves state-of-the-art performance on the IAM paragraph-level benchmark, while specialized HTR systems remain stronger on several challenging collections.

</details>

### [47] [Investigating Developer-Reported Software Security Testing Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12008)
*Md Erfan,Ahmed Ryan,Md Rayhanur Rahman*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study analyzes 17,743 Stack Overflow questions to identify developer-reported software security testing (SST) challenges. Through manual labeling, 582 SST-related questions were classified into a taxonomy of 8 categories and 31 subcategories. Key findings reveal that developers struggle most with security finding interpretation, tool selection, authentication testing, and tool integration. Challenges extend beyond vulnerability detection to include workflow, configuration, interpretation, and remediation concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software security testing is essential for identifying vulnerabilities, but developers face practical challenges in tool selection, configuration, output interpretation, authentication workflow testing, and vulnerability remediation. This study aims to empirically characterize these developer-reported challenges to improve SST usability and support.

Method: The researchers collected 17,743 SO questions using SST-related keywords, performed manual labeling to identify 582 SST-related questions, and constructed a taxonomy of 8 categories and 31 subcategories. They analyzed these challenges using prevalence, difficulty, correlation, temporal trend, co-occurrence, and held-out stability analyses.

Result: Developers frequently discuss security finding interpretation and reliability, security testing guidance and tool selection, authentication and authorization testing, and security tool integration and automation. Validation-related challenges require more technical context and longer resolution time. Security finding interpretation and remediation-related actionability show increasing trends over time. False positives frequently co-occur with explainability issues, while integration challenges often appear with tool suggestions and documentation gaps.

Conclusion: Developer-reported SST challenges extend beyond vulnerability detection to include workflow, configuration, interpretation, and remediation concerns. These findings can help researchers, practitioners, educators, and tool providers improve SST usability, documentation, result interpretation, and remediation support.

Abstract: Software security testing (SST) is essential for identifying vulnerabilities and improving software security, but developers often face practical challenges when selecting tools, configuring test environments, interpreting scanner outputs, testing authentication workflows, and acting on reported vulnerabilities. This study empirically characterizes developer-reported SST challenges in Stack Overflow (SO) discussions and examines their prevalence, difficulty, temporal evolution, co-occurrence, and taxonomy stability. We analyze 17,743 SO questions collected using SST-related keywords. Through manual labeling, we identify 582 SST-related questions and construct a taxonomy of 8 categories and 31 subcategories. We then analyze these challenges using prevalence, difficulty, correlation, temporal trend, co-occurrence, and held-out stability analyses. The results show that developers frequently discuss security finding interpretation and reliability, security testing guidance and tool selection, authentication and authorization testing, and security tool integration and automation. Validation-related challenges often require more technical context and longer resolution time. Security finding interpretation and remediation-related actionability show increasing trends over time. Co-occurrence analysis shows that false positives frequently appear with explainability, while integration challenges often appear with tool suggestions and documentation/resources. Overall, developer-reported SST challenges extend beyond vulnerability detection and include workflow, configuration, interpretation, and remediation concerns. These findings can help researchers, practitioners, educators, and tool providers improve SST usability, documentation, result interpretation, and remediation support.

</details>


### [48] [AI Safety: Not Optional, Not Later](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10630)
*Qinghua Lu,Yoshua Bengio*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multi-layered safety-by-design assurance architecture for AI systems, combining model-level supervision, system-level controls, independent verification, monitoring, evidence infrastructure, and governance frameworks to address AI safety failures that occur across multiple layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from observed incidents where AI safety failures emerge across multiple layers of the AI system stack. This indicates that single-layer safety measures are insufficient, necessitating a comprehensive architecture that addresses safety at all levels.

Method: The paper presents an assurance architecture that integrates: (1) Model-level supervision (e.g., Scientist AI), (2) System-level controls over scaffolds and harnesses, (3) Independent verification mechanisms, (4) Continuous monitoring systems, (5) Evidence infrastructure for documentation, and (6) Governance frameworks ensuring accountability and evidence interoperability.

Result: The result is a comprehensive safety-by-design architecture that provides defense in depth across multiple system layers, with integrated governance and evidence systems to ensure accountability and interoperability of safety documentation.

Conclusion: The architecture offers a holistic approach to AI safety by addressing failures at multiple levels through combined technical controls and governance mechanisms, enabling more robust and accountable AI safety assurance.

Abstract: Incidents show that AI safety failures often arise across multiple layers. We present a safety-by-design assurance architecture combining model-level supervision, such as Scientist AI, with system-level controls over scaffolds and harnesses, independent verification, monitoring, and evidence infrastructure, supported by governance for accountability and evidence interoperability.

</details>


### [49] [PRISMA-LLM: An Empirical Reporting Framework for AI-Assisted Systematic Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11559)
*Miguel Zabaleta,Baihan Lin*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper analyzes SciLitBench, a corpus of 888 review-automation papers, to characterize changes in methods, review-stage use, evaluation, and reported limitations of LLMs and AI-enabled software in systematic reviews. The study introduces PRISMA-LLM, an empirically grounded framework for implementation disclosure and consequence-sensitive evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models and AI-enabled software are increasingly participating in systematic-review decisions, but the information needed to audit these workflows is reported inconsistently. This gap in reporting transparency and evaluation standards motivated the investigation.

Method: The researchers analyzed SciLitBench, a corpus containing 888 review-automation papers with 14,726 annotations, to characterize changes in methods, review-stage use, evaluation practices, and reported limitations across the literature.

Result: Automation has shifted toward LLM- and software-facing workflows, including stages that can alter the evidence base. Since 2023, 38.0% of software/product papers reported no evaluation, compared with 9.3% of LLM papers. Reporting coverage increased with LLM workflow complexity, yet 52% of positive-only LLM evaluations still reported an unmet reliability or performance requirement.

Conclusion: The patterns identified led to the introduction of PRISMA-LLM, an empirically grounded framework that separates implementation disclosure from consequence-sensitive evaluation and limitation reporting, addressing the inconsistent reporting in LLM- and AI-enabled systematic review tools.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) and AI-enabled software increasingly participate in systematic-review decisions, yet the information needed to audit these workflows is reported inconsistently. We analyze SciLitBench, a corpus of 888 review-automation papers with 14,726 annotations, to characterize changes in methods, review-stage use, evaluation and reported limitations. Automation has shifted toward LLM- and software-facing workflows, including stages that can alter the evidence base. Since 2023, 38.0% of software/product papers reported no evaluation, compared with 9.3% of LLM papers. Reporting coverage increased with LLM workflow complexity, yet 52% of positive-only LLM evaluations still reported an unmet reliability or performance requirement. From these patterns, we introduce PRISMA-LLM, an empirically grounded framework separating implementation disclosure from consequence-sensitive evaluation and limitation reporting.

</details>


### [50] [When Agent Metrics Measure Different Things: An Evidence-Grounded Audit of the Praxa AI Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12017)
*Stefan G. Creadore,Peyton Woakz*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a retrospective measurement audit of Praxa AI implementation files, evaluating whether agent evaluations accurately measure what their labels imply. The study identifies significant measurement issues: routing reports with exempted gating failures, timing data with 32-bit integer overflow artifacts (2,147,483,647 ms), and misleading input reduction percentages (94.39% vs 46.54% depending on scope). The authors verify calculations through independent implementations and provide finite-completion bounds for missing data, but acknowledge that historical runs were not independently reproduced and statistical significance was not established.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core motivation is to expose how agent evaluations can appear numerically correct while measuring a different construct than their labels suggest. The authors aim to demonstrate that measurement artifacts (such as integer overflow, exempted gating, and selective metric reporting) can create misleading impressions of agent performance.

Method: The methodology involves: (1) Retrospective audit of Praxa AI implementation files, historical evaluation artifacts, and operational records; (2) Reproduction of descriptive calculations; (3) Independent verification of 91 timing statistics using weighted rational-arithmetic implementation; (4) Execution of 13 scoring-function and 12 analysis-verifier tests; (5) Finite-completion bounds analysis for handling missing durations without imputation.

Result: Key findings: (1) 139-case routing report shows 112 passes/27 failures despite zero gating failures due to explicit exemptions; (2) 8,843 tool-attempt rows contain 448 missing duration values; (3) 121 durations equal signed 32-bit maximum (2,147,483,647 ms) due to integer overflow from clamped lifecycle age; (4) Stratum contrast: pooled 99th percentile timing is 2,147,483,647 ms vs 38,118.31 ms for server-observed completed calls; (5) Follow-up input reduction is 94.39% but only 46.54% when including trigger calls.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that agent evaluations can be numerically correct while measuring different constructs than implied. The contribution is a source-linked case study and reusable verification package for separating gate policy, lifecycle timing, and request-level accounting from broader performance claims. Limitations include lack of independent reproduction of historical provider runs and absence of general capability superiority or population-level statistical significance establishment.

Abstract: Agent evaluations can be numerically correct while measuring a different construct from the one implied by their labels. We present a retrospective measurement audit of selected Praxa AI implementation files, historical evaluation artifacts, and operational records. A 139-case offline routing report contains 112 passes and 27 failures despite zero gating failures, because known gaps are explicitly exempted from the gate. An identifier-free export represents 8,843 tool-attempt rows: 8,395 recorded durations and 448 missing values. Of the durations, 121 equal the signed 32-bit maximum and carry abandoned-client labels; inspected database code clamps elapsed lifecycle age. The pooled recorded 99th percentile is 2,147,483,647 ms, versus 38,118.31 ms among server-observed completed calls. This is a stratum contrast, not a treatment effect. In a documented single-trajectory compaction pilot, the reported follow-up input reduction is 94.39%, but the reduction across the trigger and follow-up calls together is 46.54%. We reproduce the descriptive calculations, verify 91 timing statistics through a separate weighted rational-arithmetic implementation, and execute 13 scoring-function and 12 analysis-verifier tests. Finite-completion bounds show how missing durations limit all-row timing statements without imputing values. The contribution is a source-linked case study and reusable verification package for separating gate policy, lifecycle timing, and request-level accounting from broader agent-performance claims. Historical provider runs and the full current pipeline were not independently reproduced; general capability superiority and population-level statistical significance are not established.

</details>


### [51] [Reality Is the Final Verifier: On Two Key Gaps in Agentic Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12039)
*Alexander Krentsel,Shubham Agarwal,Mert Cemri,Shu Liu,Sidharth Sankhe,Ziming Mao,Matei Zaharia,Ion Stoica*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a "two-gap framework" for understanding failures in agentic software engineering: the requirement gap (stakeholder intent not fully captured in requirements) and the model gap (deployment environment not accurately represented). It introduces an "assurance-revision loop" that uses deployment evidence to continuously narrow these gaps rather than attempt to close them, framed as a resource-allocation problem over human judgment, agent capability, and compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formal verification cannot guarantee acceptable post-deployment behavior because requirements only approximate stakeholder intent and models only approximate the real deployment environment. This creates two fundamental failure modes: reward hacking (exploiting requirement/model omissions) and hallucination (fabricating requirements or environment assumptions).

Method: Conceptual framework analysis using the two-gap lens to unify failure modes in agentic software engineering, combined with a systems engineering perspective to propose the assurance-revision loop as a continuous narrowing approach rather than gap closure.

Result: The paper proposes casting assured agentic development as a resource-allocation problem over human judgment, agent capability, and compute - with human judgment as the bottleneck for the requirement gap and faithful (costly) evaluation as the bottleneck for the model gap. Reality remains the final verifier.

Conclusion: Acceptable behavior under actual deployment conditions is the ultimate test, while predeployment evaluations remain proxies. The goal should be continuous narrowing of gaps rather than their impossible closure in an open, changing world.

Abstract: Software development follows an implementation-verification loop in which developers or agents iteratively revise an implementation until an evaluator, such as a test suite, accepts it. The evaluator checks the implementation against a set of requirements under a model of the deployment environment. Yet even a formal proof that the implementation satisfies the requirements under the model cannot guarantee acceptable behavior after deployment. Requirements only approximate stakeholder intent, and the model only approximates the real deployment environment. We call these together - requirement gap and model gap - the two-gap framework, which unifies the main failure modes of agentic software engineer-ing: reward hacking exploits omissions in the requirements or model, while hallucination widens the gaps by fabricating requirements or environment assumptions.
  Because neither gap can generally be certified closed in an open, changing world, the goal shifts from closing them to continuously narrowing them. We therefore propose an assurance-revision loop that uses deployment evidence to revise the requirements, model, or evaluator when stakeholders reject the resulting behavior. We then cast assured agentic development as a resource-allocation problem over human judgment, agent capability, and compute. The two principal bottlenecks mirror the two gaps: human judgment for the requirement gap and faithful, costly evaluation for the model gap. Reality remains the final verifier: acceptable behavior under actual deployment conditions is the ultimate test, while predeployment evaluations remain proxies for it.

</details>


### [52] [A decision-basis contract for auditable LLM-assisted medical billing verification: deterministic rules, verbatim evidence, and fail-closed abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12156)
*Jan Hölter,Kevin Geis,Benjamin Raab,Boris Bauke*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a proof of concept for auditable LLM-assisted medical billing verification using a decision-basis contract that separates deterministic fee-catalog rule checking from LLM-based semantic assessment of free-text documentation. Four locally run open-weight models were evaluated on a synthetic catalog and 36 curated cases, showing variable outcome agreement with no consistent advantage over baseline, but improved identification of missing information with explicit documentation requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation addresses a critical challenge in healthcare billing: ensuring accuracy and transparency in medical billing verification. LLM-assisted systems could improve this process, but lack of auditability and transparency in LLM decisions poses significant risks. The paper aims to create an auditable system where LLM decisions can be inspected and verified.

Method: The method employs a two-layer decision-basis contract: (1) A deterministic layer that resolves applicable catalog releases and checks code availability, quantity limits, and exclusions; (2) A semantic layer that classifies claimed items as supported, contradicted, or missing required information. Support and contradiction require verbatim evidence spans. The system uses fail-closed abstention when rule context is unavailable, assessment fails, or evidence is missing. Evaluation included four open-weight models tested under the full contract, an ablation without explicit documentation requirements, and an end-to-end baseline.

Result: Outcome agreement varied across models with no consistent advantage over the baseline. Explicit documentation requirements improved identification of missing information for all four models. The evidence gate exposed cases where correct raw judgments lacked valid evidence, converting them to incomplete decision-basis entries.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that explicit decision records can make rule findings, documentation judgments, and abstention reasons inspectable. However, evaluation on real catalogs, independently annotated documentation, and with human reviewers is required to assess practical value. The approach shows promise for auditability but needs further validation in real-world settings.

Abstract: This work presents a proof of concept for auditable LLM-assisted medical billing verification based on a decision-basis contract. The contract separates deterministic checks of versioned fee-catalog rules from LLM-based assessment of free-text documentation. The deterministic layer resolves the applicable catalog release and checks code availability, quantity limits, and exclusions. The semantic layer classifies each claimed item as supported, contradicted, or missing required information. Support and contradiction require a verbatim evidence span; unavailable rule context, unsuccessful assessment, or missing required evidence prevents support through fail-closed abstention. We evaluated four locally run open-weight models on a synthetic catalog and 36 curated cases under the contract, an ablation without explicit documentation requirements, and an end-to-end baseline. Outcome agreement varied across models and showed no consistent advantage over the baseline. Explicit documentation requirements improved identification of missing information for all four models. The evidence gate also exposed cases in which correct raw judgments lacked valid evidence and were converted to incomplete decision-basis entries. The results show how explicit decision records can make rule findings, documentation judgments, and abstention reasons inspectable. Evaluation on real catalogs, independently annotated documentation, and with human reviewers is required to assess practical value.

</details>


### [53] [Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Scientific Code Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12190)
*Aaron Nobile,Andreas Adelmann,Mohsen Sadr*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper develops a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system for scientific code understanding that separates expensive offline ingestion from lightweight online answering, enabling small local models to achieve strong performance on a 100-question benchmark over a C++ scientific codebase.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models for coding assistants (e.g., Claude Code, Codex) rely on very large, cloud-hosted models with significant computational costs and data-privacy implications. The authors investigate whether useful, fully local coding agents can be built around small open-source models.

Method: The authors developed a RAG system that strictly separates: (1) an expensive offline ingestion stage including parsing, structural graph construction, LLM-generated entity explanations, and embedding; (2) a lightweight online answering stage. The system was evaluated on a 100-question benchmark spanning eleven categories over the IPPL scientific codebase written in C++, with answers scored by an independent frontier model as judge. Seven answering models were tested.

Result: Model family and retrieval quality matter more than parameter count. A 9B model achieved the highest average score (0.795), outperforming both larger models within the pipeline and the same models embedded in the Claude Code retrieval architecture.

Conclusion: Front-loading code understanding into a reusable, codebase-specialized vector store enables small local models to deliver grounded and repository-specific answers, making the agent well-suited as a privacy-preserving development tool for in-house scientific codebases.

Abstract: Large language models have become central to modern coding assistants, but state-of-the-art systems such as Claude Code or Codex rely on very large, cloud-hosted models with significant computational cost and data-privacy implications. This work investigates whether a useful, fully local coding agent can be built around small open-source models by shifting the computational burden away from inference. We develop a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system for scientific code understanding that strictly separates an expensive offline ingestion stage parsing, structural graph construction, LLM-generated entity explanations, and embedding from a lightweight online answering stage. The system is evaluated on a 100-question benchmark spanning eleven categories over the IPPL scientific codebase written in C++, with answers scored by an independent frontier model as the judge. Across seven answering models, we find that model family and retrieval quality matter more than parameter count, i.e. a 9B model achieves the highest average score (0.795), outperforming both larger models within our pipeline and the same models embedded in the Claude Code retrieval architecture. The results indicate that front-loading code understanding into a reusable, codebase-specialised vector store enables small local models to deliver grounded and repository-specific answers, making the agent well suited as a privacy-preserving development tool for in-house scientific codebases.

</details>


### [54] [Separating Engineering Reasoning from DEXPI Serialization in LLM-Based Greenfield Surface-Process Design: A Three-Case Study for Underground Gas Storage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12656)
*Qingchuan Zhu,Shuyue Tong,Pengju Ren*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This diagnostic study investigates whether separating engineering reasoning from Data Exchange in the Process Industry (DEXPI) serialization reduces generation burden and helps isolate different failure types in constrained greenfield surface-process design for underground gas storage. The study compares Direct DEXPI generation with an Engineering Intermediate Representation (IR) approach across three cases using one LLM model (qwen3.8-max-0902).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can produce engineering descriptions and structured process representations, but standards-level serialization (DEXPI) substantially increases the generation burden. The study aims to determine whether separating engineering reasoning from serialization helps isolate representation and engineering failures in constrained greenfield surface-process design for underground gas storage.

Method: The study compared Direct DEXPI generation with generation of a lightweight Engineering Intermediate Representation (IR) across three cases: single-pressure injection, withdrawal and export, and dual-pressure injection. All six conditions used one fixed model snapshot (qwen3.8-max-0902) with one completed hosted generation per condition. Token usage was compared: Direct prompts (~121.8k-121.9k tokens) vs. IR prompts (617-699 tokens). Validation was performed using XSD validation for Direct outputs and a minimal validator for IR outputs.

Result: Direct DEXPI outputs were XSD-valid in 2 of 3 cases, while all 3 Engineering IR outputs were structurally valid. The IR approach dramatically reduced prompt token usage (from ~121.8k to 617-699 tokens). However, one IR output was rejected due to an explicit cooling-state contradiction. Two distinct Direct DEXPI failure modes were identified: engineering inconsistency and standards-level serialization failure.

Conclusion: Deferring DEXPI serialization substantially reduces representation burden (token usage reduced by ~99.5%) and helps isolate serialization failure from engineering inconsistencies. However, reducing representation burden alone does not eliminate engineering inconsistencies—the IR approach still produced one engineering contradiction, indicating that engineering feasibility does not uniformly favor the IR method.

Abstract: Large language models can produce engineering descriptions and structured process representations, but standards-level serialization can substantially increase the generation burden. This diagnostic study examines whether separating engineering reasoning from Data Exchange in the Process Industry (DEXPI) serialization changes where representation and engineering failures occur in constrained greenfield surface-process design for underground gas storage. We compare Direct DEXPI generation with generation of a lightweight Engineering Intermediate Representation (IR) on three cases: single-pressure injection, withdrawal and export, and dual-pressure injection. All six conditions use one fixed model snapshot, qwen3.8-max-0902, with one completed hosted generation per condition. Direct outputs are XSD-valid in 2 of 3 cases, while all 3 Engineering IR outputs are structurally valid under a minimal validator. Direct prompt inputs contain approximately 121.8k-121.9k tokens, compared with 617-699 tokens for the Engineering IR prompts. Engineering feasibility does not uniformly favor the IR: one IR output is rejected for an explicit cooling-state contradiction. The cases also reveal two distinct Direct DEXPI failure modes: engineering inconsistency and standards-level serialization failure. The observed comparison shows that, in these evaluated method bundles, deferring DEXPI serialization substantially reduces representation burden and helps isolate serialization failure, but reducing representation burden alone does not eliminate engineering inconsistencies.

</details>


### [55] [What is the Difference Between Me and You? Benchmarking the Quality Gap Between Human-Written and AI-Generated Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12708)
*Cristina Improta,Pietro Liguori,Domenico Cotroneo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a large-scale comparative study of AI-generated vs human-written code across Python, Java, and C, analyzing 787,562 function pairs from three AI assistants (GPT models, DeepSeek-Coder, Qwen2.5-Coder). The study finds AI code is structurally compressed (~50% size and branching), stylistically templated, and has different defect profiles than human code. Security vulnerabilities vary by language, with LLMs producing more issues in Python/Java but fewer memory-safety problems in C. The authors release CQBench, a benchmark of 27,346 issue-prone tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI coding assistants are increasingly co-authoring production software, yet evaluation focuses only on functional correctness. This leaves open whether AI-generated code differs from human code in quality dimensions that dominate lifecycle cost (maintainability, security, defect patterns). This gap motivates the comparative analysis at scale.

Method: The study mined 787,562 function pairs from open-source repositories: human-written functions paired with AI-generated implementations from docstrings. Three AI assistants were used (OpenAI GPT models, DeepSeek-Coder, Qwen2.5-Coder). Analysis covered: (1) structural complexity metrics, (2) statistical naturalness (stylistic clustering), (3) static-analysis findings mapped onto Orthogonal Defect Classification (ODC) for defects and Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) for vulnerabilities. Comparisons were made across authors and languages.

Result: Key findings: (1) AI-generated code is structurally compressed—roughly half the size and branching of human code; (2) AI code clusters separately at the style level, appearing templated; (3) Defect profiles differ: human code shows issues of mature codebases, AI code shows repetitive boilerplate; (4) Security is language-dependent—LLMs produce more and more severe findings in Python/Java but fewer high-severity memory-safety findings than humans in C; (5) Once size is controlled, complexity metrics carry little signal, while naturalness separates authors.

Conclusion: AI-generated code differs significantly from human code in quality dimensions affecting lifecycle cost. The compressed, templated nature of AI code and its distinct defect/vulnerability profiles have practical implications for software maintenance and security. The released CQBench benchmark (27,346 issue-prone tasks) enables future QA and security testing. The study suggests that while AI code passes functional tests, quality assurance must account for these structural and security differences.

Abstract: AI coding assistants are becoming co-authors of production software, yet their evaluation centers on functional correctness, leaving open whether their code differs from human code in the quality dimensions dominating lifecycle cost. We compare human-written and AI-generated code at scale: 787,562 function pairs across Python, Java, and C, each human function mined from open-source repositories paired with implementations generated from its docstring by three AI assistants (OpenAI GPT models, DeepSeek-Coder, Qwen2.5-Coder). We characterize structural complexity and statistical naturalness, and map static-analysis findings onto Orthogonal Defect Classification for defects and the Common Weakness Enumeration for vulnerabilities, making authors and languages directly comparable. AI-generated code is structurally compressed and stylistically templated: roughly half the size and branching of human code, clustering apart at the style level. Defect profiles differ in kind: human code concentrates issues of mature codebases, AI code repetitive boilerplate; security is language-dependent, with LLMs producing more, and more severe, findings in Python and Java but fewer high-severity memory-safety findings than humans in C. Once size is controlled for, complexity metrics carry little signal, while naturalness separates authors. Finally, we release CQBench, a benchmark of 27,346 issue-prone tasks with baselines and an evaluation pipeline for quality assurance and security testing.

</details>


### [56] [Involving before Evolving: A Vision for Trustworthy Enterprise Digital Twin Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13071)
*Kérian Fiter,Adil Lagrou,Franck Dervault,Bentley Oakes*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a vision for trustworthy Enterprise Digital Twins (EDTs) engineering using an "involving before evolving" paradigm. The approach involves rapidly involving stakeholders through working prototypes before evolving toward federation and full interoperability. The vision is grounded in a collaboration with Michelin manufacturer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realizing EDTs requires navigating siloed departments, tacit knowledge, and high-stakes decisions with long-horizon consequences. Existing approaches involve domain experts during model development but focus less on early organizational buy-in in EDTs.

Method: A three-stage approach combining: (1) foundation models for rapid prototyping, (2) an ontological backbone for federated interoperability, and (3) observability tooling for stakeholder trust.

Result: An initial prototype with Michelin has helped support stakeholder buy-in, demonstrating the viability of the "involving before evolving" paradigm.

Conclusion: The "involving before evolving" paradigm offers a promising approach to EDT engineering by prioritizing early stakeholder involvement through rapid prototyping before evolving toward full federation and interoperability.

Abstract: Enterprise Digital Twins (EDTs) promise data-driven decision support at organizational scale, but realizing them requires navigating siloed departments, tacit knowledge, and high-stakes decisions with long-horizon consequences. Existing approaches involve domain experts during model development but focus less on early organizational buy-in in EDTs. We present a vision for trustworthy EDT engineering grounded in an `involving before evolving' paradigm: rapidly involving stakeholders through a working prototype before evolving toward federation and full interoperability. Our three-stage approach combines foundation models for rapid prototyping, an ontological backbone for federated interoperability, and observability tooling for stakeholder trust. We ground our vision in an ongoing collaboration with Michelin, a multinational manufacturer, where an initial prototype has helped support stakeholder buy-in.

</details>

### [57] [Space as an Interventional Invariant: Cross-Modal Predictive Geometry for Stratified Cities and Em-Spaced Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11959)
*Tao Yang,Xuhui Lin,Kunyao Li,Haijiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Space is a foundational concept across mathematics, physics, spatial cognition, urban science, and embodied intelligence, yet these fields often treat spatial structure either as a shared geometric container or as a collection of disconnected representations. Such approaches struggle to explain how heterogeneous sensory and urban processes can jointly reveal a common spatial structure, particularly when different modalities do not share the same metric or representation. This paper addresses this gap by defining space as an interventional invariant: the minimal relational structure that preserves local compatibility and the conditional laws of future observations under admissible actions. We develop a cross-modal predictive geometry that integrates local state spaces, modality-specific observation maps, an action groupoid, and a canonical predictive-state quotient, with explicit causal conditions for identifying interventional rather than merely observational structure. The key theoretical result shows that, under joint point separation, equivariance, and interventional faithfulness, the latent space is identifiable up to the centraliser of the intervention group, thereby reducing representational ambiguity to residual coordinate freedom. The framework is further extended to stratified urban systems using sheaf-valued representations, allowing geometric, physical, mobility, social, and economic layers to coexist without being reduced to a single metric. Synthetic experiments under noise evaluate equivariance, predictive sufficiency, holonomy, restriction-map recovery, cross-scale consistency, and context saturation. The resulting framework provides a unified and falsifiable foundation for spatial cognition, urban science, embodied AI, and em-spaced intelligence.

</details>


### [58] [Scalable Discrete-to-Continuous Channel Simulation for Compression and Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12067)
*Joseph Rowan,Buu Phan,Ashish J. Khisti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel channel simulation scheme for compressing samples from prescribed probability distributions. Unlike existing methods that suffer from high computational costs, random stopping times, or potentially infinite sample requirements, the proposed approach uses a fixed number of random samples with runtime independent of the channel and input. The method generates samples from target distributions, applies latent permutation, and uses exponential race for sample selection. It scales to O(n log n) time using polar and multilevel coding, with applications in variable-rate compression and differentially private distributed mean estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from limitations in existing channel simulation algorithms: high computational costs, random stopping times, and potential requirement for infinite number of shared random samples. These issues make existing approaches impractical for machine learning systems that need to compress samples from prescribed probability distributions.

Method: The proposed method uses a fixed number of random samples (runtime independent of channel/input) by: (1) generating one sample or fixed number of samples from each potential target distribution, (2) applying a latent permutation to the samples, (3) performing sample selection using exponential race. The approach is scaled to handle long blocklengths in O(n log n) time using polar and multilevel coding to reduce per-symbol overhead.

Result: The scheme provides: (1) exact and approximate simulation of discrete-to-continuous channels, (2) flexible tradeoff between number of generated samples and compression rate, (3) O(n log n) complexity for long blocklengths, (4) reduced per-symbol overhead. Applications demonstrated include variable-rate compression with stochastic VQ-VAEs and communication-efficient differentially private distributed mean estimation via exact simulation of the Gaussian mechanism.

Conclusion: The paper presents a practical channel simulation scheme that overcomes limitations of prior methods by using fixed sample numbers and achieving O(n log n) scalability. The approach enables efficient variable-rate compression and differentially private distributed learning, demonstrating versatility across machine learning applications.

Abstract: Channel simulation has recently emerged as a useful component in machine learning systems where samples from a prescribed probability distribution are to be compressed. Yet, general channel simulation algorithms often suffer from high computational costs, random stopping times or, in the worst case, can require generating an infinite number of shared random samples. We introduce a scheme for both exact and approximate simulation of discrete-to-continuous channels which conversely uses a fixed number of random samples, and therefore has a runtime independent of the channel and the input. Unlike existing channel simulation schemes which generate a sequence of independent samples from a proposal distribution, our approach generates one sample, or alternatively a fixed number of samples, from each potential target distribution. We then apply a latent permutation to the samples before performing sample selection using an exponential race. Our scheme provides a flexible tradeoff between the number of generated samples and the compression rate. Using polar and multilevel coding, we scale our approach to handle long blocklengths in $O(n \log n)$ time in order to benefit from reduced per-symbol overhead. We conclude by demonstrating applications to variable-rate compression with stochastic VQ-VAEs and communication-efficient differentially private distributed mean estimation via exact simulation of the Gaussian mechanism.

</details>


### [59] [Explanations-Driven Active Feature Acquisition for Algorithmic Recourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12179)
*Vinura Galwaduge,Jagath Samarabandu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of combining algorithmic recourse with active feature acquisition, where features are costly to obtain. The authors propose EDFA (Explanation-Driven Feature Acquisition), which uses Markov Blanket theory to select features based on their explanatory value per unit cost, rather than treating explanation as an afterthought.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing active feature acquisition (AFA) methods are explanation-agnostic—they acquire features without considering the explanatory value of the resulting recourse. This is problematic because in cost-constrained prediction settings, explanations should drive which features to acquire, not merely report on decisions after they are made.

Method: The paper uses Markov Blanket theory to unify counterfactual, semifactual, and alterfactual explanations. It characterizes how available recourse grows as features are acquired. Based on this framework, the authors propose EDFA (Explanation-Driven Feature Acquisition), which selects features by explanatory value per unit cost. The method also provides distribution-free validity guarantees for recourse issued from partial information.

Result: Experiments on 7 publicly available datasets with neural network-based predictive models show that EDFA acquires substantially fewer features than state-of-the-art AFA baselines while maintaining comparable accuracy and yielding more decision-relevant, actionable recourse.

Conclusion: The paper successfully integrates algorithmic recourse with active feature acquisition, demonstrating that explanation-driven feature selection can reduce costs while maintaining predictive performance and improving the actionability of recourse recommendations.

Abstract: Algorithmic recourse methods typically assume that a predictive model has access to all features of an individual. In practice, decisions are often made with partial information, because features are costly to acquire. Active feature acquisition addresses cost-constrained prediction, but existing methods are explanation-agnostic: prior work provides explanations only after acquiring additional features, rather than using explanations to drive acquisition. This work flips that and treats algorithmic recourse and feature acquisition jointly. We use Markov Blanket theory to unify counterfactual, semifactual, and alterfactual explanations and to characterize how available recourse grows as features are acquired. Building on this framework, we propose an Explanation-Driven Feature Acquisition (EDFA) method that selects features by explanatory value per unit cost. The framework is further extended with distribution-free validity guarantees for recourse issued from partial information, which signal trustworthy, lower-cost recourse, along with a lower bound on the calibration data required to certify them. Experiments on 7 publicly available datasets with neural network-based predictive models show that EDFA acquires substantially fewer features than state-of-the-art AFA baselines while maintaining comparable accuracy and yielding more decision-relevant, actionable recourse. The implementation is available on GitHub.

</details>


### [60] [Predicting Collision Cross Sections with GRACE: Geometric Residual Adduct Conditioning via Early-fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12223)
*Parthasarathy Suryanarayanan,Susanta Das,Shreyans Sethi,Kenneth M. Merz,,Joseph A. Morrone*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRACE is a 3D molecular collision cross section (CCS) predictor that uses residual learning and early-fusion adduct conditioning to achieve state-of-the-art prediction accuracy across multiple evaluation splits and external test sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current CCS predictors either ignore explicit 3D structure or treat adduct identity as a late categorical feature, limiting their ability to capture adduct-dependent geometric effects that influence CCS values.

Method: GRACE adapts a pretrained molecular geometry encoder using two key innovations: (1) a residual objective relative to an adduct-aware physical descriptor baseline, and (2) adduct conditioning within the encoder via learned adduct tokens and low-rank attention adapters (early fusion). Evaluated on 9,000+ molecule-adduct CCS records using random, scaffold, and adduct-sensitive splits.

Result: GRACE achieves best mean percentage difference among evaluated models: 1.67% (random split), 2.11% (scaffold split), and 2.36% (adduct-sensitive split). Shows consistently lower error across four independent external test sets and outperforms four previously reported physics-based workflows on a held-out set.

Conclusion: Residual learning and encoder-level adduct conditioning are effective inductive biases for fast, accurate CCS prediction. The residual approach stabilizes training by removing dominant mass-CCS trend, while early fusion improves adduct-sensitive prediction compared to late fusion approaches.

Abstract: Collision cross section (CCS), derived from ion mobility mass spectrometry, is a common descriptor for molecular annotation. Prediction is challenging for machine learning models because it reflects the size, shape, and ionization state of a gas-phase molecular ion. Most predictors either ignore explicit 3D structure or treat adduct identity as a late categorical feature, which limits their ability to capture adduct-dependent geometric effects. We present GRACE (Geometric Residual Adduct Conditioning via Early-fusion), a 3D CCS predictor that adapts a pretrained molecular geometry encoder using geometric residual adduct conditioning via early fusion. GRACE combines two inductive biases: a residual objective relative to an adduct-aware physical descriptor baseline and adduct conditioning within the encoder via a learned adduct token and low-rank attention adapters. We evaluate the model on a curated set of over 9,000 experimental molecule-adduct CCS records with random, scaffold, and adduct-sensitive splits designed to separate interpolation, scaffold generalization, and adduct-driven generalization. GRACE achieves the best mean percentage difference among the evaluated learned models on all three splits: 1.67% on the random split, 2.11% on the scaffold split, and 2.36% on the adduct-sensitive split. Diagnostic analyses suggest that residual learning stabilizes training by removing the dominant mass-CCS trend, while early fusion improves adduct-sensitive prediction relative to late fusion. Across four independent external test sets, GRACE shows consistently lower error than the other evaluated models. On a held-out set, GRACE also attains the lowest mean percent difference when compared with four previously reported physics-based workflows. These results support residual learning and encoder-level adduct conditioning as practical inductive biases for fast, accurate CCS prediction.

</details>


### [61] [Adaptive Chemotherapy Control under Tumor Heterogeneity via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12264)
*Bereket Sitotaw Kidane,Md Samiul Haque Motayed,Shuo Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops closed-loop deep reinforcement learning (DRL) chemotherapy dosing policies (TD3 for continuous, DQN for discrete actions) and benchmarks them against Pontryagin's Maximum Principle open-loop control on a heterogeneous tumor model. TD3 shows higher efficacy while DQN shows more consistent dosing, revealing an efficacy-consistency trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tumor heterogeneity and drug resistance complicate patient-specific model-based optimal chemotherapy control. Current approaches struggle to generalize across diverse patient populations.

Method: Developed and compared closed-loop DRL dosing policies with continuous (TD3) and discrete (DQN) action spaces trained on a high-dimensional heterogeneous tumor model. Benchmark against PMP-derived open-loop control. Evaluated on 100-patient virtual cohort with ±10% parametric perturbations.

Result: TD3 achieves higher average tumor reduction; DQN yields tighter inter-patient dosing consistency. This reveals an efficacy-consistency trade-off in DRL-based chemotherapy dosing.

Conclusion: Simulation-trained DRL can learn state-dependent feedback dosing policies that complement open-loop optimal control benchmarks. Translation to clinical settings requires addressing partial observability and state estimation for sparse/noisy measurements.

Abstract: Designing effective chemotherapy regimens is hindered by tumor heterogeneity and drug resistance, which complicate the deployment of patient-specific model-based optimal control across diverse populations. We develop and compare closed-loop deep reinforcement learning (DRL) dosing policies with continuous (TD3) and discrete (DQN) action spaces trained on a high-dimensional heterogeneous tumor model. The DRL policies are benchmarked against a Pontryagin's Maximum Principle (PMP)-derived open-loop benchmark. We assess generalization under parametric heterogeneity using a 100-patient virtual cohort with plus or minus 10 percent uniform perturbations in growth and drug-sensitivity parameters. Across this cohort, TD3 achieves higher average tumor reduction, while DQN yields tighter inter-patient dosing consistency, revealing a clear efficacy-consistency trade-off in this study. Our simulations assume full observation of all tumor subpopulations; translation to sparse and noisy clinical measurements will require partial-observability formulations and/or state estimation. Overall, the results show that simulation-trained DRL can learn state-dependent feedback dosing policies that complement open-loop optimal control benchmarks.

</details>


### [62] [Amortized Low-Rank Adaptation for Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12278)
*Fernando Palafox,David Fridovich-Keil*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CLAW addresses the challenge of adapting world models to unknown test-time environments by using a hypernetwork to generate low-rank (LoRA) adapters at test time, balancing computational efficiency with expressivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models enable agents to plan by predicting consequences of actions, but become inaccurate when environment changes. Existing approaches (in-context learning vs gradient-based adaptation) trade off computational cost against expressivity. There needs to be a method that achieves both high expressivity and computational efficiency.

Method: CLAW uses a hypernetwork to generate low-rank (LoRA) adapters at test time. During pretraining, the method simulates adaptation to various environments and jointly trains the hypernetwork and base world model. At test time, the base model is frozen and the hypernetwork generates adapters from a small batch of test-time transitions via a forward pass.

Result: Using only seconds of test-time data, CLAW outperforms gradient-based adaptation and in-context learning during online adaptation. It avoids overfitting in data-scarce regimes. The advantage comes from expressive adapters rather than context conditioning. Joint pretraining of hypernetwork and base model outperforms post-hoc training.

Conclusion: CLAW successfully addresses the tradeoff between computational cost and expressivity in world model adaptation. The approach demonstrates that jointly pretraining the hypernetwork with the base model is superior to post-hoc training, and that expressive adapters generated by the hypernetwork are the key to performance rather than mere context conditioning.

Abstract: World models let agents plan by predicting the consequences of their actions, but changes in the environment can make them inaccurate. We study the problem of adapting a world model to an unknown test-time environment, drawn from a known environment family, using only a few episodes of interaction. Existing approaches trade off computational cost against expressivity, i.e., the range of models a method can produce. For example, in-context learning is computationally cheap but limited in expressivity, and gradient-based adaptation is expressive but computationally expensive. We present CLAW (Context-conditioned Low-rank Adaptation of World models), which addresses this tradeoff by using a hypernetwork to generate low-rank (LoRA) adapters at test time. During pretraining, we simulate adaptation to a variety of environments and jointly train the hypernetwork and base world model. At test time, we freeze the base model and use a forward pass of the hypernetwork to generate adapters from a small batch of test-time transitions. We evaluate CLAW in locomotion and manipulation environment families that vary in dynamics, embodiment, and reward. We show that, using only seconds of test-time data, CLAW outperforms gradient-based adaptation and in-context learning during online adaptation. We also show that CLAW avoids overfitting in data-scarce regimes, that its advantage comes from the expressive adapters rather than context conditioning, and that pretraining the hypernetwork jointly with the base model outperforms training it post hoc.

</details>


### [63] [FRIST: FMRI Representation Informed Shared-space Training Improves EEG-only Individual-Finger BCI Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12298)
*Jintao Zhang,Yidan Ding,Joshua Kosnoff,Maxim Karrenbach,Hanwen Wang,Bin He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FRIST is a two-stage EEG decoding framework that leverages fMRI-derived spatial information to improve finger-level motor decoding from scalp EEG. It learns fMRI-informed spectral projections from simultaneous EEG-fMRI recordings and uses fMRI-derived class geometry to guide residual refinement, achieving significant accuracy improvements across multiple decoding scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Individual-finger decoding from scalp EEG remains challenging because finger representations are spatially close in the sensorimotor cortex and blurred by volume conduction. There is a need to integrate the high spatial resolution of fMRI with the real-time applicability of EEG for naturalistic brain-computer interface control.

Method: FRIST uses a two-stage approach: (1) learns fMRI-informed spectral projections from simultaneous EEG-fMRI recordings, and (2) uses fMRI-derived class geometry to guide residual refinement of EEG predictions. It transfers information across recordings through shared finger labels without requiring paired trials and uses only EEG at inference. Evaluated using EEGNet and EEG Conformer as feature extractors.

Result: FRIST improved group average accuracy from 66.93% to 74.53% for two-class ME, 44.83% to 56.58% for three-class ME, 80.78% to 85.63% for two-class MI, and 60.93% to 69.90% for three-class MI. With EEG Conformer, it achieved 87.40% in two-class MI and 72.54% in three-class MI. It also improved decoding even when the target participant's own fMRI data were unavailable.

Conclusion: fMRI provides useful spatial constraints for EEG representation learning. FRIST offers a multimodal strategy for integrating the spatial specificity of fMRI with the real-time applicability of EEG, improving noninvasive EEG-based finger-level BCI decoding.

Abstract: Finger-level motor decoding is important for naturalistic brain-computer interface (BCI) control, yet individual-finger decoding from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) remains challenging because finger representations are spatially close in the sensorimotor cortex and blurred by volume conduction. Leveraging the high spatial resolution of functional MRI (fMRI), we introduce fMRI Representation-Informed Shared-Space Training (FRIST), a two-stage EEG decoding framework that first learns fMRI-informed spectral projections from simultaneous EEG-fMRI recordings and then uses fMRI-derived class geometry to guide residual refinement of EEG predictions. FRIST transfers information across recordings through shared finger labels without requiring paired trials and uses only EEG at inference. We evaluated 12 able-bodied participants during movement execution (ME) and motor imagery (MI) under two-class and three-class chronological session-held-out decoding simulating the online scenario. Using EEGNet as the EEG feature extractor, FRIST increased group average accuracy from 66.93% to 74.53% for two-class ME, from 44.83% to 56.58% for three-class ME, from 80.78% to 85.63% for two-class MI, and from 60.93% to 69.90% for three-class MI compared with the EEG-only EEGNet baseline. FRIST is also shown to improve EEG-only decoding when the target participant's own fMRI data were unavailable. FRIST also generalized across multiple EEG decoding backbones, reaching 87.40% in two-class MI and 72.54% in three-class MI with EEG Conformer as the EEG feature extractor. These findings indicate that fMRI provide useful spatial constraints for EEG representation learning. FRIST improves noninvasive EEG-based finger-level BCI decoding, offering a multimodal strategy for integrating the spatial specificity of fMRI with real-time applicability of EEG.

</details>


### [64] [Sampling via Decision-Flow: Training-Free Extraction of Improved Latent Reasoning Paths in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12317)
*Zhendong Mi,Shaoyi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decision-Flow Sampling (DF-Sample) is a training-free, inference-time framework that constructs hierarchical reasoning trees, scores terminal nodes, and back-propagates utilities to evaluate global trajectories before committing to a path. This method unlocks latent high-quality reasoning chains in pretrained base LLMs without costly RL fine-tuning, achieving 45.6% on GPQA (vs. 38.9% power sampling and 39.9% GRPO).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Central question: Does RL instill genuinely new capabilities in LLMs or merely reshape how existing knowledge is expressed? Based on the distribution-sharpening hypothesis (RL reallocates probability toward high-reward trajectories already latent in base models), the authors ask whether these latent reasoning paths can be unlocked without costly RL fine-tuning.

Method: DF-Sample is a training-free, data-free inference-time framework that: (1) constructs a hierarchical reasoning tree, (2) scores terminal nodes for quality, (3) back-propagates utilities to inform each intermediate branching decision. Unlike conventional sampling that makes local step-wise choices, DF-Sample performs explicit global trajectory evaluation before committing to a path, recovering high-quality but low-probability reasoning chains that standard decoding misses.

Result: On GPQA, DF-Sample achieves 45.6% accuracy, surpassing power sampling (38.9%) and GRPO (39.9%). Across three models and four benchmarks, DF-Sample consistently outperforms baselines, demonstrating substantial latent reasoning potential in pretrained base models.

Conclusion: A training-free inference-time method can outperform trained RL methods (like GRPO), indicating that significant reasoning capabilities already exist in pretrained base models and can be unlocked through intelligent inference-time strategies rather than expensive RL fine-tuning.

Abstract: A central question in LLM reasoning is whether reinforcement learning (RL) instills genuinely new capabilities or merely reshapes how existing knowledge is expressed during inference. Building on the distribution-sharpening hypothesis, which holds that RL reallocates probability mass toward high-reward trajectories already latent in base models, we ask: can we unlock those latent paths without costly RL fine-tuning? We present Decision-Flow Sampling (DF-Sample), a training-free, data-free inference-time framework that constructs a hierarchical reasoning tree, scores terminal nodes for quality, and back-propagates utilities to inform each intermediate branching decision. Unlike conventional sampling strategies that make purely local step-wise choices, DF-Sample performs explicit global trajectory evaluation before committing to a path, recovering high-quality but low-probability reasoning chains that standard decoding overlooks. On GPQA, DF-Sample achieves 45.6% accuracy, surpassing power sampling (38.9%) and GRPO (39.9%), showing that a training-free method can outperform a trained one. Across three models and four benchmarks, DF-Sample consistently outperforms baselines, indicating substantial latent reasoning potential in pretrained base models.

</details>


### [65] [Simulating Disengaged Students to Evaluate LLM-based Tutors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12331)
*Xianghui Meng,Jionghao Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DAS2 is a pre-deployment protocol that models five learner-engagement states (engaged, gaming, wheel-spinning, off-task, mixed) to evaluate AI tutor performance across different disengaged behaviors. It addresses the limitation that simulated students often ignore disengaged behaviors, which is crucial since tutors need different responses for different learner states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulated students are used to evaluate tutoring strategies, but current simulations fail to account for disengaged behaviors (gaming the system, wheel-spinning, off-task behavior). Since tutors may need different responses for different learner states, simulations should model these disengaged states to provide meaningful pre-deployment evaluation of AI tutors.

Method: 1) Used ASSISTments09 dataset with two independent coders labeling 100 tutoring sessions (84% agreement, Cohen's kappa = 0.78)
2) Compared human consensus labels with DAS2 rule-based labels (81% match, kappa = 0.75)
3) Tested fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B and prompt-only GPT-4o for simulating different learner states
4) Evaluated five AI tutors from Claude, Llama, Gemini, Qwen, and GPT families
5) Validated with human judges to compare automated vs human evaluation

Result: 1) Conditioning simulations on intended learner states reduced correctness-rate gap: gaming (0.54 → 0.20), wheel-spinning (0.51 → 0.18)
2) Fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B better matched authentic response-time distributions
3) Prompt-only GPT-4o generated more distinguishable learner states
4) Relative rankings of AI tutors remained stable across learner states and interaction lengths, but absolute performance varied
5) Automated tutor evaluation does not fully align with human judgment

Conclusion: DAS2 provides a reproducible pre-deployment framework for evaluating how AI tutors respond to diverse learner-engagement states. The framework reveals state-specific differences in tutor support while maintaining stable relative rankings across different learner conditions.

Abstract: Simulated students generated by computational models provide a practical way to evaluate tutoring strategies and pedagogical approaches used by human and AI tutors. However, such simulations should account for disengaged behaviors, including gaming the system, wheel-spinning, and off-task behavior, because tutors may need different responses for different learner states. We present Disengagement-Aware Student Simulators (DAS2), a reproducible pre-deployment protocol that models five learner-engagement states: engaged, gaming, wheel-spinning, off-task, and mixed, and evaluates AI tutor performance across these states. Using ASSISTments09, two coders independently labeled 100 sampled tutoring sessions based on anonymized interaction-log summaries. They achieved 84% agreement (Cohen's kappa = 0.78), and among agreed cases, human consensus labels matched DAS2 rule-based labels in 81% of cases (kappa = 0.75). Conditioning simulations on intended learner states reduced the correctness-rate gap between simulated and authentic sessions from 0.54 to 0.20 for gaming and from 0.51 to 0.18 for wheel-spinning. Fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B better matched authentic response-time distributions, while prompt-only GPT-4o generated more distinguishable learner states. Evaluation of five AI tutors from the Claude, Llama, Gemini, Qwen, and GPT families showed that relative rankings remained stable across learner states and interaction lengths, while absolute performance varied, revealing state-specific differences in tutor support. Human validation further showed that automated tutor evaluation does not fully align with human judgment. DAS2 provides a pre-deployment framework for evaluating how AI tutors respond to diverse learner-engagement states before deployment.

</details>


### [66] [ParaRecover: A Process-Level Benchmark for Error Localization and Recovery in Parallel Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12345)
*Bowen Guan,Zhentao Yin,Yanming Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ParaRecover is a process-level benchmark for evaluating error localization and recovery in multi-turn parallel tool-use agents, featuring 14 error types, 10,626 instances, and the SDE rubric measuring structural integrity, diagnostic reasoning, and evolutionary strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent benchmarks mainly evaluate final task success or tool-call correctness, providing limited insight into whether agents can reliably diagnose and recover from intermediate execution failures. This is particularly critical in multi-turn parallel tool-use scenarios where errors may propagate across dependent branches and trigger cascading failures.

Method: The benchmark is built upon a fine-grained taxonomy of 14 error types covering planning dependencies, tool selection, and argument matching. The SDE rubric is proposed to enable fine-grained, process-oriented evaluation by measuring structural integrity, diagnostic reasoning, and evolutionary strategy during agent execution.

Result: Experiments across more than ten mainstream LLMs reveal that even state-of-the-art models still struggle with multi-turn error propagation, implicit tool-use failures, and precise replanning. The SDE rubric provides effective supervision signals for improving agents' reflective recovery capabilities.

Conclusion: Even state-of-the-art models struggle with multi-turn error propagation, implicit tool-use failures, and precise replanning. The SDE rubric effectively provides supervision signals for improving agents' reflective recovery capabilities, and the benchmark data and code are available at the provided GitHub link.

Abstract: Existing agent benchmarks mainly evaluate final task success or tool-call correctness, providing limited insight into whether agents can reliably diagnose and recover from intermediate execution failures. This limitation becomes particularly critical in multi-turn parallel tool-use scenarios, where errors may propagate across dependent branches and trigger cascading failures. We introduce ParaRecover, a process-level benchmark for evaluating error localization and recovery in multi-turn parallel tool-use agents. Built upon a fine-grained taxonomy of 14 error types covering planning dependencies, tool selection, and argument matching, the benchmark comprises 10,626 instances spanning two difficulty levels. To enable finegrained, process-oriented evaluation, we further propose the SDE rubric, which measures structural integrity, diagnostic reasoning, and evolutionary strategy during agent execution.Experiments across more than ten mainstream LLMs reveal that even state-of-the-art models still struggle with multi-turn error propagation,implicit tool-use failures, and precise replanning. Moreover, we demonstrate that the SDE rubric provides effective supervision signals for improving agents' reflective recovery capabilities. Our data and code are available at https://github.com/gbw206/ParaRecover.

</details>


### [67] [When Connected Does Not Mean Similar: Charting the Homophily Boundary of SNAP-KG for Streaming Entity Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12356)
*Jui-Chien Lin,Oshani Seneviratne*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: SNAP-KG is a framework for assigning newly arriving entities to semantic communities in a growing knowledge graph (KG) using only their raw features, with no graph access and no retraining at inference time. It was evaluated on five multi-view benchmarks and a 2.4M-node OGB-WikiKG2 KG. In each of these datasets, at least one graph view is homophilous, meaning that connected nodes usually belong to the same class, and SNAP-KG performs well on all of them.
