This paper asks what happens outside that setting. We extend the evaluation to three heterophilous graphs (Texas, Wisconsin, Chameleon) and measure the edge homophily of every view. When no homophilous view is available, clustering quality drops sharply for both SNAP-KG and the transductive baselines used in its original evaluation. What decides this is the homophily of the relation, not the number of relations. Multi-view fusion still helps, but only when at least one homophilous relation provides a reliable foundation. The homophily assumption is therefore shared by the whole method family, not specific to SNAP-KG. We argue that heterophilous multi-view clustering is a separate research problem, outside the scope of this work. As future work, we outline how a heterophily-aware teacher could be distilled into SNAP-KG's projector to serve both homophilous and heterophilous KGs.

</details>


### [68] [LatentVerse: A Framework for Understanding Shared and Modality-Specific Information in Multimodal Latent Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12364)
*Majd Alafrange,Samuel Friedman,John Kitonyo,Sana Tonekaboni,Mahnaz Maddah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Latent embeddings have become a central data abstraction in modern machine learning, especially in biomedicine, where foundation models are increasingly used to encode multimodal data like clinical text, medical images, omics, and physiological signals. However, the utility and value of these representations depends on understanding their quality, structure, and the information they encode. Existing analysis workflows for evaluating representations remain fragmented across custom scripts, isolated metrics, and most importantly lack multimodal analysis, limiting accessibility and reproducibility. We present LatentVerse, a representation analysis resource that combines a web-based visual analytics platform for accessible, report-driven exploration with a command-line interface for scalable technical workflows. LatentVerse unifies diagnostics for various representation quality metrics and extends to multimodal settings by decomposing embeddings into shared and modality-specific components. We evaluate LatentVerse through controlled unimodal and multimodal simulations, discovery-oriented analyses on real biomedical embeddings, and a user study across diverse use cases. By supporting thorough and interpretable evaluation of latent spaces, LatentVerse makes foundation model representations more understandable in biomedical and data science applications.

</details>


### [69] [Split Conformal Prediction with Label-Shift-Adjusted Bayesian Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12386)
*Hyeonsu Lee,Juyeon Kim,Erkhembayar Jadamba,Seungjin Choi,Hyunjin Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses label shift in conformal prediction by proposing the Label-Shift-Adjusted Bayesian Score (LSA score), which corrects Bayesian nonconformity scores using a posterior predictive tilting identity to align source predictive distributions with the target domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conformal prediction assumes exchangeability, which is violated under label shift (where P(Y) changes but P(X|Y) remains stable). Existing methods either use residual-based scores (ignore predictive uncertainty, produce uniform intervals) or Bayesian approaches (produce adaptive intervals but misaligned with target domain under label shift).

Method: The authors derive LSA score from a posterior predictive tilting identity showing that the target predictive is an importance-weighted transformation of the source predictive. This provides a direct correction to the Bayesian score. The method is instantiated with Bayesian Ridge Regression, where the correction admits a closed form.

Result: On molecular property prediction under controlled label shift, LSA score consistently yields shorter intervals than residual-based and source-based Bayesian scores while maintaining comparable target domain coverage. Under stronger shift, all methods incur coverage loss due to pseudo-label-based density-ratio estimation.

Conclusion: LSA score provides a principled way to obtain adaptive uncertainty intervals under label shift by correcting Bayesian predictive distributions. The method is generalizable to any source predictive with tractable log-density, with closed-form solutions available for specific models like Bayesian Ridge Regression.

Abstract: Conformal prediction provides distribution-free uncertainty quantification under exchangeability. However, this assumption is violated by label shift, where the marginal distribution of labels changes while the conditional distribution of inputs given labels remains stable. Under such shifts, standard conformal procedures no longer maintain their intended coverage behavior. Existing approaches address this via importance weighting. They pair the reweighting with residual-based nonconformity scores that ignore predictive uncertainty. The resulting intervals have uniform width. Bayesian conformal methods produce adaptive intervals by leveraging predictive distributions. They evaluate conformity under the source predictive, which is misaligned with the target domain under label shift. We propose the \emph{Label-Shift-Adjusted Bayesian Score} (LSA score), a nonconformity score derived from a posterior predictive tilting identity. This identity shows that the target predictive is an importance-weighted transformation of the source predictive. We use it to derive a direct correction to the Bayesian score. We evaluate the method on molecular property prediction under controlled label shift. The LSA score consistently yields shorter intervals than residual-based and source-based Bayesian scores. Coverage in the target domain remains comparable. Under stronger shift, all methods incur some coverage loss due to pseudo-label-based density-ratio estimation. The LSA score is defined for any source predictive with a tractable log-density. We instantiate it with Bayesian Ridge Regression, where the correction admits a closed form.

</details>


### [70] [RiPPLE: Cross-Space Performance Prediction from Early Training for Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12418)
*Yifan Yang,Zhaoyan Wang,Zheng Gao,Xiaoyu Li,Jiaojiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RiPPLE introduces a novel approach to Neural Architecture Search (NAS) that reduces evaluation cost by using partial training as label source for anchors, extrapolating their learning curves, and propagating labels to unevaluated architectures across the search space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation addresses the high cost of NAS: fully training enough architectures to rank an entire search space is expensive, while zero-cost proxies have varying ranking quality, and learned predictors typically require fully trained labels or partial-training features for individual candidates.

Method: RiPPLE treats partial training as a source of labels for a small coverage set of anchors. It trains these anchors to an early prefix, extrapolates their learning curves to surrogate labels, and propagates the labels over label-free architecture features. The method uses feature, readout, and encoding rules selected without held-out accuracy and reused across search spaces.

Result: RiPPLE provides a whole-space ranking from a fractional anchor-training budget. Evaluated on twelve benchmark cells from four search-space families and on the larger DARTS space, examining ranking quality, label efficiency, architecture selection, and the roles of readout, coverage, and propagation.

Conclusion: The method achieves efficient architecture ranking with reduced computational cost while maintaining ranking quality across different search spaces, with comparisons interpreted under their respective evaluation and cost protocols.

Abstract: Neural architecture search (NAS) evaluates candidate networks, but fully training enough architectures to rank an entire space is expensive. Zero-cost proxies score architectures at initialization, yet their ranking quality varies across search spaces. Learned predictors reduce evaluation cost but typically require fully trained labels or partial-training features for individual candidates. We introduce $\textbf{RiPPLE}$, $\underline{\textbf{R}}$anking v$\underline{\textbf{i}}$a $\underline{\textbf{P}}$refix-$\underline{\textbf{P}}$ropagated $\underline{\textbf{L}}$abel $\underline{\textbf{E}}$xtrapolation, which treats partial training as a source of labels for a small coverage set of anchors. RiPPLE trains these anchors to an early prefix, extrapolates their learning curves to surrogate labels, and propagates the labels over label-free architecture features. The early-training signal remains a label on the anchors rather than a per-candidate feature. Feature, readout, and encoding rules are selected without held-out accuracy and reused across search spaces. We evaluate the method on twelve benchmark cells from four search-space families and on the larger DARTS space. The results examine ranking quality, label efficiency, architecture selection, and the roles of readout, coverage, and propagation. RiPPLE provides a whole-space ranking from a fractional anchor-training budget, with comparisons interpreted under their respective evaluation and cost protocols.

</details>


### [71] [MInTRL: Off-policy Intervention can boost On-policy RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12419)
*Mingyu Chen,Yefan Tao,Gerald Friedland,Xuezhou Zhang,Chris Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MInTRL (Minimal Intervention Reinforcement Learning) introduces sparse, local interventions during on-policy rollouts to expand exploration without sacrificing learnability. A judge-intervention policy corrects errors in the current policy's output, then returns control to the policy. Training uses sequence-level advantage-regression to avoid importance sampling. The method outperforms on-policy and off-policy baselines on math and code benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is balancing exploration and learnability in reinforcement learning. On-policy methods are limited to trajectories the policy can discover itself, while off-policy methods can leverage external knowledge but suffer from distribution shift. The goal is to expand exploration without sacrificing learnability.

Method: MInTRL uses sparse, local interventions during generation: a judge-intervention policy periodically reviews the current policy's output, replaces erroneous suffixes with short corrections, and returns control to the policy. Training uses a sequence-level advantage-regression objective that eliminates the need for importance sampling.

Result: MInTRL consistently outperforms standard on-policy and off-policy baselines across math and code benchmarks. Ablations show effectiveness with self-intervention and across different judge policies, with performance peaking at moderate intervention intensity.

Conclusion: Minimal intervention is an effective paradigm for enhancing on-policy RL. Sparse, local interventions can substantially improve coverage beyond finite-budget on-policy sampling while preserving the overall on-policy nature of trajectories.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is typically performed on-policy, keeping training data close to the current policy but limiting learning to trajectories that the policy can discover itself. Off-policy methods such as supervised fine-tuning, on the other hand, can leverage external knowledge beyond the base model's capabilities, but may suffer from large distribution shift. The key challenge is thus to expand exploration without sacrificing learnability. In this work, we introduce Minimal Intervention Reinforcement Learning (MInTRL), which expands the exploration frontier through sparse, local interventions in otherwise on-policy rollouts. During generation, a judge-intervention policy periodically reviews the current policy's output, replaces erroneous suffixes with short corrections, and immediately returns control to the policy. During training, MInTRL adopts a sequence-level advantage-regression objective that eliminates the need for importance sampling. We show that sparse, local interventions can substantially improve coverage beyond finite-budget on-policy sampling while preserving the overall on-policy nature of the resulting trajectories. Across math and code benchmarks, MInTRL consistently outperforms standard on-policy and off-policy baselines. Ablations show that MInTRL remains effective with self-intervention and across different judge policies, while performance peaks at moderate intervention intensity, highlighting the importance of intervening minimally. These results establish minimal intervention as an effective paradigm for enhancing on-policy RL.

</details>


### [72] [Granularity-Adaptive Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon LLM Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12424)
*Taoran Liang,Yang Liu,Shang Luo,Yingguang Yang,Rongrong Zhang,Yingzong Min,Yulin Huang,Jianshen Zhang,Yongzhi Qi,Kefu Xu,Congjing Ran,Bin Chong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GACA introduces an uncertainty-based criticality adaptive advantage estimator that dynamically blends step-level and episode-level advantage signals based on per-step uncertainty (negative log-likelihood), improving over GRPO and GiGPO on ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks at 1.5B and 7B scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing critic-free methods (GRPO) broadcast episode-level rewards to all steps, losing fine-grained credit assignment. GiGPO groups steps by anchor states but uses fixed weighting, treating pivotal decisions and routine transitions equally. The authors argue granularity should be state-dependent.

Method: GACA scores each step using the negative log-likelihood from its own rollout, then blends step-level and episode-level advantages with a per-step weight that increases with NLL. Higher NLL (higher uncertainty) → more weight on fine-grained signal; lower NLL → more weight on episode-level signal.

Result: GACA improves task success over GRPO and GiGPO on ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks at both 1.5B and 7B model scales.

Conclusion: Uncertainty-based adaptive mixing provides better credit assignment than fixed mixing or pure episode-level signals. The method successfully identifies which decisions drove outcomes by leveraging NLL as a criticality proxy.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is now the standard way to train large language model agents on long-horizon tasks, where dozens of interdependent actions precede a single sparse reward. Critic-free, group-relative methods such as GRPO suit this regime, but they broadcast one trajectory-level scalar to every step and cannot say which decision drove the outcome. GiGPO recovers a step-level signal by grouping time steps that share an anchor state, yet it merges the step- and episode-level estimates under one fixed weight, spending the same resolution on a pivotal branching decision as on a routine, near-deterministic transition. We argue that the right resolution is state-dependent, and propose GACA, a critic-free estimator whose granularity follows an uncertainty-based criticality proxy. GACA scores every step by the negative log-likelihood its own rollout already records, then blends the two advantages with a per-step weight that grows with that score, so the gradient places more weight on the fine-grained signal at above-average NLL and on the episode-level signal below it. We derive an exact risk decomposition for the implemented mixture and show that sufficiently small modulation improves on fixed mixing under positive directional alignment. A separate conditional result bounds local action-value variation using expected NLL, while an error-projection analysis characterizes when mixing adds value beyond scalar uncertainty reweighting. On ALFWorld and WebShop, GACA improves task success over GRPO and GiGPO at both 1.5B and 7B scales.

</details>


### [73] [Observation-Anchored Selective Assimilation for Longitudinal Tumor-State Proxy Forecasting in Post-Treatment Glioma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12435)
*Yeonjae Jung,Minwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel digital-twin framework for glioma tumor-state forecasting using post-treatment MRI, featuring SegMamba-based prediction and Observation-Anchored Selective Assimilation (OASA) to update patient-specific tumor-state estimates while maintaining accuracy comparable to persistence methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-treatment MRI provides serial observations for updating tumor-state proxy estimates, but variable appearances and trajectories complicate forecasting. Existing methods struggle with the complexity of glioma progression patterns.

Method: Formulated forecasting as an observation-aware digital-twin update using an intermediate observation as anchor. Used SegMamba-based single-step forecaster to predict update proposals from multimodal source-state tensors. Implemented OASA with validation-selected tiered case-level rules and voxel-wise soft gate for selective assimilation. Compared 7 methods: initial-scan forecasting, rollout without assimilation, latest-observation persistence, direct prediction, OASA, OASA + calibration, and morphological dilation.

Result: Among 120 retained triplets (81/24/15 train/val/test), OASA maintained Dice at τ=0.2 comparable to persistence (0.6071 ± 0.0025 vs 0.6070) while yielding higher Dice at τ=0.5 (0.4269 ± 0.0079 vs 0.3981). Calibration increased Dice at τ=0.2 to 0.6178 ± 0.0025, increased FP support (11,836→18,663), and reduced FN support (22,107→17,536).

Conclusion: OASA effectively maintains forecasting performance while selectively applying updates. Calibration improves near-threshold Dice but reflects support calibration rather than improved biological predictive capability. The approach provides a promising framework for longitudinal tumor-state forecasting in glioma patients.

Abstract: Post-treatment MRI in patients with glioma provides serial observations for updating patient-specific tumor-state proxy estimates, but variable appearances and trajectories complicate forecasting. We formulate forecasting as an observation-aware digital-twin update in which an intermediate observation anchors the patient-specific state. Among 203 patients and 594 follow-up time points, a predefined no-new-treatment criterion retained 120 of 236 candidate triplets, split into 81/24/15 training/validation/test triplets at the patient level. Each time point was represented by a continuous voxel-wise tumor-state proxy map in [0,1] derived from MRI lesion labels. A SegMamba-based single-step forecaster predicted update proposals from multimodal source-state tensors. Observation-Anchored Selective Assimilation (OASA) retained the observed intermediate proxy as the state anchor and selectively applied updates through a validation-selected tiered case-level rule and voxel-wise soft gate. We compared initial-scan forecasting, rollout without assimilation, latest-observation persistence, direct prediction, OASA, OASA + calibration, and morphological dilation. Checkpoints, OASA rules, and calibration thresholds were selected using validation data only. Across three seeds on 15 held-out test triplets, OASA maintained Dice at $τ$ = 0.2 comparable to persistence (0.6071 $\pm$ 0.0025 vs. 0.6070) while yielding numerically higher Dice at $τ$ = 0.5 (0.4269 $\pm$ 0.0079 vs. 0.3981), with a small RMSE increase. Calibration increased Dice at $τ$ = 0.2 to 0.6178 $\pm$ 0.0025, increased false-positive (FP) support (11,836$\rightarrow$18,663), and reduced false-negative (FN) support (22,107$\rightarrow$17,536). This reflects near-threshold support calibration rather than improved biological predictive capability. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/jsudg436/longitudinal-proxy-forecasting.

</details>


### [74] [Beyond the Query: Do Retrieval Signals Improve Adaptive Multimodal RAG Routing?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12437)
*Qiaomu Li,Qiuyuan Zhang,Nong Ming*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether retrieval-time signals add incremental routing value in Adaptive RAG systems beyond what the query alone provides. Across document, audio, and video RAG domains, the authors find that adding retrieval signals does not reliably improve routing decisions compared to query-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive RAG systems often use retrieval-time signals to decide whether to perform additional retrieval, reranking, or multimodal steps. The authors question whether these signals actually add routing value once the query itself is already known, or if they are unnecessarily credited with routing improvements.

Method: The authors conduct a controlled comparison across three modalities (document, audio, and video RAG). They compare matched query-only routers against query+retrieval routers while holding constant: optional actions, router family, training procedure, and evaluation metrics.

Result: Adding tested retrieval signals does not produce reliable routing improvement over the query-only baseline. While some retrieval signals correlate with whether later steps will help, this predictability does not consistently lead to better RUN/SKIP decisions.

Conclusion: Retrieval-state features should not be credited with routing value unless they demonstrably improve over a matched query-only control. The paper provides a methodological lesson: incremental value of retrieval signals must be empirically demonstrated rather than assumed. This does not prove routing or retrieval state is useless, but rather that its value requires proper verification.

Abstract: Adaptive RAG often uses retrieval-time signals to decide whether another retrieval, reranking, or multimodal step should run. We ask whether these signals add routing value once the query itself is already known. Across document, audio, and video RAG, we compare matched query-only and query+retrieval routers while holding the optional actions, router family, training procedure, and evaluation fixed. On the held-out final evaluation, adding the tested retrieval signals does not produce a reliable routing improvement over the query-only baseline. Some retrieval signals are associated with whether a later step will help, but that predictability does not consistently lead to bet- ter RUN/SKIP decisions. The main lesson is therefore methodological: retrieval-state features should not be credited with routing value unless they improve over a matched query-only control. Our results do not show that routing or retrieval state is generally useless; they show that the incremental value of retrieval signals must be demonstrated rather than assumed.

</details>


### [75] [3D Digital Twin Visualization of Multiclass GRF-Based Gait Disorder Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12442)
*Nayoung Son,Minwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An integrated framework for classifying healthy gait and multiple musculoskeletal impairment groups using bilateral GRF and COP signals, achieving 99.00% validation and 90.07% test accuracy, with class-specific ε-LRP for explainability and Blender-based 3D visualization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated gait analysis requires both accurate classification and interpretable outputs to improve model transparency and clinical applicability.

Method: Bilateral GRF and COP signals were normalized over the stance phase and standardized using training-set statistics. A deep learning model was trained for classification, with class-specific ε-LRP applied for explainability. Processed signals and predictions were synchronized within a Blender-based 3D visualization for sample-level inspection.

Result: Validation accuracy of 99.00% and test accuracy of 90.07% under session-level split. ε-LRP identified positive and negative contributions across both sides, multiple signal components, and different stance phases.

Conclusion: The proposed framework integrates classification, explainability, and 3D visualization to improve model transparency, enabling clinicians to inspect gait trials and classification results at the sample level.

Abstract: Automated gait analysis requires accurate classification and interpretable outputs. We propose an integrated framework for classifying healthy gait and multiple musculoskeletal impairment groups using bilateral ground reaction force (GRF) and center-of-pressure (COP) signals. The signals were normalized over the stance phase and standardized using training-set statistics. The model achieved a validation accuracy of 99.00\% and a test accuracy of 90.07\% under a session-level split. Class-specific $ε$-LRP identified positive and negative contributions across both sides, multiple signal components, and different stance phases. Separately, the processed GRF signals and model predictions were synchronized within a Blender-based 3D visualization, enabling sample-level inspection of gait trials and classification results. The proposed framework integrates classification, explainability, and 3D visualization to improve model transparency. The source code is available in the following repository: https://github.com/nyoico/grf-gait-3d-visualization.git

</details>


### [76] [A Differentially Private Federated Proximal Optimization Framework for Customer Churn Prediction in Heterogeneous Federated Telecom Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12470)
*Joydeb Kumar Sana,Subrata Chakraborty,M M Manjurul Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Differentially Private FedProx (DP-FedProx) framework for customer churn prediction in telecommunications. The method addresses privacy concerns and non-IID data challenges in federated learning while providing privacy guarantees with competitive prediction performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The telecommunication industry faces significant customer churn issues. While centralized machine learning is commonly used for churn prediction, it raises privacy concerns and may violate data protection regulations. Additionally, real-world customer data is often heterogeneous (non-IID), which can degrade federated learning performance, and trained models are vulnerable to privacy attacks.

Method: The researchers trained and compared four federated learning models: Federated Averaging (FedAvg), DP-FedAvg, FedProx, and the proposed DP-FedProx framework. They also included centralized and local models as baselines. Experiments were conducted on two public telecom churn datasets using seven evaluation metrics. SHAP analysis was performed for model explainability.

Result: FedProx-based models consistently outperformed FedAvg-based models. The proposed DP-FedProx framework achieved competitive prediction performance compared to the best centralized model with only a small accuracy reduction while providing privacy guarantees. SHAP analysis revealed that the DP-FedProx method prioritizes revenue group features.

Conclusion: The DP-FedProx framework provides a practical balance between prediction performance and data privacy protection for customer churn prediction in the telecom industry, making it a viable solution for collaborative model training across multiple operators.

Abstract: Customer churn is one of the major issues in the telecommunication industry. To predict customer churn, conventional centralized machine learning approaches have been widely used. This centralized approach requires customer data to be stored in a central repository, which raises privacy concerns and may violate data protection regulations. Federated learning addresses this problem by allowing multiple telecom operators to collaboratively train a global model without transferring their raw customer data. However, real-world customer data are often heterogeneous (non-IID), which may negatively affect the performance of standard federated learning. Trained models can also suffer from privacy attacks. To address those issues, we propose a Differentially Private (DP) based Federated Proximal optimization (FedProx) framework. All experiments were performed on two publicly available telecom churn datasets. We trained Federated Averaging (FedAvg), DP-FedAvg, FedProx, and the proposed DP-FedProx framework. For baseline comparison, we also used several centralized and local models. To evaluate the models, we employed seven widely used evaluation metrics. The experimental results show that the FedProx based models consistently outperform the FedAvg based models. Compared with the best centralized model, the proposed DP-FedProx framework achieves competitive prediction performance with only a small reduction in accuracy while providing privacy guarantees. To explain our model, we conducted SHAP analysis which shows that DP-FedProx method priorities revenue group features. These results indicate that the proposed DP-FedProx framework provides a practical balance between prediction performance and data privacy protection.

</details>


### [77] [Temporal Recurrence Favors Fewer Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12531)
*Ivan Anokhin,Johan Obando-Ceron,Irina Rish,Sebastian Risi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates optimal compute allocation between temporal recurrence and within-step depth in streaming tasks. By varying depth, expert width, and parallel experts across compute budgets, the study finds that temporal recurrence shifts the optimal allocation toward substantially fewer layers while achieving comparable or better performance on Sokoban and FineWeb language modeling tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To answer the fundamental question: once temporal recurrence provides sequential computation across time steps, how much depth is still needed within each step? This addresses the compute-allocation trade-off between depth and recurrence in recurrent models.

Method: Varies within-step depth, expert width, and number of parallel experts per layer across several compute budgets. Compares the best observed recurrent and non-recurrent allocations under approximately matched per-step computation across Sokoban and autoregressive FineWeb language modeling tasks.

Result: Temporal recurrence shifts the best observed compute allocation toward substantially fewer layers, with comparable or better performance compared to non-recurrent baselines.

Conclusion: Temporal recurrence can effectively replace substantial within-step depth, allowing for more efficient model architectures that rely on recurrence across time rather than deep processing within each step.

Abstract: In streaming tasks, recurrent models can carry latent computation across time, allowing each update to build on representations produced earlier. This raises a basic question: once temporal recurrence provides sequential computation across steps, how much depth is still needed within each step? Prior work has shown that recurrence can make shallow models competitive. We instead study this question as a compute-allocation problem, varying within-step depth, expert width, and the number of parallel experts per layer across several compute budgets. For each budget, we compare the best observed recurrent and non-recurrent allocations and the performance they achieve under approximately matched per-step computation. Across Sokoban and autoregressive FineWeb language modeling, we find that temporal recurrence shifts the best observed compute allocation toward substantially fewer layers, with comparable or better performance.

</details>


### [78] [$\text{GSF-}χ$: Global Stereochemical Fields for Chiral Graph Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12532)
*Jiaqing Xie,Yuxin Wang,Xipeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GSF-χ is a graph transformer architecture that encodes molecular chirality by using stereogenic units to modulate all pairwise interactions, featuring a Chiral-RoPE mechanism that inverts under reflection and a C_2 projection for ECD spectral analysis. The method achieves state-of-the-art results on central-ECD, axial Rotation, and Symbol tasks, with the Rotation improvement attributed to global signed support rather than model capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enantiomers (mirror-image molecules) share identical atoms, bonds, and pairwise distances but exhibit different behaviors in chiral environments. Current molecular encoders fail to properly handle chirality - they either become blind to reflection or cannot distinguish between enantiomers. A proper encoder must respect atom relabelings and proper rotations while remaining sensitive to improper rotations (reflections).

Method: The paper introduces GSF-χ, a graph transformer where stereogenic units (central or axial) modulate all pairwise interactions rather than treating single atoms as special. Key technical innovations: (1) Reflection-even phase fields created by stereogenic units over all atoms, (2) A handedness pseudoscalar χ that sets direction of relative rotation on latent query-key blocks (Chiral-RoPE), which reflection inverts rather than leaves fixed, (3) C_2 projection separating mirror-even ECD peak counts/positions from mirror-odd peak signs.

Result: GSF-χ leads every central-ECD output and improves axial Rotation and Symbol tasks by 12.6% and 7.9% over the strongest baseline. Equal-budget controls show the Rotation advantage comes from global signed support rather than parameter or edge count. The C_2 projection yields exact enantiomer-pair consistency at small raw-accuracy cost under complete supervision and becomes predictive when mirror supervision is scarce.

Conclusion: The method successfully encodes molecular chirality through stereogenic unit modulation and Chiral-RoPE. The C_2 projection provides theoretical guarantees for enantiomer consistency while maintaining practical utility. The improvement in Rotation tasks is specifically attributed to the global signed support mechanism rather than increased model capacity.

Abstract: Enantiomers share atoms, bonds, and pairwise distances yet can behave differently in chiral environments, so molecular encoders must respect atom relabelings and proper rotations without becoming blind to reflection. We introduce GSF-$χ$, a graph transformer in which stereogenic units modulate all pairwise interactions rather than single out one atom as special. Each central or axial stereogenic unit creates a reflection-even phase field over all atoms, a handedness pseudoscalar $χ$ sets the direction of a relative rotation on latent query--key blocks, giving a \textbf{Chiral-RoPE} that reflection inverts rather than leaves fixed. A $C_2$ projection separates mirror-even ECD peak counts and positions from mirror-odd peak signs. We prove the operator's even--odd decomposition and its annotation-inversion, permutation, and unit-order identities under explicit canonical-role conditions; property tests and a coordinate-reflection audit verify the laws end to end. GSF-$χ$ leads every central-ECD output and improves axial Rotation and Symbol by $12.6\%$ and $7.9\%$ over the strongest baseline. Equal-budget controls attribute the Rotation advantage to global signed support rather than parameter or edge count; the $C_2$ projection yields exact enantiomer-pair consistency at a small raw-accuracy cost under complete supervision and becomes predictive when mirror supervision is scarce.

</details>


### [79] [Quality-Constrained Routing over a Fixed Pool of Quantized Mixture-of-Experts Instances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12550)
*Zhenghong Huang,Hongfan Wu,Jiheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses efficient routing in quantized Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) systems by introducing FWP (Fragility-Weighted Perplexity), a metric that predicts quantization degradation for each request. The method routes requests within a fixed resident pool to maximize throughput under quality constraints, achieving a 2.5% relative improvement over baseline approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantization damage in MoE systems varies significantly across requests and bitwidths, but instance materialization and replica counts consume memory and require slow reconfiguration. Existing methods treat these as upstream provisioning decisions, leaving routing within a fixed pool as an underexplored problem.

Method: 1) FWP (Fragility-Weighted Perplexity) computed from prompt tokens on reference-instance prefill, calibrated to candidate-instance degradation; 2) Exact two-expert affinity-fragility decomposition; 3) Conditional multi-layer top-k expansion with explicit bias, interaction, route-change, separability, and higher-order terms; 4) Window-level linear program yielding signed reduced-reward scores KKT-consistent with LP optimum under optimal prices and primal-feasible tie allocation.

Result: On 88 extended Qwen prompts with W2, W3, W4 quantization of all 6,144 expert blocks: mean ΔNLL of 0.9437, 0.1832, and 0.0513 respectively. Under τ=0.1513, FWP allocation achieves 1.284× offline model-based multiplier versus 1.253× for request-agnostic mixing and 1.000× for static W4, representing a 2.5% relative improvement.

Conclusion: FWP-based routing within a fixed resident pool effectively maximizes throughput under quality-degradation budgets. The calibrated risk prediction enables informed routing decisions that outperform request-agnostic approaches, demonstrating the value of request-specific quantization fragility assessment.

Abstract: Quantized Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) services can hold several pre-materialized instances of one base model, but quantization damage varies sharply across requests and bitwidths. Because instance materialization and replica counts consume memory and require slow reconfiguration, we treat them as upstream provisioning decisions and study routing within a fixed resident pool. Within this fixed-pool boundary, we route each request to maximize modeled throughput under a class-level expected quality-degradation budget and measured instance capacities. To predict this request-specific risk, we introduce FWP (Fragility-Weighted Perplexity), computed from prompt tokens on a reference-instance prefill and calibrated to candidate-instance degradation. Underlying FWP is an exact two-expert affinity--fragility decomposition and a conditional multi-layer top-$k$ expansion whose bias, interaction, route-change, separability, and higher-order terms remain explicit. Using these calibrated risks, a window-level linear program yields a signed reduced-reward score that is KKT-consistent with the LP optimum under optimal prices and primal-feasible tie allocation. On 88 extended Qwen prompts, complete W2, W3, and W4 instances quantizing all 6,144 expert blocks incur mean $Δ$NLL of $0.9437$, $0.1832$, and $0.0513$. Under the same population and $τ=0.1513$, FWP allocation reaches a $1.284\times$ offline model-based multiplier versus $1.253\times$ for request-agnostic mixing and $1.000\times$ for static W4, an incremental $2.5\%$ relative FWP gain.

</details>


### [80] [TokenMapper: A Step Toward Interoperable Speech Token Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12563)
*Tal Kozakov,Tal Rosenwein,Eliya Nachmani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TokenMapper enables direct token-to-token translation between heterogeneous neural audio codecs (different vocabularies and codebook structures) without requiring waveform reconstruction, addressing latency and information loss issues in cross-model speech systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Different neural audio codecs produce tokens with incompatible spaces (vocabulary, codebook structure), forcing applications like conversational voice agents and speech-to-speech translation to decode to waveform and re-encode, causing latency and information loss.

Method: TokenMapper: a direction-aware framework for direct token-to-token translation in the discrete domain. Supports structurally mismatched token spaces including single-to-multi codebook mappings under shared effective token rate. Evaluated on GLM-4-Voice, MiMi, and DualCodec.

Result: Translation WER reaches within 2.5-6.8% absolute of native reconstructions. Human MOS: 2.29-4.39, following same direction trends as UTMOS. End-to-end latency reduced by 4.8-94.5% vs waveform bridging, up to 972ms per utterance saved.

Conclusion: TokenMapper provides practical cross-model speech token interoperability without intermediate waveform reconstruction, enabling efficient communication between heterogeneous speech models.

Abstract: Neural audio codecs discretize speech into token sequences, but the resulting token spaces differ in vocabulary and codebook structure, preventing direct communication across models. This limitation affects applications such as conversational voice agents and speech to speech translation systems where multiple speech models must interact. As a result, transferring information between speech systems typically requires decoding to waveform audio and re-encoding with a second tokenizer, increasing latency and introducing potential information loss. To address these limitations, we present TokenMapper, a direction aware framework for direct token to token translation between heterogeneous speech tokenizers in the discrete domain. TokenMapper supports structurally mismatched token spaces, including mappings between single codebook and multi codebook representations, under a shared effective token rate. Experiments on GLM-4-Voice, MiMi and DualCodec show consistent cross model performance. Specifically, translation WER approaches native reconstructions within 2.5-6.8% absolute WER, human MOS for TokenMapper outputs ranges from 2.29 to 4.39, following the same direction level trends as UTMOS and end to end latency is reduced by 4.8-94.5% relative to waveform bridging, reaching up to 972 ms per utterance. These results provide a practical step toward cross model speech token interoperability without intermediate waveform reconstruction.

</details>


### [81] [SCOPE-OPSD: Fisher-Conditioned Privileged Subspaces for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12579)
*Yunmeng Chen,Kunyu Wang,Peihan Li,Yi Wang,Shuyin Xia,Yi Liu,Xinyong Cheng,Dehui Wang,Xiangyong Zhai,Yanxing Liu,Song Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCOPE-OPSD introduces a second supervision channel for on-policy self-distillation by projecting teacher-student residuals onto a Fisher-conditioned rank-64 subspace, achieving consistent gains over baseline OPSD and random controls across multiple Qwen3 model sizes and training steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether the aligned final-layer discrepancy between teacher and student offers a useful second supervision channel beyond next-token probabilities in OPSD, and how to test this channel without confounding its geometry with auxiliary strength.

Method: SCOPE-OPSD projects the privileged teacher-student residual onto a frozen rank-64 factor estimated from residual covariance and language-model-head Fisher sensitivity. It reuses forward passes required by OPSD without adding rollouts or inference-time modules. A matched Random control preserves the structured factor's rank and nonzero spectrum with per-arm gradient-RMS calibration to isolate the effect of data-dependent orientation.

Result: Across 25/50/75/100-step trajectories for Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B: Structured is never below Pure OPSD with strict gains in 11/12 combinations and one tie. Structured exceeds matched Random in 10/12 combinations. At step 75 on Qwen3-1.7B, Structured outperforms matched Random by 1.39 Macro Avg@12 points across two independent reruns. Cross-fitted diagnostic shows 4.40× greater held-out privileged-gap capture than random orientation.

Conclusion: The results support a compact, Fisher-conditioned privileged subspace for short-budget OPSD, demonstrating that final-layer discrepancy provides a useful second supervision channel when properly conditioned on Fisher information.

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) scores student-generated prefixes with a solution-conditioned self-teacher, yet transfers supervision only through next-token probabilities. We ask whether the aligned final-layer discrepancy offers a useful second channel, and how to test that channel without confusing its geometry with auxiliary strength. SCOPE-OPSD projects the privileged teacher-student residual onto a frozen rank-64 factor estimated from residual covariance and language-model-head Fisher sensitivity. It reuses the forwards already required by OPSD and adds neither rollouts nor inference-time modules. A matched Random control preserves the structured factor's rank and nonzero spectrum and uses per-arm gradient-RMS calibration, isolating the effect of the data-dependent orientation. Across the complete 25/50/75/100-step trajectories for Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B, Structured is never below Pure OPSD, with strict gains in 11 of the 12 model-checkpoint combinations and an exact tie at 4B step 25. Structured also exceeds matched Random in 10 of the 12 combinations. At step 75 on Qwen3-1.7B, Structured exceeds matched Random by 1.39 Macro Avg@12 points in each of two independent training reruns. A cross-fitted diagnostic also shows 4.40 times greater held-out privileged-gap capture than the matched random orientation. The results support a compact, Fisher-conditioned privileged subspace for short-budget OPSD.

</details>


### [82] [Clustering-Based Balanced Sampling and Allocation with Data Parallelism for High-Performance Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12584)
*Hyunjin Kim,Youngeun Nam,Jaemin Han,Wonhyeok Choi,Jae-Gil Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CluSTER addresses inefficient instruction-tuning for LLMs by using gradient-space clustering and DP-aware balanced sampling to reduce redundant computation while maintaining model quality. Achieves up to 69.6% training time reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instruction-tuning datasets are large, redundant, and imbalanced. Naive large-batch fine-tuning overrepresents sample groups while underrepresenting informative ones, especially under data parallelism across multiple GPUs.

Method: CluSTER uses gradient-space clustering to curate a representative reduced dataset, with DP-aware balanced allocation ensuring dual-level coverage across clusters and workers. It preserves original data distribution through weighted updates.

Result: CluSTER reduces training time by up to 69.6% with almost no accuracy loss compared to prior sampling and data reduction methods, across multiple instruction-tuning datasets.

Conclusion: CluSTER effectively reduces redundant computation and improves training stability without compromising model quality, offering a practical solution for efficient LLM instruction-tuning in distributed settings.

Abstract: Instruction-tuning datasets for large language models (LLMs) are often large, redundant, and imbalanced, limiting efficient adaptation. Naive large-batch fine-tuning repeatedly includes overrepresented sample groups while weakly covering underrepresented but informative ones, especially under data parallelism (DP) across multiple GPUs. We propose CluSTER, a Cluster-aware balanced Sampling framework for Training Efficient data Reduction in DP instruction tuning. CluSTER curates a representative reduced dataset through gradient-space clustering and DP-aware balanced allocation, ensuring dual-level coverage across clusters and workers, while preserving the original data distribution by weighted update. As a result, CluSTER reduces redundant computation and improves training stability without compromising model quality. Across multiple instruction-tuning datasets, CluSTER reduces training time by up to 69.6% with almost no accuracy loss compared to prior sampling and data reduction methods. Code is available at https://github.com/kaist-dmlab/CluSTER.

</details>


### [83] [Where Decoder Cosine Similarity Fails for SAE Feature Flow Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12591)
*Hendrik Droste,Christian Medeiros Adriano,Kathrin Korte,Holger Giese*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates how state features and update features interact in foundation models using sparse autoencoders (SAEs). The researchers constructed a "transition atlas" to map how residual-state features and MLP-update features jointly predict target residual features, validating candidate transitions through ablation experiments on Pythia-160M and Gemma-3-4B models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As foundation models are increasingly adapted through fine-tuning, model editing, and alignment procedures, understanding the internal computations that support these adaptations becomes crucial. The motivation is to develop tools for steering model updates by mapping how features flow through the model.

Method: The researchers constructed a transition atlas of triples (s_k + u_j → t_ℓ) where residual-state features and MLP-update features jointly predict target residual features. They validated candidate triples by ablating the decoded update feature. Experiments were conducted on: 1) A 20M-token Pythia-160M model (L_7 → L_8), and 2) A 20M-token Gemma-3-4B model (L_21 → L_22) for cross-model validation.

Result: In the Pythia-160M run, they found 38,125 strong ablation-effect transitions, but 88.0% had both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. In the Gemma-3-4B run, only the top 30,000 candidate triples were validated, and 53.6% of strong-effect triples had both similarities below 0.7. The Gemma result was directionally consistent with Pythia but weaker.

Conclusion: The results suggest that feature flow atlases can serve as diagnostics of representation-update mechanisms and inform tools for steering model updates. However, many strong transitions have low cosine similarity, indicating complex feature interactions. Future work will validate more complex patterns across layers, models, and SAE families.

Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly adapted through fine-tuning, model editing, and alignment procedures while retaining previously acquired capabilities. Understanding the internal computations that support these adaptations is therefore becoming increasingly important for continual model evolution. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) provide interpretable feature dictionaries for residual-stream activations and sublayer outputs, but it remains unclear how state features and update features interact to produce downstream residual features. In this work, we focus on MLP updates as a first test case. We construct a transition atlas of triples $s_k + u_j \rightarrow t_\ell$, where a residual-state feature and an MLP-update feature jointly predict a target residual feature, and validate candidate triples by ablating the decoded update feature. In a 20M-token Pythia-160M $L_7 \rightarrow L_8$ run, we find 38,125 strong ablation-effect transitions, but 88.0% have both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. As a preliminary cross-model check, a run of 20M-token Gemma-3-4B $L_{21} \rightarrow L_{22}$ causally validates only the top 30,000 ranked candidate triples by ablating the decoded update feature, and 53.6% of strong-effect triples have both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. The Gemma result is directionally consistent with Pythia, but weaker, since update-target cosine recovers many of the strongest Gemma effects and the run is not a full-atlas causal validation. Ultimately, our results suggest that feature flow atlases can serve as diagnostics of representation-update mechanisms and thereby inform tools for steering model updates. Future work will validate more complex patterns across layers, models, and SAE families.

</details>


### [84] [SIMS: Scale-Invariant Merit-Function-Based Scalarization for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12599)
*Zebin Chen,Fei Xing,Yang Chen,Hua Liu,Andy HF Chow,Yuhua Qian,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses a key issue in multi-task learning (MTL) where existing scalarization methods in multi-objective optimization (MOO) are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives. The authors propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS), which uses a logarithmic transformation to make optimization invariant to the magnitudes of losses, since Pareto optimality is invariant to positive scaling of objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that existing merit-function-based scalarization approaches are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives in practical MTL, where task losses commonly differ by orders of magnitude. The optimization process often favors objectives with larger scales even though the underlying Pareto optimal solutions remain invariant to rescaling (i.e., multiplying an objective by a positive constant). This is a fundamental issue that affects the quality of solutions in multi-task learning.

Method: The proposed method, SIMS (Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization), adopts a transformation-induced merit function to convert the multi-objective optimization problem of MTL to a single objective that renders optimization invariant to the magnitudes of losses. Theoretically, the authors prove that the requirement for scale invariance uniquely determines this transformation to be logarithmic. They further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error.

Result: The paper demonstrates through extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the logarithmic transformation is the unique solution for achieving scale invariance in multi-objective optimization for multi-task learning. SIMS provides a theoretically grounded and practically effective approach to handling the scale sensitivity problem in MTL, outperforming existing methods on benchmark datasets.

Abstract: Multi-task learning (MTL) requires navigating unavoidable trade-offs among competing objectives. This paradigm is frequently formulated as multi-objective optimization (MOO), where the scalarization is favored to reduce an MOO problem to a single objective. We empirically find that existing merit-function-based scalarization approaches are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives in practical MTL, where task losses commonly differ by orders of magnitude. The optimization process often favors objectives with larger scales even though the underlying Pareto optimal solutions remains invariant to rescaling (i.e., multiplying an objective by a positive constant). To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. Specifically, SIMS adopts a transformation-induced merit function to convert the MOO problem of MTL to a single objective that renders optimization invariant to the magnitudes of losses. Theoretically, we prove that the requirement for scale invariance uniquely determines this transformation to be logarithmic. We further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error. Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [85] [Correlation-Guided Fast Machine Unlearning via Hessian Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12620)
*Ayushi Thakur,Ruchir Gupta,Amit Kumar Jaiswal,Prayag Tiwari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a computationally efficient machine unlearning framework for security systems that achieves 82× speedup over existing influence function methods while preserving model utility. The method identifies correlated data points and applies a closed-form parameter update rule with Hessian damping for numerical stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing adoption of ML in network and distributed security systems (intrusion detection, spam filters, threat intelligence) creates urgent need for mechanisms to selectively remove specific training data. This is driven by: 1) eliminating compromised/adversarial data points from production models, 2) GDPR's "right to be forgotten" privacy regulations. Existing approximate unlearning techniques are computationally prohibitive due to repeated expensive Hessian-inverse-vector computations.

Method: The proposed framework: 1) Identifies correlated data points in the training set, 2) Applies a theoretically derived closed-form parameter update rule, 3) Ensures numerical stability through Hessian damping. The approach was evaluated across seven diverse dataset-architecture combinations including large-scale CIFAR-100 with ResNet-50.

Result: Achieved 82× wall-clock speedup over standard influence function unlearning while preserving model utility with 10^-2 improvement in accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines. Demonstrated superior forgetting effectiveness with membership inference attack success rate of 0.660 and tug-of-war score of 0.950.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a computationally efficient solution for machine unlearning in security systems, with theoretical guarantees and numerical stability. It successfully balances the trade-off between forgetting effectiveness and model utility, making it practical for real-world deployment in security applications.

Abstract: The increasing adoption of machine learning in network and distributed security systems has created an urgent need for mechanisms that can selectively and efficiently remove the influence of specific training data to eliminate compromised or adversarial data points from production models. Privacy regulations such as GDPR's \emph{right to be forgotten} also pose similar requirements. However, existing approximate unlearning techniques remain computationally prohibitive for deployment in real-world security systems, as they require repeated expensive Hessian-inverse-vector computations for each data point removal, creating a bottleneck when processing multiple related requests in scenarios such as intrusion detection systems, spam filters, and threat intelligence platforms. Thus, we introduce a computationally efficient unlearning framework that identifies correlated data points in the training set and applies a theoretically derived closed-form parameter update rule, achieving an $82\times$ wall-clock speedup over standard influence function unlearning while preserving model utility with a $10^{-2}$ improvement in accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines. Our method establishes theoretical guarantees and ensures numerical stability through Hessian damping. Our evaluation across seven diverse dataset architecture combinations, including large-scale CIFAR-100 with ResNet-50, demonstrates superior forgetting effectiveness, with membership inference attack success rates of 0.660 and tug-of-war scores of 0.950.

</details>


### [86] [Geometric-to-Semantic Spherical Transfer Learning for Cortical Sulci Labeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12627)
*Saeb Tounsi,Joël Chavas,Pietro Gori,Vincent Frouin,Denis Rivière,Jean-François Mangin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Geometric-to-Semantic Spherical Transfer Learning framework for sulcal labeling on cortical surfaces that uses self-supervised pre-training on ~30,000 unlabeled UK Biobank subjects to overcome extreme data scarcity (N=62 labeled subjects), achieving a mean Dice of 0.77.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning on cortical surfaces faces a dilemma: capturing complex sulcal topology requires high-capacity models, but the extreme scarcity of expert annotations (N=62) causes severe overfitting. Standard supervised approaches fail to generalize, especially for variable and small tertiary sulci with high topological ambiguity.

Method: The framework uses a two-stage approach: (1) Pre-training a spherical encoder on massive unlabeled data (UK Biobank, ~30,000 subjects) using continuous surface features (curvature and depth) in a self-supervised manner; (2) Integrating extracted sulcal fundi (semantic lines) into the pre-trained backbone via a soft-initialized Topological Prior Injector to bridge the geometric-to-semantic domain gap without catastrophic forgetting.

Result: The approach achieves a mean Dice of 0.77, outperforming fully supervised baselines trained from scratch. Local analysis shows self-supervised geometric priors yield the largest gains on variable and tertiary sulci (up to 14.8% improvement), confirming that learning cortex shape is highly beneficial for identifying its rarest parts.

Conclusion: Pre-training on large unlabeled datasets using geometric features (curvature and depth) creates effective topological priors that significantly improve labeling of rare and variable sulcal structures. The Geometric-to-Semantic Transfer Learning framework successfully bridges the domain gap between purely geometric features and semantic anatomical lines, enabling effective deep learning with limited expert annotations.

Abstract: Deep learning on cortical surfaces faces a dilemma: capturing the complex topology of over 60 nomenclature-dependent sulci per hemisphere requires high-capacity models, yet the extreme scarcity of expert annotations ($N=62$ subjects) inevitably causes overfitting. Standard supervised approaches fail to generalize in this data-scarce regime, particularly for variable and small sulci where topological ambiguity is high. To overcome this limitation, we introduce a Geometric-to-Semantic Spherical Transfer Learning framework.
  First, we leverage massive unlabeled data (UK Biobank, $\approx$30,000 subjects) to pre-train a spherical encoder using a locally-optimized strategy. By relying solely on continuous surface features (curvature and depth), the relevance of this pre-training is confirmed by the model's ability to detect localized and rare topological traits, such as sulcal interruptions. The downstream labeling task, however, introduces extracted sulcal fundi (lines) as an explicit semantic input. To bridge this dimensional domain gap (from purely geometric to semantic) without causing catastrophic forgetting, these anatomical lines are integrated into the pre-trained backbone via a soft-initialized Topological Prior Injector.
  Our experiments demonstrate that this approach outperforms fully supervised baselines trained from scratch, achieving a mean Dice of 0.77. Crucially, a local analysis reveals that the self-supervised geometric priors yield the largest performance gains on variable and tertiary sulci (up to 14.8%), confirming that learning the cortex shape is highly beneficial for identifying its rarest parts.

</details>


### [87] [Explaining Time Series Forecasting with Horizon-Resolved Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12639)
*Seunghan Lee,Jun Seo,Jaehoon Lee,Junhyeok Kang,Sangjun Han,Sungdong Yoo,Minjae Kim,Tae Yoon Lim,Dongwan Kang,Hwanil Choi,Soonyoung Lee,Wonbin Ahn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Horizon-Resolved eXplanation (HRX), a framework that provides step-specific importance maps for time series forecasting, addressing the limitation that existing methods assume all forecast steps depend on the same past values.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time series explanation methods output a single importance vector, assuming all predicted steps depend on the same past values. However, this assumption is flawed—as different forecast steps actually depend on different past values, requiring horizon-specific explanations.

Method: HRX is a plug-in framework with three components: 1) an estimator that extracts importance maps from any differentiable forecaster without modifying the backbone, 2) an evaluation protocol that validates the horizon axis by measuring forecast step changes when high-ranked inputs are removed, and 3) a rank criterion that predicts whether horizon resolution is worthwhile for a given time series. The method leverages the finding that step-wise dependence is low-dimensional, with explanations built from a few shared maps that don't grow with forecast length.

Result: Experiments across various backbones and datasets demonstrate that the improvement comes from the horizon axis and that HRX holds for estimators of previous explanation methods.

Conclusion: The horizon axis provides meaningful improvements in time series explanations, and the low-dimensional structure of step-wise dependence makes HRX computationally efficient while maintaining effectiveness across different forecasting models.

Abstract: Recent advances in explaining time series (TS) models have produced methods that identify which past values a prediction depends on. However, most existing methods return a single importance vector, assuming that every predicted step depends on the same past values. In this paper, we show that this assumption does not hold, as different forecast steps depend on different past values. Motivated by this observation, we propose Horizon-Resolved eXplanation (HRX), which adds a horizon axis to the explanation, so that every forecast step receives its own importance map. HRX is a simple yet effective plug-in framework with three components: 1) an estimator that reads these maps out of any differentiable forecaster without modifying the TS backbone, 2) an evaluation protocol that validates the horizon axis by measuring how much a single forecast step changes when the inputs an importance map ranks highest are removed, and 3) a rank criterion that predicts in advance whether the axis is worth resolving on a given TS. We further show that this step-wise dependence is low-dimensional, as the explanations of all steps are built from a few shared maps whose number does not grow with the forecast length. Extensive experiments across various backbones and datasets show that the improvement comes from the horizon axis and holds for estimators of previous explanation methods. Code is available at https://github.com/seunghan96/HRX.

</details>


### [88] [Distortion of AI Alignment Revisited: RLHF is a Decent Utilitarian Aligner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12651)
*Kazusato Oko,Annie Ulichney,Nika Haghtalab,Han Bao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the distortion of Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) in pluralistic settings with heterogeneous user preferences. Previous work showed exponential distortion scaling with the Bradley-Terry temperature parameter β. The authors demonstrate this degradation is due to distribution mismatch between the preference data distribution (μ) and the KL reference policy (π_ref), not a fundamental property of RLHF.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard RLHF paradigm for aligning large language models has been questioned in pluralistic settings. Prior work showed exponential distortion scaling with β when users have heterogeneous preferences. This work investigates whether this is fundamental to RLHF or caused by distribution mismatch.

Method: Fine-grained analysis of RLHF distortion with reward clipping, establishing tight upper and lower bounds across multiple KL regularization strength regimes under the Bradley-Terry model.

Result: The distortion is Θ(βB + β), where B is the upper bound on log density ratio between μ and π_ref. When no distribution mismatch exists (μ = π_ref), RLHF achieves optimal distortion of O(β).

Conclusion: Exponential degradation is not inherent to RLHF but caused by distribution mismatch. To maximize average utility, prefer on-policy sampled preference data or fine-tune before RLHF on data from a source close to μ.

Abstract: While Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is the standard paradigm for aligning large language models with human preferences, its effectiveness in pluralistic settings has been called into question. Notably, recent work by Gölz et al. (2025) demonstrated that the \textit{distortion} -- defined as the multiplicative gap between the average user utility of the RLHF policy and the optimal average utility -- can scale exponentially with the Bradley-Terry temperature parameter $β$ when users have heterogeneous preferences. In this work, we present a fine-grained analysis of the distortion of RLHF with reward clipping and demonstrate that such exponential degradation is not a fundamental property of the algorithm but rather a consequence of distribution mismatch between the distribution generating preference data ($μ$) and the KL reference policy ($π_{\mathrm{ref}}$). To this end, we establish tight upper and lower bounds on the distortion of RLHF across multiple regimes of the KL regularization strength. We show that in a representative regime, under the Bradley-Terry model, the distortion is $\tildeΘ(βB + β)$, where $B$ is an upper bound on the log density ratio between $μ$ and $π_{\mathrm{ref}}$. In particular, when there is no distribution mismatch (i.e., $μ= π_{\mathrm{ref}}$), RLHF achieves the optimal distortion of $O(β)$ up to a constant. Our results suggest that, to reasonably maximize average utility with RLHF, it is preferable to use on-policy sampled preference data or to fine-tune before RLHF on data from a source close to $μ$.

</details>


### [89] [ProactiveBench: Can Streaming Video Models Really Interact Like Humans?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12658)
*Kaixuan Du,Xin Wan,YuKun Wang,Hang Zhang,Meng Cao,Dai Guan,Ming Chen,Ni Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProactiveBench evaluates streaming video understanding models on proactive interaction rather than reactive querying. It tests models at one-second intervals without explicit response cues across six subtasks with varying trigger ambiguity and timing tolerance. The evaluation reveals that premature responses outnumber missed responses in 4 out of 6 evaluated systems, highlighting a significant gap in temporal decision-making for human-like interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current streaming video understanding evaluations are reactive—they query models at selected timestamps but fail to assess when models should respond. Human-like interaction requires proactive behavior: monitoring standing requests, responding within appropriate intervals after target events, and remaining silent otherwise. This gap in evaluation methodology prevents proper assessment of temporal decision-making capabilities.

Method: ProactiveBench evaluates models at one-second stream intervals without explicit response cues. It includes six subtasks varying in trigger ambiguity and timing tolerance: (1) Event Sensitivity, which geometrically combines response and silence rates on the same recording; (2-5) Four window-based subtasks distinguishing early, in-window, and missed responses; and (6) Duplicate Counting, which penalizes both omissions and repetitions.

Result: Premature responses outnumber missed responses for four out of the six evaluated systems, revealing a substantial gap in the temporal decision-making required for human-like interaction.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates that current streaming video understanding models struggle with temporal decision-making—they tend to respond too early rather than missing responses entirely. ProactiveBench provides a standardized benchmark for evaluating proactive interaction capabilities in multimodal streaming contexts, addressing a critical gap in existing evaluation paradigms.

Abstract: Streaming video understanding requires models to process continuous multimodal input while maintaining temporal context. Existing evaluations are predominantly reactive: they query a model at a selected timestamp and therefore do not assess when it should respond. Proactive interaction instead requires monitoring a standing request, responding within an appropriate interval after the target event, and otherwise remaining silent. We introduce ProactiveBench, which evaluates models at one-second stream intervals without an explicit response cue. Its six subtasks vary trigger ambiguity and timing tolerance. Event Sensitivity geometrically combines response and silence rates on the same recording; four window-based subtasks distinguish early, in-window, and missed responses; and Duplicate Counting penalizes omissions and repetitions. Premature responses outnumber missed responses for four of the six evaluated systems, revealing a substantial gap in the temporal decision-making required for human-like interaction.

</details>


### [90] [SIFPBPNet: A Dual-Path Network for Wearable and Cuffless Blood Pressure Estimation via Individualized Steady-state Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12690)
*Shuailong Tang,Xiaoyu Li,Donglin Xie,Wei Chen,Guangpu Zhu,Yelei Li,Yali Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes SIFPBPNet, a dual-path deep learning architecture for cuffless blood pressure monitoring using PPG signals. The model addresses population heterogeneity and the "one-to-many mapping" problem through separate steady-state and instantaneous feature extraction paths, achieving MAE of 8.57/5.97 mmHg for systolic/diastolic BP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous cuffless blood pressure monitoring using photoplethysmography (PPG) is valuable for low-cost, personalized cardiovascular health management. However, two critical challenges limit accuracy: (1) significant population heterogeneity, and (2) the "one-to-many mapping" problem where similar waveforms across individuals correspond to different BP levels. Conventional population-based models fail to address these issues effectively.

Method: The proposed SIFPBPNet employs a dual-path architecture: (1) Steady-state Feature Path (SFP) - uses a Graph Attention Network (GAT) to extract individual-specific and long-term characteristics from multi-day historical PPG trajectories; (2) Instantaneous Feature Path (IFP) - captures short-term dynamics from current PPG segments. A cross-attention mechanism incorporates steady-state prior into the instantaneous feature processing. The SFP module is designed as a plug-and-play component that can be integrated into various backbone architectures.

Result: Experiments on a large-scale wearable dataset demonstrate that SIFPBPNet achieves a Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of 8.57 mmHg for systolic BP and 5.97 mmHg for diastolic BP, outperforming state-of-the-art models. The SFP module consistently improves performance when integrated into various backbone architectures, yielding 2.8-13.1% relative MAE reductions for systolic BP.

Conclusion: The SFP module shows strong generalizability and plug-and-play transferability across different backbone architectures. These results underscore the great potential of SIFPBPNet for accurate cuffless BP monitoring, particularly for addressing individual variability in PPG-based blood pressure estimation.

Abstract: Continuous and cuffless blood pressure (BP) monitoring using photoplethysmography (PPG) is of great interest for low-cost and personalized cardiovascular health management. However, significant population heterogeneity and the "one-to-many mapping" problem, where similar waveforms across individuals correspond to different BP levels, limit the accuracy of conventional population-based models. To address this challenge, we propose a dual-path architecture termed SIFPBPNet, which separately represents steady-state and instantaneous features, through a Steady-state Feature Path (SFP) and an Instantaneous Feature Path (IFP). The SFP employs a Graph Attention Network (GAT) to extract individual-specific and long-term characteristics from multi-day historical PPG trajectories. In parallel, the IFP captures short-term dynamics from current PPG segments and incorporates the steady-state prior via a cross-attention mechanism. Experiments on a large-scale wearable dataset demonstrate that SIFPBPNet achieves a Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of 8.57 and 5.97 mmHg for systolic and diastolic BP, respectively, outperforming state-of-the-art models. Furthermore, the SFP module consistently improves performance when integrated into various backbone architectures, yielding 2.8-13.1% relative MAE reductions for systolic BP. These results highlight the strong generalizability and plug-and-play transferability of the SFP module, underscoring its great potential for accurate cuffless BP monitoring.

</details>


### [91] [Write on Paper and Get the Online Digital Trace:\newline A New Era for Handwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12702)
*Florent Imbert,Yann Soullard,Eric Anquetil,Tanja Harbaum,Alexey Serdyuk,Fabian Kress,Tim Hamann,Peter Kampf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A solution for digitizing handwriting on regular paper by combining a sensor-equipped digital pen with AI algorithms and embedded AI for real-time trajectory reconstruction, enabling seamless capture of handwritten content without specialized substrates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional handwriting on regular paper offers rich haptics, no latency, and improved information retention, but no low-cost, widely accepted solution exists to digitize such pen traces. The challenge is accurately tracking pen trajectory without external reference systems while allowing unrestricted pen movement.

Method: Integration of hardware development (sensor-equipped pen) with software innovations using advanced AI algorithms and adaptive AI techniques to reconstruct handwriting trajectory in real-time using embedded AI processing.

Result: The abstract does not report specific quantitative results; it presents the approach as a proposed solution aimed at advancing the state-of-the-art in automated trace reconstruction.

Conclusion: This work aims to enable seamless connection between traditional handwriting on paper and digital trace capture, advancing automated trace reconstruction capabilities for handwriting digitization.

Abstract: Capturing the digital trace of handwriting usually requires a specific stylus and a compatible substrate, be it a capacitive touchscreen, an ElectroMagnetic Resonance (EMR) tablet as used in Wacom systems or special paper. While writing on regular paper offers rich haptics, no latency and is well known for improving information retention, no low-cost and widely accepted, effective solution exists to digitize such a pen trace. The challenge is to accurately track the pen's trajectory without an external reference system while allowing unrestricted freedom of pen movement across a surface. We propose an innovative solution that combines a digital pen, advanced artificial intelligence algorithms, and adaptive AI techniques to reconstruct the digital trace of handwriting.
