Our approach integrates hardware development, focusing on a sensor-equipped pen, with software innovations to optimize trajectory reconstruction and processing in real time using an embedded AI. This work aims to advance the state-of-the-art in automated trace reconstruction of handwriting, enabling a seamless connection between traditional handwriting on paper and capturing the trace digitally.

</details>


### [92] [InRTL: Effective Intra-Inter Interaction Learning for Relational Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12712)
*Weichen Li,Ken Zhong,Zheng Wang,Li Pan,Jianhua Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: InRTL is a unified framework for relational table learning that models dependencies both within tables (intra-table) and across tables (inter-table) connected through PK-FK relationships. It uses a column-aware encoder, Transformer-based self-attention and cross-attention modules, with linearized attention and heterogeneous GNNs for scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational table learning is an important research direction for modeling multiple tables connected through PK-FK relationships, but a principled modeling framework tailored to this task remains underexplored.

Method: InRTL uses: (1) Column-aware table encoder for initial row representations; (2) Transformer-based self-attention for intra-table learning; (3) Cross-attention for inter-table learning; (4) Linearized attention and heterogeneous GNNs for scalability improvement.

Result: Extensive experiments on 10 datasets covering 24 real-world tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.

Conclusion: InRTL provides a unified framework that explicitly models dependencies both within and across relational tables, achieving effective performance across multiple real-world tasks.

Abstract: Relational table learning has recently emerged as an important research direction for modeling multiple tables connected through primary key-foreign key (PK-FK) relationships. Despite recent advances, a principled modeling framework tailored to this task remains underexplored. In this paper, we propose Intra-Inter Relational Table Learning (InRTL), a unified framework that explicitly models dependencies both within and across relational tables. Specifically, InRTL formalizes two complementary interaction patterns: intra-table interactions, describing associations among rows within the same table, and inter-table interactions, describing dependencies between rows across PK-FK-linked tables. To model these dependencies, we develop a column-aware table encoder to generate initial row representations, followed by Transformer-based self-attention and cross-attention modules for intra-table and inter-table learning, respectively. To further improve scalability, InRTL incorporates linearized attention and heterogeneous graph neural networks to simplify the self-attention and cross-attention operations. Extensive experiments on ten datasets covering 24 real-world tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. Code is available at https://github.com/W1nterFloW/InRTL.

</details>


### [93] [Physics-Guided Synthetic High-Frequency Ultrasound Generation for Skin Layer Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12735)
*Junkyung ju,Kyungho Yoon,Minwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a physics-guided synthetic HFUS (high-frequency ultrasound) generation framework to address the scarcity of densely annotated skin data for automated skin-layer segmentation. The framework uses k-Wave simulation on multilayer acoustic skin phantoms to generate paired synthetic images and dense layer masks. Experiments show that synthetic pretraining followed by real fine-tuning achieves performance comparable to real-only training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The lack of densely annotated HFUS data for deep skin structures (dermis, subcutaneous tissue, fascia, muscle) limits automated skin-layer analysis. Existing datasets mainly annotate superficial targets like epidermis and SLEB.

Method: The framework constructs multilayer acoustic skin phantoms with layer-dependent acoustic properties and uses k-Wave simulation to generate synthetic HFUS images with dense layer masks and metadata. The generated data is used for pretraining segmentation models, then fine-tuned on real Mendeley HFUS data.

Result: Synthetic pretraining with real fine-tuning achieved comparable performance to real-only training. Mean Dice/IoU improved in 3 out of 4 evaluated architectures.

Conclusion: Physics-guided synthetic HFUS images contain transferable anatomical and textural cues for real-domain skin layer segmentation. However, further reduction of the synthetic-real appearance gap is needed to achieve greater performance gains.

Abstract: High-frequency ultrasound (HFUS) enables noninvasive visualization of superficial skin structures, but automated skin-layer analysis is limited by the scarcity of densely annotated data. Existing real HFUS datasets commonly provide annotations for superficial targets such as the epidermis and subepidermal low-echogenic band (SLEB), while dense labels for deeper structures such as dermis, subcutaneous tissue, fascia, and muscle are rarely available. We propose a physics-guided synthetic HFUS generation framework for skin layer segmentation. The framework constructs multilayer acoustic skin phantoms, assigns layer dependent acoustic properties, and uses k-Wave simulation to generate paired synthetic HFUS images, dense layer masks, and simulation metadata. To evaluate whether the generated data provide transferable supervision, we use it for downstream segmentation pretraining and fine-tune the models on real Mendeley HFUS data. Synthetic pretraining followed by real fine-tuning achieved real-domain performance comparable to real-only training and improved mean Dice/IoU in three of four evaluated trainable architectures. These results suggest that physics-guided synthetic HFUS images contain transferable anatomical and textural cues for real-domain skin layer segmentation, although further reduction of the synthetic-real appearance gap is needed to enable greater gains. The code and data are available at: https://github.com/Finn-02/synthetic-hfus-skin-layer-segmentation.

</details>


### [94] [Optimizing for the decision not the prediction: an exploration of Smooth Net Benefit as a training objective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12752)
*Koen M. F. Gorgels,Lasai Barreñada,Maarten van Smeden,Ben Van Calster,Ewout W. Steyerberg,Wouter A. C. van Amsterdam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Smooth Net Benefit (σNB), a differentiable training objective designed to align prediction model training with threshold-specific clinical utility, replacing standard negative log-likelihood (NLL) training. Evaluation across Framingham cardiovascular risk data and 44 TabZilla datasets shows modest, context-dependent improvements primarily for logistic regression, with no benefit or slight degradation for more flexible models (GAMs, XGBoost).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard prediction models are trained using objectives like Bernoulli NLL, which optimize overall probabilistic accuracy but may not align with downstream clinical decisions that depend on specific risk thresholds. The motivation is to bridge this gap by introducing a training objective that directly optimizes clinical utility at decision-relevant thresholds.

Method: σNB was proposed as a differentiable approximation of Net Benefit (a decision-theoretic metric). The method was evaluated using three model classes: logistic regression, generalized additive models (GAMs), and XGBoost with three Hessian implementations. Experiments used the Framingham cardiovascular risk dataset and 44 TabZilla datasets comprising 72 dataset-threshold combinations.

Result: σNB training did not consistently improve Net Benefit in Framingham. Across TabZilla benchmark: (1) Logistic regression: mean standardized Net Benefit increased from 0.5669 (NLL) to 0.5765 (σNB), mean difference 0.0096 (95% CI -0.0001 to 0.0193); (2) GAMs: decreased from 0.5921 to 0.5625 (mean difference -0.0296); (3) XGBoost: NLL achieved 0.6745 vs 0.6723-0.6735 across σNB implementations. In logistic regression, σNB gains were positively associated with XGBoost's performance advantage over NLL-trained logistic regression.

Conclusion: σNB does not support being a general replacement for NLL training. Decision-focused optimization may be most useful when limited model flexibility leaves greater scope for improvement. Further investigation is warranted in settings where conventional likelihood-based training may not adequately capture decision-relevant structure.

Abstract: Objective Prediction models are commonly trained using objectives such as Bernoulli negative log-likelihood (NLL), although downstream clinical decisions may depend on specific risk thresholds. We introduce Smooth Net Benefit ($σ$NB), a differentiable approximation of Net Benefit designed to align model training with threshold-specific clinical utility.
  Materials and Methods We evaluated $σ$NB as a training objective for logistic regression, generalized additive models (GAMs), and XGBoost with three Hessian implementations. Experiments used the Framingham cardiovascular risk dataset and 44 TabZilla datasets comprising 72 dataset-threshold combinations.
  Results $σ$NB training did not consistently improve Net Benefit in Framingham. Across the TabZilla benchmark, mean standardized Net Benefit for logistic regression increased from 0.5669 with NLL to 0.5765 with $σ$NB (mean difference 0.0096, 95% CI -0.0001 to 0.0193). For GAMs, mean standardized Net Benefit decreased from 0.5921 to 0.5625 (mean difference -0.0296, 95% CI -0.0721 to 0.0129). For XGBoost, NLL achieved 0.6745 compared with 0.6723--0.6735 across $σ$NB implementations. In logistic regression, $σ$NB gains were positively associated with the performance advantage of XGBoost over NLL-trained logistic regression.
  Discussion The effect of $σ$NB was context dependent, with modest gains concentrated in logistic regression and little benefit for more flexible model classes. This suggests that decision-focused optimization may be most useful when limited model flexibility leaves greater scope for improvement.
  Conclusion Our results do not support $σ$NB as a general replacement for NLL training, but support further investigation of decision-focused objectives in settings where conventional likelihood-based training may not adequately capture decision-relevant structure.

</details>


### [95] [Can We Trust LLM Judges: A Study of Capability-Dependent Biases and Multi-Judge Ensemble for Bias Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12002)
*Gemma Zhang,Prachi Badarayani,Asmi Kumar,Sadid Hasan,Sulaiman Vesal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses systematic biases in LLM-as-a-judge systems for absolute scoring tasks. The authors show that a model's task accuracy strongly predicts its judging accuracy and inversely predicts its directional bias, but more capable examinee models receive more lenient judgments from all judges. They propose calibrated weighted majority voting (WMV), an ensemble method that uses a disagreement-based estimator to derive error rates without labeled data, achieving performance within 0.5 percentage points of an oracle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work has studied biases in pairwise LLM-as-a-judge settings, but less attention has been given to absolute scoring tasks, which mirror more realistic use cases. Individual judges exhibit systematic biases that undermine reliability, and there is a need for methods that can improve evaluation accuracy without requiring labeled data.

Method: The authors conducted experiments across four benchmarks and six models (36 judge-examinee pairs). They proposed calibrated weighted majority voting (WMV), which aggregates multiple LLM judges weighted by online estimates of their false-positive and false-negative rates. A key innovation is the disagreement-based estimator that derives error rates purely from inter-judge agreement patterns, requiring no ground-truth labels or task metadata.

Result: The study found that a model's task accuracy strongly predicts its judging accuracy (Pearson r ≥ 0.90 on most models) and inversely predicts its directional bias (r ≤ -0.83). However, more capable examinee models consistently receive more lenient judgments from all judges (r ≥ 0.83). In simulated experiments with shifting task distributions, the label-free WMV tracks an oracle with perfect error-rate knowledge to within 0.5 percentage points on average, outperforming both individual judges and unweighted majority voting.

Conclusion: Principled multi-judge calibration can simultaneously improve accuracy and correct for systematic leniency without requiring labeled data. This offers a scalable path to reliable automated evaluation as model capabilities increase.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used as automated judges for model training and evaluation, yet individual judges exhibit systematic biases that undermine reliability. Much of prior work has studied biases in pairwise LLM-as-a-judge settings; in this paper, we focus on absolute scoring tasks, which mirror more realistic use cases. Across four benchmarks and six models (36 judge-examinee pairs), we show that a model's task accuracy strongly predicts its judging accuracy (Pearson $r \geq 0.90$ on most models) and inversely predicts its directional bias ($r \leq -0.83$), but that accuracy alone does not ensure fair evaluation: more capable examinee models consistently receive more lenient judgments from all judges ($r \geq 0.83$). To address this, we propose calibrated weighted majority voting (WMV), an ensemble evaluation method that aggregates multiple LLM judges weighted by online estimates of their false-positive and false-negative rates. We introduce a disagreement-based estimator that derives these error rates purely from inter-judge agreement patterns, requiring no ground-truth labels or task metadata. In a simulated experiment with shifting task distributions, our label-free WMV tracks an oracle with perfect error-rate knowledge to within 0.5 percentage points on average, outperforming both individual judges and unweighted majority voting. These results demonstrate that principled multi-judge calibration can simultaneously improve accuracy and correct for systematic leniency without requiring labeled data, offering a scalable path to reliable automated evaluation as model capabilities increase.

</details>


### [96] [Curriculum-Based Adversarial Heterogeneous Agent Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Quad-Copter Landing in Maritime Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12758)
*Allan Minh-Tam Nguyen,Sree Showrya Kotala,Stefan Banioi-Crijman,Kurt Driessens,Rico Möckel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenging problem of recovering UAVs in maritime environments using a robotic arm mounted on a ship. The study proposes using Heterogeneous-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (HAPPO) with an adversarial wind agent (HARL-AC) for training robust cooperative control policies, demonstrating superior generalization to out-of-distribution sea states compared to domain randomization approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering UAVs in maritime environments is highly challenging due to wind turbulence and ship-deck motion, making conventional landing approaches unreliable. This necessitates alternative control and learning approaches for safe and reliable UAV recovery at sea.

Method: The study trains quadrotor UAV capture policies using HAPPO reinforcement learning in NVIDIA Isaac Lab. Two training approaches are compared: (1) HARL-AC with curriculum learning and an adversarial wind agent, and (2) curriculum-based domain randomization. A benchmark trained on a single sea state is also included for comparison.

Result: In-distribution evaluation on sea states 0/4/5 shows comparable success rates for HARL-AC and domain randomization, achieving up to 97.5% success. For out-of-distribution sea states 7/8/10, HARL-AC demonstrates superior generalization with up to 16% higher median success rate at sea state 10 and 14% lower crash rates. The adversarially trained policy exhibits more cautious behavior with slightly increased timeouts (<3%) but provides safer recovery in severe, unseen conditions.

Conclusion: The adversarial training approach (HARL-AC) achieves better generalization to out-of-distribution sea states while maintaining safer behavior, suggesting that incorporating adversarial wind agents in reinforcement learning training is effective for improving robustness in challenging maritime UAV recovery scenarios.

Abstract: Recovering unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) in maritime environments is challenging due to wind turbulence and ship-deck motion, making it a valuable test case for alternative control and learning approaches as conventional landing approaches often become unreliable. We study simulated mid-air capture of quadrotor UAVs by a ship-mounted robotic arm, learning robust cooperative control policies with Heterogeneous-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (HAPPO) Reinforcement Learning. We train with HAPPO using a curriculum and an adversarial wind agent (HARL-AC) in NVIDIA Isaac Lab, and compare the obtained control policies against those generated through curriculum-based domain randomization and a benchmark trained on a single sea state. In-distribution evaluation on sea states $0/4/5$ shows comparable success for HARL-AC and domain randomization of up to $97.5\%$. On out-of-distribution sea states $7/8/10$, HARL-AC generalizes better, achieving up to $16\%$ higher median success rate at sea state 10, and substantially lower crash rates of up to $14\%$ compared to the domain randomization policy. Furthermore, we show that the adversarially trained policy shows more cautious behavior, slightly increasing timeouts by $<3\%$, but yields safer recovery behavior in severe, unseen conditions.

</details>


### [97] [PLSP (Pre-hoc Liminal Space Profiling): OOD Prediction over Detection -- An Anticipatory Approach for Machine Learning Model Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12225)
*Vipul Bansal,Himanshu Buckchash,Balasubramanian Raman,Deepak Dhungana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes PLSP, a pre-hoc anticipatory framework for Out-of-Distribution (OOD) prediction, shifting from traditional post-hoc OOD detection methods. The key contribution is the CREDibility Score (CREDS), a dataset-independent metric for OOD prediction, along with credibility curves and credibility heat maps to characterize model behavior across distributional shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current OOD detection methods are post-hoc and rely on indirect metrics like accuracy and AUC-ROC during inference to assess model response to OOD data. This approach is limited because it only detects OOD data after the fact rather than predicting it beforehand. The authors argue for a paradigm shift from OOD detection to OOD prediction.

Method: The proposed PLSP framework introduces three main components: (1) CREDibility Score (CREDS) - a dataset-independent metric for OOD prediction; (2) credibility curves to study maximum credibility a model can attain; (3) credibility heat maps and volume under surface to characterize pre-hoc model behavior across different datasets.

Result: Experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate that the proposed CREDS metric serves as a valuable measure for improving robustness of machine learning models toward OOD prediction.

Conclusion: This work provides a novel perspective on signal processing under distributional shifts. The pre-hoc approach with CREDS metric offers a promising direction for building more robust ML models that can anticipate rather than merely detect OOD data.

Abstract: Out-of-Distribution (OOD) data poses a significant threat to machine learning models, often leading to model failure during deployment. All existing OOD detection methods are post-hoc, relying on evaluation metrics such as accuracy and AUC-ROC during inference to indirectly assess the model's response to OOD data by measuring deviations. In contrast to existing approaches, the proposed work shifts the paradigm from OOD detection to OOD prediction by proposing a pre-hoc anticipatory framework called PLSP for OOD prediction. We make several key contributions: (a) a dataset-independent metric called the CREDibility Score (CREDS) is proposed for OOD prediction; (b) credibility curves are introduced to study the maximum credibility a model can attain; and (c) credibility heat maps (and volume under surface) are introduced to characterize pre-hoc model behavior across different datasets. This work provides a novel perspective on signal processing under distributional shifts. Experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate that the proposed metric serves as a valuable measure for improving the robustness of machine learning models toward OOD prediction.

</details>


### [98] [Convergence of Stochastic Gradient Methods under Heavy-Tailed Noise and Hölder Smoothness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12785)
*Misbah Uz Zaman,Anirbit Mukherjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper establishes convergence guarantees for stochastic gradient methods under relaxed assumptions: Hölder continuous gradients (replacing Lipschitz smoothness) and bounded α-th moment gradient noise (generalizing finite variance). Three algorithms are analyzed: standard SGD, δ-regularized gradient clipping (δ-GClip), and standard gradient clipping (G-Clip), with different convergence rates derived for different regimes of α relative to 1+s.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical convergence guarantees for stochastic gradient methods require Lipschitz-smooth objectives and finite-variance gradient noise, but these assumptions are frequently violated in practice. This work aims to bridge the gap between theory and practice by studying nonconvex stochastic optimization under more realistic conditions.

Method: The analysis uses (L,s)-Hölder continuous gradients to characterize smoothness when s ∈ (0,1], and a bounded α-th moment condition (α ∈ (1,2]) to characterize heavy-tailed gradient noise. The paper analyzes three algorithms: standard SGD, δ-regularized gradient clipping (δ-GClip), and standard gradient clipping (G-Clip).

Result: Three convergence results are established: (1) SGD achieves O(T^{-s/(1+s)}) when α ≥ 1+s; (2) δ-GClip achieves O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(1+s)(2α-1)]}) under the same condition; (3) G-Clip recovers the first rate for α ≥ 1+s and achieves O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(α-1)+s(2α-1)]}) in the very heavy-tailed regime α < 1+s.

Conclusion: This work extends classical convergence results to heavy-tailed noise and Hölder smoothness simultaneously, providing the first convergence guarantee for any stochastic gradient-based method in the very heavy-tailed regime (α < 1+s).

Abstract: Classical convergence guarantees for stochastic gradient methods typically assume Lipschitz-smooth objectives and finite-variance gradient noise, both frequently violated in practice. In contrast, we study nonconvex stochastic optimization under the joint relaxation of these assumptions: objectives with $(L,s)$-Hölder continuous gradients, $s\in(0,1]$, and gradient noise satisfying only a bounded $α$-th moment condition for $α\in(1,2]$. We establish three convergence results. Firstly, that standard SGD converges at rate $O(T^{-s/(1+s)})$ whenever $α\ge1+s$, extending the classical nonconvex SGD rate to heavy-tailed noise and Hölder smoothness simultaneously. Secondly, we analyze $δ$-regularized gradient clipping ($δ$-GClip), a provable trainer of wide and deep nets, and establish a stationarity rate of $O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(1+s)(2α-1)]})$ under the same condition. Thirdly, we analyze standard gradient clipping (G-Clip) and show that it recovers the above rate for $α\ge1+s$ while in the very heavy-tailed regime $α<1+s$, it has a convergence rate $O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(α-1)+s(2α-1)]})$ --- the first convergence guarantee in this regime for any stochastic gradient based method.

</details>


### [99] [VertiFuseX: Generalizable Financial Forecasting via Multi-Stream Temporal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12793)
*Aashish Bohra,Vivek Vijay*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VertiFuseX is a hybrid LSTM architecture that combines multi-scale temporal representations (LSTM, Bi-LSTM, St-LSTM) through penultimate-layer vertical fusion, achieving 30-54% MAPE reduction and over 40% improvement in MAE/RMSE compared to LSTM baselines for stock price prediction on 10 global equity indices over 15 years.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stock price prediction is challenging due to non-stationary and noisy financial time series. Existing deep learning models suffer from rigid decision-level fusion, ad hoc hyperparameter tuning, compressed final-layer outputs causing information loss, overfitting, and limited cross-market generalization.

Method: VertiFuseX uses penultimate-layer vertical fusion of multi-scale temporal representations, stacking and reweighting penultimate features from LSTM, Bi-LSTM, and St-LSTM branches, integrating a parallel DNN stream, with joint optimization via backpropagation under fixed hyperparameter configuration.

Result: Achieves 30-54% MAPE reductions and over 40% improvements in MAE and RMSE versus LSTM-based baselines; outperforms seven state-of-the-art models across 33 metric-dataset comparisons; reduced maximum drawdowns and superior risk-adjusted returns in trading simulation; 675k parameters, 2.6 MB memory, 1.5 ms inference latency.

Conclusion: Penultimate-layer fusion drives superior performance over final-layer fusion and decision-level ensembling. VertiFuseX is a lightweight, interpretable, deployment-ready framework for robust financial forecasting with consistent emphasis on mid-range dependencies at lags 9-15 days.

Abstract: Stock price prediction remains challenging due to the non-stationary and noisy nature of financial time series. Existing deep learning models often rely on rigid decision-level fusion, ad hoc hyperparameter tuning, and compressed final-layer outputs, causing information loss, overfitting, and limited cross-market generalization. We propose VertiFuseX, a hybrid LSTM architecture using penultimate-layer vertical fusion of multi-scale temporal representations. VertiFuseX stacks and reweights penultimate features from LSTM, Bi-LSTM, and St-LSTM branches, integrates a parallel DNN stream, and jointly optimizes all components via backpropagation under a fixed hyperparameter configuration. This preserves richer intermediate temporal information across scales. Evaluated on 15 years (2010-2024) of closing prices from 10 global equity indices using strict chronological out-of-sample testing with the final 365 trading days held out, VertiFuseX achieves 30-54% MAPE reductions and over 40% improvements in MAE and RMSE versus LSTM-based baselines, and outperforms seven state-of-the-art models across 33 metric-dataset comparisons. Ablation studies confirm penultimate-layer fusion drives these gains over final-layer fusion and decision-level ensembling. Gradient-based saliency analysis shows consistent emphasis on mid-range dependencies at lags 9-15 days. Economic validation via algorithmic trading simulation under extreme market regimes shows reduced maximum drawdowns and superior risk-adjusted returns. With 675k parameters, a 2.6 MB memory footprint, and 1.5 ms/sample inference latency, VertiFuseX offers a lightweight, interpretable, deployment-ready framework for robust financial forecasting.

</details>


### [100] [GenOR-Twin: A Semantic Middleware for Integrating Operational Discourse with Mathematical Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12863)
*Rahimeh Neamatian Monemi,Shahin Gelareh,Lubin Cui,Nelson Maculan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GenOR-Twin is a neuro-symbolic framework that uses LLMs as semantic translators to convert unstructured operational logs into mathematical optimization constraints, featuring dynamic constraint injection, bidirectional Digital Twin coupling, and adaptive decision policies for real-time optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the translation gap between unstructured operational logs and rigorous mathematical optimization, enabling static optimization models to become resilient systems that adapt to real-world uncertainty and variability.

Method: The framework uses LLMs as semantic translators (not direct solvers) combined with: (1) a dynamic constraint injection mechanism for runtime translation of qualitative events into formal mathematical constraints, (2) bidirectional coupling between operational observations and Knowledge Graph, and (3) an adaptive decision policy that selects between low-complexity schedule repair and full re-optimization based on system slack analysis.

Result: The approach was demonstrated across six distinct optimization domains, showing generalization capability and ability to handle operational uncertainty and variability in real-world environments.

Conclusion: The framework successfully bridges neuro-symbolic AI with Digital Twin technology, turning static optimization models into resilient systems that can dynamically adapt to qualitative human inputs and real-time operational changes while maintaining feasibility guarantees of exact combinatorial methods.

Abstract: We introduce GenOR-Twin, a neuro-symbolic framework that bridges the translation gap between unstructured operational logs and rigorous mathematical optimization. Our architecture uniquely positions Large Language Models as semantic translators rather than direct solvers, ensuring that the system retains the feasibility guarantees of exact combinatorial methods. { \color{red}We design a dynamic constraint injection mechanism (the runtime translation of qualitative disruption events into formal mathematical constraints) that allows the system to structurally modify the optimization problem's feasibility region in real-time based on qualitative human inputs. The resulting bidirectional coupling---where operational observations update the virtual model state and optimized decisions are reflected back into the Knowledge Graph---satisfies the synchronization requirement of a proper Digital Twin. The framework features an adaptive decision policy} that automatically selects between low-complexity schedule repair and full re-optimization by analyzing the available system slack. Finally, we demonstrate the generalization of this approach across six distinct optimization domains, {\color{red}turning static models into resilient systems that adapt to the operational uncertainty and variability of real-world environments.}

</details>


### [101] [What an odour descriptor corpus can and cannot measure: valence, attenuation, and the ceiling of the public record](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12875)
*Stylianos Kampakis,Fabio Rovai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper audits four odor descriptor corpora from Pyrfume to test whether shared descriptor words measure the same thing across different datasets. It finds high heterogeneity (I²=80%) and that even the best machine learning models (using Morgan fingerprints + full descriptors) only achieve ~33% of human panel reliability. The critical missing factor is valence (pleasantness), which when directly measured reaches 54.6% of achievable reliability but can only be recovered at 16.6% from descriptors alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine olfaction relies on pooled public descriptor corpora, but it was unknown whether the same descriptor word measures equivalent concepts across different corpora, or what the performance ceiling of these corpora actually is.

Method: The authors audited four Pyrfume corpora using McNemar's test (conditional test of corpus effect), I² statistic for heterogeneity, tetrachoric agreement and Cohen's kappa, ETS differential functioning analysis, and compared ML model performance against human panel reliability using Morgan fingerprints with RDKit descriptors.

Result: Corpora show heterogeneous disagreement across descriptors (I²=80%) that increases with labeling breadth (z=17.2). Median tetrachoric agreement (0.795) substantially exceeds median κ (0.413), indicating sources agree on which molecules deserve a descriptor but disagree on how readily to apply it. 36 of 109 descriptors show large differential functioning. Best models reach only 32.9-33.9% of human reliability ceiling. Direct pleasantness ratings achieve 54.6-57.1% but valence inferred from descriptors yields just 16.6%.

Conclusion: Descriptor pooling cannot be repaired by simple offset corrections. The critical missing variance is valence (pleasantness), which must be measured directly rather than inferred from descriptors. Five raters exceed structure plus full descriptor records; 15-20 raters saturate reliability. The authors release a descriptor crosswalk and 20 machine-checked theorems.

Abstract: Machine olfaction trains on pooled public descriptor corpora, but whether a shared descriptor word measures the same thing across corpora has not been tested, nor has the ceiling of what any of them can measure.
  We audit four corpora from Pyrfume. Conditioning on the molecule makes McNemar's test the exact conditional test of the corpus effect. Corpora disagree heterogeneously across descriptors ($I^2 = 80\%$) and non-uniformly with labelling breadth ($z = 17.2$), so no single offset repairs pooling. Median tetrachoric agreement is 0.795 against median $κ$ of 0.413: sources largely concur on which molecules deserve a word and differ on how readily they apply it. Of 109 descriptors with an estimable effect, 36 show large differential functioning on the ETS scale.
  Against a human panel's reliability, Morgan fingerprints with the full RDKit descriptor block reach 32.9\% of achievable; adding every label from two merged corpora reaches 33.9\%. The gap does not close with model capacity, encoding choice, more molecules, or more words.
  The missing variance is valence. One pleasantness rating per molecule reaches 54.6\% of achievable (57.1\% on an independent older instrument). Valence recovered from descriptors ($ρ= 0.457$) yields only 16.6\%, so it must be measured. Five raters exceed structure plus the full descriptor record; fifteen to twenty saturate. We release a descriptor crosswalk and twenty machine-checked theorems.

</details>


### [102] [Large Distant Gradients Need Not Be Reliable: reliability-weighted credit assignment for long-horizon autoregressive forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12890)
*Junhao Zhao,David Michael Simberg,Jacob Kang,Colin Connor Kurniawan,Nan Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Internal Dual-Wiener (Internal-DW) is a backward-only intervention for autoregressive forecasting that reliability-weights internal gradient routes to address the problem where distant gradients can dominate updates while amplifying both predictable signal and unpredictable noise. It achieves 5.2%-13.8% forecast error reduction across four testbeds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In autoregressive forecasting, long prediction rollouts provide distant supervision, but backpropagation through time (BPTT) carries gradients through many autoregressive steps. Repeated Jacobian products can make distant gradients dominate while amplifying both predictable signal and unpredictable noise together - meaning large distant gradients don't necessarily carry reliable learning signal.

Method: Internal-DW is a principled backward-only intervention that preserves the full forward rollout and all horizon losses while reliability-weighting internal gradient routes. At each residual block, bounded Wiener gains are derived for identity and nonlinear routes that balance preserving predictable learning signal against suppressing unpredictable variation. These gains are estimated from route-level gradient statistics and an explicit noise model.

Result: In a controlled system with known gradient SNR, distant gradients can grow even as their SNR falls. Internal-DW reduces held-out error in recovering predictable gradient signals and improves forecasting. On four history-dominated, weak-drive testbeds, Internal-DW reduces forecast error by 5.2%-13.8% relative to full BPTT, outperforms gradient clipping and Jacobian regularization on all four, and outperforms validation-selected truncated BPTT (TBPTT) on three. It also extends or preserves the fitted optimal training-horizon range. The method has an applicability boundary: benefit diminishes when usable history is limited or when the selected sampler fails to represent dominant drive-dependent variation.

Conclusion: Retaining long-horizon supervision does not require trusting every backward contribution equally. The Internal-DW approach provides a principled way to reliability-weight gradient routes, though its benefit diminishes when usable history is limited or when the selected sampler fails to represent dominant drive-dependent variation.

Abstract: In autoregressive forecasting, long prediction rollouts provide distant supervision, but backpropagation through time (BPTT) carries gradients from those losses through many autoregressive steps. Repeated Jacobian products can make distant gradients dominate the update while amplifying predictable signal and unpredictable noise together; a large distant gradient therefore need not carry reliable learning signal. Motivated by this observation, we introduce Internal Dual-Wiener routing (Internal-DW), a principled backward-only intervention that preserves the full forward rollout and all horizon losses while reliability-weighting internal gradient routes. At each residual block, we derive bounded Wiener gains for the identity and nonlinear routes that balance preserving predictable learning signal against suppressing unpredictable variation, and estimate them from route-level gradient statistics and an explicit noise model. In a controlled system with known gradient signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), we show that distant gradients can grow even as their SNR falls, and that Internal-DW reduces held-out error in recovering predictable gradient signals and improves forecasting. On four history-dominated, weak-drive testbeds, Internal-DW reduces forecast error by 5.2%-13.8% relative to full BPTT, outperforms gradient clipping and Jacobian regularization on all four, and outperforms validation-selected truncated BPTT (TBPTT) on three. It also extends or preserves the fitted optimal training-horizon range across these four testbeds. Across the full benchmark suite, the current Internal-DW estimator has a clear applicability boundary: its benefit diminishes or reverses when usable history is limited or when the selected sampler fails to represent dominant drive-dependent variation. The results show that retaining long-horizon supervision does not require trusting every backward contribution equally.

</details>


### [103] [Quantifying the Value of Privileged Information Using a PAC-Bayesian Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12891)
*Vasily Bokov,Sebastian Schmitt,Vedran Dunjko,Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces an algorithm-agnostic, information-theoretic framework based on PAC-Bayes theory to quantify how much value privileged information (PI) can provide in Learning Using Privileged Information (LUPI) scenarios. The authors propose a training-time metric that measures the potential gain from PI without requiring test-time data access, validated in both supervised and unsupervised settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LUPI theory offers per-algorithm performance guarantees but lacks a general framework explaining how and when PI transfers useful knowledge. The authors aim to bridge this gap by providing an algorithm-agnostic approach to measure the potential extractable gain from privileged features before committing to a model.

Method: The authors develop an information-theoretic approach using the PAC-Bayes framework. They compare the tightest achievable risk bound with and without PI to quantify the potential value of PI. A training-time metric is introduced that can directly measure this potential from empirical training risk, bypassing the need for test-time data access.

Result: The proposed training-time metric shows robust correspondence with true test-time performance gains in both supervised and unsupervised settings. This validates the framework's effectiveness in quantifying the potential of privileged features.

Conclusion: This work takes a necessary step toward an information-theoretic understanding of LUPI, providing a practical tool for practitioners to quantify the potential of privileged features before model selection. The algorithm-agnostic nature of the approach makes it widely applicable across different learning scenarios.

Abstract: In practice, various learning scenarios provide access to auxiliary features exclusively during training. Incorporating such data to enhance model performance gave rise to a paradigm known as Learning Using Privileged Information (LUPI). While this extra information is intended to improve the resulting model, establishing a generalized, cohesive understanding of how privileged information (PI) transfers useful knowledge remains a challenge. Vapnik's original theory and subsequent works offer performance guarantees in certain cases, but these results are inherently per-algorithm and rely on setting-specific proof approaches. Consequently, a more general framework explaining how and when PI transfers useful knowledge is still missing. To bridge this gap, we introduce an algorithm-agnostic, information-theoretic approach based on the PAC-Bayes framework. Rather than asking whether a particular algorithm exploits PI, we ask how much value it could offer: comparing the tightest achievable risk bound with and without PI yields its potential - an upper limit on the extractable gain. We introduce a metric that quantifies this potential directly from empirical training risk, bypassing the need for test-time data access, and validate our findings in both supervised and unsupervised settings. The results demonstrate a robust correspondence between our training-time metric and true test-time performance gains. Ultimately, this work takes a necessary step toward an information-theoretic understanding of LUPI, and quantifying the potential of privileged features before committing to a model.

</details>


### [104] [Behavior Quotient Learning for Low-Rank Adaptation of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12896)
*Pengyang Zhou,Xiaobin Tu,Zhengxi Liu,Rongkun Xue,Haochen Li,Miancan Liu,Ziyuan Chen,Yinggui Wang,Jinkui Ren,Xiantao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BQ-LoRA is a low-rank adaptation framework for LLM-based agents that addresses challenges of learning from diverse trajectories under fixed rank budget by using behavior quotient balancing (BQB) to filter redundant updates and decision preserving compression (DPC) to maintain decision quality while staying within rank constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches distribute agent capabilities across multiple LoRA adapters, causing storage overhead and routing complexity. A single LoRA avoids this but faces two key challenges: (1) diverse trajectories with different interaction traces cause redundant behavioral changes due to equivalent decision distribution changes, and (2) aggregated updates may exceed the adapter's rank budget, leading to weight space approximation that distorts decision changes.

Method: BQ-LoRA consists of two complementary modules: (1) BQB constructs a local behavior quotient manifold from decision distributions and reweights trajectory update directions according to their local density in the quotient tangent space, filtering redundant updates. (2) DPC projects the balanced gradient onto an intrinsic fixed rank tangent space and refactorizes by jointly controlling effective weight error and decision distribution distortion.

Result: Experiments on AppWorld and BrowseComp-Plus compare BQ-LoRA with standard LoRA and recent low-rank adaptation methods, demonstrating improvements in agent task completion.

Conclusion: BQ-LoRA successfully enables a single LoRA adapter to learn from diverse agent trajectories while maintaining decision quality within fixed rank budget constraints, with both BQB and DPC components providing complementary contributions.

Abstract: LLM-based agents rely on heterogeneous interaction capabilities to accomplish complex tasks. Existing approaches often distribute these capabilities across multiple LoRA adapters, which increases adapter storage requirements and introduces routing overhead during inference. A single LoRA avoids this overhead, but learning from diverse agent trajectories under a fixed rank budget presents two challenges. First, trajectories with different interaction traces and parameter gradients can induce equivalent changes in decision distributions, causing repeated updates to overemphasize redundant behavioral changes. Second, an aggregated update may exceed the rank budget of the adapter, and approximating it in weight space can distort the decision changes that it is intended to produce. We propose BQ-LoRA, a low-rank adaptation framework that organizes trajectory updates through a local behavior quotient manifold. It contains two modules, i.e., behavior quotient balancing (BQB) and decision preserving compression (DPC). BQB constructs the quotient manifold from decision distributions and reweights trajectory update directions according to their local density in the quotient tangent space. DPC projects the balanced gradient onto the intrinsic fixed rank tangent space and refactorizes the resulting target by jointly controlling effective weight error and distortion of decision distributions. Experiments on AppWorld and BrowseComp-Plus compare BQ-LoRA with standard LoRA and recent low-rank adaptation methods, while separate ablations evaluate the complementary contributions of both components.

</details>


### [105] [Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling for Full-Waveform Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12899)
*Chen Min,Haowen Jiang,Zheng Ma,Xiongbin Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PSG (Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling) is a new method for full waveform inversion (FWI) that couples a persistent physical velocity to a diffusion prior through a Gaussian bridge. It refines the physical state by waveform fitting regularized by denoised velocity, which then guides the reverse diffusion process. This approach separates wave-equation and denoiser gradients while preserving conventional FWI initialization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full waveform inversion (FWI) is ill-posed and nonlinear, making accurate subsurface velocity reconstruction strongly dependent on initialization and prior information. While diffusion posterior sampling provides a learned geological prior, directly coupling its denoiser to the nonlinear wave solver can yield unreliable physical guidance. A method was needed that could leverage diffusion-based priors while maintaining reliable physical constraints.

Method: PSG couples a persistent physical velocity to the diffusion prior through a Gaussian bridge. The physical state is refined by waveform fitting regularized by the denoised velocity, and in turn guides the reverse diffusion process. This formulation separates the wave-equation and denoiser gradients while preserving conventional FWI initialization and accumulated optimization history.

Result: On four OpenFWI families, PSG's terminal denoised estimates outperform classical and diffusion-based baselines under clean and missing-trace acquisitions and maintain strong structural recovery under measurement noise. Repeated stochastic runs preserve the dominant geological structures, with ensemble variability concentrated near geological interfaces and positively associated with local inversion error. A frozen OpenFWI-trained prior further supports inversion of the larger Marmousi, Overthrust, and BP2004 Salt models, recovering complex geological structures without retraining.

Conclusion: PSG successfully integrates physical constraints with diffusion-based priors for FWI, achieving superior performance over classical and diffusion-based baselines across various acquisition conditions. The method demonstrates robust generalization to complex geological structures (Marmousi, Overthrust, BP2004 Salt) without requiring retraining, making it a promising approach for practical seismic inversion problems.

Abstract: Full waveform inversion (FWI) estimates subsurface velocity from seismic recordings, but its ill-posedness and nonlinearity make accurate reconstruction strongly dependent on initialization and prior information. Diffusion posterior sampling provides a learned geological prior, yet directly coupling its denoiser to the nonlinear wave solver can yield unreliable physical guidance. We propose Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling (PSG), which couples a persistent physical velocity to the diffusion prior through a Gaussian bridge. The physical state is refined by waveform fitting regularized by the denoised velocity, and in turn guides the reverse diffusion process. This formulation separates the wave-equation and denoiser gradients while preserving conventional FWI initialization and accumulated optimization history. On four OpenFWI families, PSG's terminal denoised estimates outperform classical and diffusion-based baselines under clean and missing-trace acquisitions and maintain strong structural recovery under measurement noise. Repeated stochastic runs preserve the dominant geological structures, with ensemble variability concentrated near geological interfaces and positively associated with local inversion error. A frozen OpenFWI-trained prior further supports inversion of the larger Marmousi, Overthrust, and BP2004 Salt models, recovering complex geological structures without retraining.

</details>


### [106] [Hidden in Rounds: Predicting the Time Cost of 802.11 Contention in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12903)
*Satwat Bashir,Tasos Dagiuklas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper evaluates federated learning over IEEE 802.11 wireless networks using ns-3 simulations, measuring frame-delivery ratio and saturation throughput to estimate communication time. Across 720 runs with varying configurations, all runs reached target accuracy, with communication time increasing significantly across client density ranges, though the study has notable limitations in not measuring actual end-to-end training time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study addresses the challenge of implementing federated learning over shared wireless channels (IEEE 802.11), where clients compete for channel access when sending model updates. The motivation is to understand how wireless channel characteristics affect federated learning performance.

Method: The authors use ns-3 network simulator to measure frame-delivery ratio and saturation throughput for different client densities and offered loads. A FedAvg trainer uses the frame-delivery ratio as a proxy for update-admission probability and employs an equation to estimate communication time. The method does NOT simulate complete model update delivery or measure actual end-to-end training time.

Result: All 720 runs achieved target accuracy within the round budget. Rounds-to-target showed little variation with offered load, while communication time-to-target increased by about two orders of magnitude across the client-density range. A Bianchi-anchored estimator produced mean absolute percentage error of 2.3% to 10.2%. No statistically distinguishable accuracy gap was detected between excluded classes, though confidence intervals were wide.

Conclusion: The study provides empirical results for federated learning over IEEE 802.11 under specific configurations, but has significant limitations: results apply only to evaluated configurations with no general convergence or fairness guarantees, and the error metrics were measured against the same equation used for estimation rather than independently measured completion times. The method serves as a first-order approximation but does not capture full end-to-end training dynamics.

Abstract: Federated learning over IEEE~802.11 shares the wireless channel among clients that send model updates. We use ns-3 to measure the frame-delivery ratio and saturation throughput for different client densities and offered loads. A separate FedAvg trainer uses the frame-delivery ratio as a first-order proxy for the update-admission probability and uses an equation to estimate communication time. The method does not simulate the delivery of a complete model update or measure end-to-end training time. Across 720 evaluated runs with two datasets, two data partitions, six client densities, six offered loads, and five seeds, all runs reached their predefined target accuracy within the round budget. Rounds-to-target changed little with offered load, while communication time-to-target increased by about two orders of magnitude across the client-density range. A Bianchi-anchored estimator produced a mean absolute percentage error from $2.3\%$ to $10.2\%$ on held-out configurations. This error is measured against communication time constructed from the same round-duration equation, not against independently measured completion time. We also compare uniform participation with persistent heterogeneous participation. The study does not detect a statistically distinguishable excluded-class accuracy gap over five seeds, but the confidence intervals are wide. The results apply only to the evaluated configurations and do not provide a general convergence or fairness guarantee.

</details>


### [107] [Offline Reinforcement Learning for Wind Farm Control: A Wind Tunnel Study under Dynamic Wind Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12905)
*Yuhan Su,Hongyang Dong,Simone Tamaro,Filippo Campagnolo,Carlo L. Bottasso,Xiaowei Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes MTD3-BC, an offline reinforcement learning algorithm for wind farm power maximization through yaw control under varying wind direction conditions. The method requires no wake model and only a small offline dataset, achieving ~10% power gains over greedy baseline while being experimentally validated in a wind tunnel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wind farm power output is significantly reduced by wake effects, where upstream turbines create wakes that decrease downstream turbine efficiency. Traditional wake steering methods rely on physics-based wake models that are computationally expensive and often inaccurate under dynamic conditions. Online RL methods require extensive simulator interactions, making them costly. This work aims to develop a computationally efficient, model-free approach that can be trained entirely from offline data without requiring a wake model or online interactions.

Method: The proposed MTD3-BC algorithm combines Modified Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (MTD3) with Behavior Cloning (BC) for offline reinforcement learning. A new action consistency term is introduced into the policy optimization objective to ensure smooth and moderate yaw adjustments. The algorithm is trained on a pre-collected offline dataset without requiring interactions with a wind farm simulator during training. A wind tunnel experiment was conducted to validate the algorithm under varying wind directions.

Result: MTD3-BC successfully mitigates wake effects and achieves approximately 10% farm-level power gains over the baseline greedy strategy. The performance is on par with a data-calibrated model-based wake-steering benchmark. The method requires no wake model and only a small fraction of the training cost compared to online RL approaches. This is the first experimental validation of an offline RL wind farm control policy.

Conclusion: Offline RL (MTD3-BC) is a viable and efficient approach for wind farm yaw control optimization. The method achieves significant power improvements without requiring physics-based wake models or expensive online training. The experimental validation in wind tunnel demonstrates practical applicability. This work represents the first experimental demonstration of offline RL for wind farm control, opening new avenues for efficient wake steering in real-world applications.

Abstract: This paper addresses the wind farm power maximization problem in the presence of wind direction changes. Specifically, a model-free Modified Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient with Behavior Cloning (MTD3-BC) algorithm is proposed to tackle this task through yaw control under varying wind direction conditions. MTD3-BC is an offline reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm that aims to infer good behavior from only a precollected offline dataset. Additionally, to ensure smooth and moderate yaw adjustments, a new action consistency term is introduced into the policy optimization objective. Unlike online RL methods, MTD3-BC does not require extensive interactions with a wind farm simulator during training, significantly reducing computational costs and training time. A wind tunnel experiment is conducted to validate the effectiveness of the algorithm under varying wind directions. The results demonstrate that MTD3-BC successfully mitigates wake effects, delivering farm-level power gains of approximately 10\% over the baseline greedy strategy and performance on par with a data-calibrated model-based wake-steering benchmark, while requiring no wake model and only a small fraction of the training cost of online RL. To our knowledge, this is the first time an offline RL wind farm control policy has been validated and demonstrated experimentally.

</details>


### [108] [Reinforcement Learning over Patient Trajectories for Clinical Reasoning in EHR Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12277)
*Yuxin Xiao,Sheng Zhang,Chandan Singh,Tristan Naumann,Hoifung Poon,Jianfeng Gao,Xiaodong Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning framework for Electronic Health Record (EHR) foundation models, treating them as generative policies over patient trajectories to improve clinical reasoning capabilities beyond traditional next-token prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current EHR foundation models, while showing strong performance across clinical prediction tasks, are limited by their next-token prediction approach on incomplete and limited EHR data, constraining their clinical reasoning capabilities.

Method: The researchers formulate clinical prediction problems (e.g., hospital readmission) as event-conditioned, time-windowed reasoning tasks. They design time-aware, rollout-sensitive rewards that account for finite rollout lengths and temporally inconclusive outcomes. This RL framework treats EHR models as generative policies over patient trajectories.

Result: RL fine-tuning consistently improves over pre-trained backbones and strong baselines. It enables smaller models to surpass larger pre-trained models in data-limited regimes and induces positive transfer across tasks. The fine-tuned models generate trajectories with stronger structural and semantic alignment to ground truth and greater downstream utility.

Conclusion: Reinforcement learning fine-tuning is an effective approach for improving EHR foundation models, offering better data efficiency, cross-task transfer learning, and trajectory generation quality compared to traditional pre-training methods.

Abstract: Electronic health record (EHR) foundation models trained on longitudinal patient trajectories have demonstrated strong performance across diverse clinical prediction tasks. However, their clinical reasoning capabilities remain constrained by next-token prediction on limited and incomplete EHR data. To address this, we propose a reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning framework that treats EHR foundation models as generative policies over patient trajectories. We formulate common clinical prediction problems (e.g., hospital readmission) as event-conditioned, time-windowed reasoning tasks. We then design time-aware, rollout-sensitive rewards to account for finite rollout lengths and temporally inconclusive outcomes. We find that RL fine-tuning consistently improves over pre-trained backbones and strong baselines. Notably, it enables smaller models to surpass larger pre-trained models in data-limited regimes and induces positive transfer across tasks. Further analysis shows that RL fine-tuned models generate trajectories with stronger structural and semantic alignment to ground truth and greater downstream utility.

</details>


### [109] [A Large-Scale AIS Dataset from Finnish Water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12938)
*Debayan Bhattacharya,Ikram Ul Haq,Carlos Pichardo Vicencio,Sebastien Lafond*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: This research paper contributes to the maritime research community by introducing a comprehensive AIS dataset from Finnish waters, specifically the Baltic Sea region. AIS data, initially designed for collision prevention, have evolved into a versatile tool with applications across diverse maritime domains. Our paper not only curates and categorises existing AIS datasets but also introduces a collected AIS dataset from the Baltic Sea area, renowned for its intercontinental cargo routes, military activities, and frozen water expanses. This dataset includes 229 millions data points and provides researchers with a resource for studying maritime activities and vessel behaviour in this dynamic region, notably distinguished by the inclusion of data from Finnish lakes. Our analysis includes a detailed listing of ship types and relevant features, empowering researchers to explore various maritime domains. To enhance comprehension and analysis, we provide visualisations of maritime traffic patterns. By publishing this AIS dataset, we aim to catalyse innovation and collaboration in maritime research, offering a gateway to deeper insights into maritime activities in the Baltic Sea and Finnish lakes.

</details>


### [110] [Information-Induced Training Geometry: Exact Reduction, Canonical Completion, and Structured Expressivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12991)
*Zavier Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses how partial information from an information channel determines complete positive-definite geometry in affine-invariant Riemannian spaces, with applications to optimization and compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to understand how limited information (visible through an information channel) constrains and determines the full geometric structure of positive-definite matrices, specifically addressing what degrees of freedom remain unidentified when only partial information is available.

Method: The work employs affine-invariant Riemannian geometry, split-Hadamard metric subymmetry, and conic optimization techniques to analyze the positive-definite cone under rank stratification and reference-dependent completions.

Result: The central result establishes full-column-rank positive-definite compression with explicit unique completion. The completion forms a gauge-invariant rank stratification, with a closed-form pullback pair metric that separates visible-metric motion from subspace rotation. The metric theorem equates ball submetry, fiber distance, and lossless reduction of monotone radial visible decision problems.

Conclusion: The results characterize exact reduction, reference-dependent completion, and structured expressivity for finite-dimensional affine-invariant models, providing closed-form prior-data shrinkage and deterministic/finite-sample bounds for recovery of visible geometry.

Abstract: Training data constrains optimizer geometry through the covectors visible to a declared information channel. We study how such partial information determines a full positive cometric relative to a reference and which degrees of freedom remain unidentified. Our central result resolves full-column-rank positive-definite compression under affine-invariant Riemannian geometry. The compression map is a split-Hadamard metric submetry and admits an explicit unique completion that is the affine-invariant nearest full geometry realizing a visible target and yields exact full-to-visible variational reduction. When the channel moves, the completions form a gauge-invariant rank stratification of the positive-definite cone. Its closed-form pullback pair metric separates visible-metric motion from subspace rotation through a reference-mismatch weight, yields an explicit positive-semidefinite multi-direction Gram matrix, and exposes the precise singularity of reference-valued modes. The mechanism is explained by a metric theorem equating ball submetry, attained fiber distance, and lossless reduction of every monotone radial visible decision problem. A smooth split-Hadamard theorem supplies coherent information sheets, proximal commutation, and solution-wise gradient-flow lifting. The positive-definite realization also gives closed-form prior-data shrinkage. Diagonal and block optimizer families reduce to relative-interior conic image tests with valid facial certificates, while deterministic and finite-sample bounds quantify recovery of the visible geometry and its subspace. Together these results characterize exact reduction, reference-dependent completion, and structured expressivity for the stated finite-dimensional affine-invariant model.

</details>


### [111] [Dimension-Corrected Hitting Times for Heavy-Tailed Spectral Emergence in Neural Optimizer Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12994)
*Zongmin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the timing (step complexity) of heavy-tail emergence in neural network weight matrices, formulating it as a right-censored hitting-time problem. The authors discover a dimension-corrected spectral-gap law where onset time depends on both the initial spike-bulk gap and network dimension, with theoretical support from projected singular-basis spreading analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heavy-tailed empirical spectral densities of weight matrices are used as diagnostics of implicit self-regularization in neural networks, but the step complexity (when heavy tails emerge) remains poorly understood. Existing understanding fails to explain onset time from first-step spectral properties alone.

Method: The authors formulate spectral heavy-tail formation as a right-censored hitting-time problem (treating incomplete runs as censored rather than discarded). They use controlled full-batch teacher-student dynamics, fit a dimension-corrected spectral-gap law (τ_HT ≈ CΔ_1^(-γ)d^ρ), and apply right-censored lognormal accelerated-failure-time (AFT) models for comparison. They also provide theoretical analysis including: exact early loss dynamics in linear networks, Adam recurrence analysis, and projected singular-basis spreading with spectral-tail potential contraction.

Result: The dimension-corrected spectral-gap law achieves R²=0.683 with γ=0.626 and ρ=0.772 across 330 completed runs. The AFT model comparison strongly favors the dimension-corrected model (AIC: 628.70 vs 706.62). Theoretically: (1) exact early loss dynamics in linear networks do not determine factor spectral tails; (2) Adam recurrences alone do not imply spectral redistribution; (3) projected singular-basis spreading implies contraction of spectral-tail potential, yielding a dimension-corrected hitting-time bound. Empirically: projected-kernel profiles support the spreading mechanism; Adam and AdamW agree; GD and signGD differ in regime coverage; real pretrained models (Qwen2.5-0.5B, Pythia-70M) show non-Gaussian spectral tails.

Conclusion: The result establishes a reproducible spectral hitting-time law with rigorous conditional theory—heavy-tail onset depends on both initial spectral gap and network dimension. This is not a claim that Adam necessarily generates heavy tails from first principles, but rather a reproducible empirical law with theoretical grounding in projected singular-basis spreading dynamics.

Abstract: Heavy-tailed empirical spectral densities of neural-network weight matrices are widely used as diagnostics of implicit self-regularization, but the step complexity of heavy-tail emergence remains poorly understood. We formulate spectral heavy-tail formation as a right-censored hitting-time problem: a run that does not reach a heavy-tail diagnostic within the observation horizon is treated as censored rather than discarded. In controlled full-batch teacher--student dynamics, we find that the first-step spike--bulk gap alone does not explain onset time. Instead, finite-onset regression supports a dimension-corrected spectral-gap law, (τ_{\mathrm{HT}}\approx CΔ_1^{-γ}d^ρ), with (R^2=0.683), (γ=0.626), and (ρ=0.772) across 330 completed runs. Right-censored lognormal accelerated-failure-time models further favor the dimension-corrected model over a gap-only model, improving AIC from 706.62 to 628.70. Theoretically, we prove that exact early loss dynamics in linear networks do not determine factor spectral tails, that Adam recurrences alone do not imply spectral redistribution, and that projected singular-basis spreading implies contraction of a spectral-tail potential and hence a dimension-corrected hitting-time bound. Empirically, projected-kernel profiles support the sufficient spreading mechanism, Adam and AdamW agree under tested grids, GD and signGD do not reach onset in the same regimes, and real pretrained Qwen2.5-0.5B and Pythia-70M transformer weights show non-Gaussian spectral-tail structure relative to matched Gaussian nulls. The result is a reproducible spectral hitting-time law with rigorous conditional theory, not a claim that Adam necessarily generates heavy tails from first principles.

</details>


### [112] [A Full Adam Theorem for Spectral Heavy-Tail Onset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12996)
*Zongmin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves a full Adam theorem for spectral heavy-tail onset in a closed Gaussian Stein-Hermite teacher-student state-evolution model. The theorem establishes the hitting time law τ_ε = Θ(Δ_1^{-γ}d^ρ log(Ψ_0/ε)) where Δ_1 is the first spike-bulk spectral gap. The result is comprehensive, formalizing each step from Adam's momentum and denominator to the spectral hitting law within the closed state-evolution framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation appears to be establishing a theoretical foundation for understanding Adam optimizer's behavior in deep learning, specifically focusing on spectral heavy-tail onset phenomena. The paper aims to provide rigorous mathematical guarantees for Adam's convergence properties in Gaussian teacher-student settings.

Method: The methodology involves: deriving full-batch Adam recurrences, using Stein-Hermite calculus for population gradients, proving finite-width covariance concentration, converting multi-step Adam momentum into exact non-centered Gaussian sign kernels, controlling diagonal Adam denominator via basis-homogenization theorem, deriving regularly varying projected update responses from Hermite edge-transfer theorem, pushing responses through exact Gram updates, and proving approximate-target KL contraction with matching upper and lower hitting bounds.

Result: The main result is the hitting time law τ_ε = Θ(Δ_1^{-γ}d^ρ log(Ψ_0/ε)), fully formalized within the closed state-evolution model. Additional results show that stronger arbitrary-gradient Adam theorems are impossible, and exact two-step linear-network loss dynamics cannot identify factor spectra or heavy-tail hitting times.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a comprehensive Adam theorem is achievable within the closed Gaussian Stein-Hermite teacher-student state-evolution framework, but with limitations: stronger arbitrary-gradient theorems are impossible, and linear-network dynamics cannot fully characterize spectral properties.

Abstract: We prove a full Adam theorem for spectral heavy-tail onset in a closed Gaussian Stein-Hermite teacher-student state-evolution model. The theorem begins with the actual full-batch Adam recurrences, derives the population gradient by Stein-Hermite calculus, proves finite-width covariance concentration, converts multi-step Adam momentum into an exact non-centered Gaussian sign kernel, controls the diagonal Adam denominator by a basis-homogenization theorem, derives a regularly varying projected update response from a Hermite edge-transfer theorem, pushes the response through the exact Gram update, and proves approximate-target KL contraction with matching upper and lower hitting bounds. The final law is (τ_\varepsilon=Θ(Δ_1^{-γ}d^ρ\log(Ψ_0/\varepsilon))), where (Δ_1) is the first spike-bulk spectral gap. The result is full in the following precise sense: every step from Adam's momentum and denominator to the spectral hitting law is formalized inside the closed state-evolution model. We also prove that a stronger arbitrary-gradient Adam theorem is impossible, and that exact two-step linear-network loss dynamics do not identify factor spectra or heavy-tail hitting times.

</details>


### [113] [Dual-guided Hierarchical Edge Localization for Large-scale Optimal Transport Across Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13010)
*Wenzhou Xia,Qiaoqiao Ding,Jingwei Liang,Xiaoqun Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HELLO is a hierarchical solver for large-scale discrete optimal transport that uses dual potentials to guide coarse-to-fine initialization and refinement. It achieves order-of-magnitude runtime improvements over baselines while scaling to 1.28 million samples in 8192 dimensions on a single H100 GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unregularized discrete optimal transport requires solving a linear program with quadratically many transport variables, making it computationally prohibitive for large-scale problems. Existing methods struggle with scalability across varying feature dimensions.

Method: The method casts large-scale discrete OT as edge localization using dual potentials. It employs: (1) Coarse-to-fine initialization via recursive subsampling hierarchy that propagates coarse dual potentials to assign candidate edges; (2) Iterative refinement that inserts the largest dual violators in each row/column until relative KKT residual meets tolerance; (3) Budgeted pruning for linear memory complexity. For exact-arithmetic refinement, finite termination at global optimum is proved under a symbolic lexicographic rule.

Result: At million-point scale, HELLO achieves lower transport objectives with order-of-magnitude runtime improvements over strong baselines across feature dimensions from single digits to thousands. It scales to 1.28 million samples per marginal in 8192 dimensions on a single H100, using 41.6 GiB peak GPU memory while satisfying full relative KKT residual below 10^-6.

Conclusion: The framework supports general pairwise costs and serves as a scalable balanced-OT oracle for semi-discrete OT, Gromov-Wasserstein, unbalanced OT, and OT-based Flow Matching, enabling practical large-scale optimal transport applications.

Abstract: Optimal transport (OT) compares distributions and aligns datasets in machine learning, yet unregularized discrete OT requires a linear program with quadratically many transport variables. We propose HELLO, a hierarchical solver that casts large-scale discrete OT as edge localization and uses dual potentials to guide both coarse-to-fine initialization and within-level refinement. Initialization propagates coarse dual potentials across a recursive subsampling hierarchy to assign candidate edges. Refinement then iteratively inserts the largest dual violators in each row and column until the relative KKT residual meets a prescribed tolerance, while budgeted pruning ensures linear memory complexity. For exact-arithmetic refinement, we prove finite termination at a global optimum under a symbolic lexicographic rule. At the million-point scale, HELLO attains lower transport objectives with order-of-magnitude runtime improvements over strong baselines across feature dimensions from single digits to thousands. It further scales to 1.28 million samples per marginal in 8192 dimensions on a single H100, using 41.6 GiB peak GPU memory while satisfying a full relative KKT residual below $10^{-6}$. Beyond standard discrete OT, the framework supports general pairwise costs and serves as a scalable balanced-OT oracle for semi-discrete OT, Gromov--Wasserstein, unbalanced OT, and OT-based Flow Matching.

</details>


### [114] [Attention Quantization for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13031)
*Jonas M. Kübler,Benjamin Jäger,Klemens Flöge,Noah Hollmann,Frank Hutter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses inference efficiency for tabular foundation models by developing an FP8 quantization strategy for QKV matrices in attention calculations, achieving up to 1.7x speedup without accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular foundation models are architecturally similar to transformer-based LLMs but differ in size and serving patterns. The authors argue that optimizing attention calculation is more critical than weight or KV cache quantization (common in LLMs) for these models.

Method: The authors developed a quantization strategy for queries, keys, and values to FP8 precision, using explicit FP8 matrix multiplication instructions. A key insight is that quantization error must be aligned between test rows and training rows to maintain accuracy.

Result: The Triton kernel achieves up to 1.7x speedup over regular 16-bit kernels. On TabPFN-v3 and TabICLv2 models, there is no relevant accuracy loss across TabArena and BeyondArena benchmarks.

Conclusion: FP8 quantization for attention calculations is an effective optimization for tabular foundation models, providing significant speedup while preserving accuracy when quantization error alignment is properly handled.

Abstract: With the recent rise and adoption of tabular foundation models, optimizing their inference performance becomes an emerging field for efficiency research. While the models are architecturally similar to transformer-based large language models (LLMs), the size and serving patterns differ significantly. We show that the focus should be on the attention calculation and less on weight or KV cache quantization, which are more popular in LLMs. We develop a quantization strategy for queries, keys, and values to FP8 and use explicit FP8 matrix multiplication instructions to speed up the attention calculation.
