We find that it is crucial to align the quantization error in the test rows with the quantization error in the training rows, as otherwise the accuracy drops drastically. Our Triton kernel achieves a speedup up to 1.7x over regular 16-bit kernels, and we show that on TabPFN-v3 and TabICLv2 there is no relevant accuracy loss across TabArena and BeyondArena.

</details>


### [115] [Groupoid-Based Internal State Representations for Reinforcement Learning with Local Symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13035)
*Ben Opperman,Eduardo Alonso,Esther Mondragón*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a groupoid-based reinforcement learning framework that captures local, state-dependent symmetries to improve sample efficiency and convergence in environments with partial symmetries. The approach uses orbit representatives and transporters to map states to canonical forms, enabling learning in a symmetry-reduced space while preserving important distinctions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical RL assumes globally structured MDPs with uniformly applicable actions and transitions, which limits the ability to exploit modularity and local context-dependent regularities present in realistic environments. Existing approaches rely on fixed group actions or predefined state abstractions, restricting their adaptability.

Method: The proposed framework uses groupoids to capture local, state-dependent symmetries. The agent maintains orbit representatives together with transporters that map raw states to canonical forms, enabling learning and decision-making in a symmetry-reduced space while preserving local distinctions.

Result: Empirical results demonstrate improved sample efficiency and convergence in dense and large-scale environments exhibiting strong partial symmetries, yielding substantial performance gains over standard Q-learning.

Conclusion: Dynamically exploiting local symmetry provides a practical and mathematically principled route to scalable and generalizable reinforcement learning, with the groupoid framework offering a principled way to discover and utilize equivalence structures during interaction.

Abstract: Symmetries play a central role in reducing the complexity of reinforcement learning problems, yet most existing approaches rely on fixed group actions or predefined state abstractions. Classical reinforcement learning algorithms typically assume a globally structured Markov decision process with uniformly applicable actions and transitions, an assumption that limits their ability to exploit modularity and local, context-dependent regularities present in many realistic environments. We propose a reinforcement learning framework using groupoids to capture local, state-dependent symmetries and support the dy- namic discovery of equivalence structures during interaction. The agent maintains orbit representatives together with transporters that map raw states to canonical forms, enabling learning and decision-making to be performed in a symmetry-reduced space while preserving local distinctions. Empirical results demonstrate that the proposed groupoid-based approach improves sample efficiency and convergence in dense and large-scale environments exhibiting strong partial symmetries, yielding substantial performance gains over standard Q-learning. These findings show that dynamically exploiting local symmetry provides a practical and mathematically principled route to scalable and generalisable reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [116] [Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13039)
*Ricardo Ribeiro Pereira,Jacopo Bono,Hugo Ferreira,Pedro Ribeiro,Pedro Saleiro,Pedro Bizarro,Carlos Soares*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a new framework called Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains (TrED) that formalizes transfer learning as a continuous trajectory rather than isolated static problems. The key insight is that real-world data availability evolves over time, but current transfer learning research treats different settings (domain generalization, adaptation, multi-domain learning) as separate problems. TrED unifies these by treating them as phases in a trajectory that a learning system must navigate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current transfer learning research is fragmented into isolated sub-areas (domain generalization, domain adaptation, multi-domain learning), each making different assumptions about target data availability. However, in real-world applications, data availability is not fixed but evolves over time as instances and labels are progressively collected. Each classical setting describes only a snapshot of a trajectory that a deployed system must traverse in full.

Method: The authors formalize TrED with three components: (1) a data availability process fixed by the environment, (2) a learning protocol that the method is free to choose, and (3) an evaluation criterion that scores the whole trajectory of models rather than a single one. They examine existing transfer learning literature to identify promising mechanisms and evaluate whether current methods can handle this unified framework.

Result: Most existing transfer learning methods are tailored to a single regime (e.g., only domain adaptation or only domain generalization). Even the strongest existing candidates do not optimize the whole trajectory—they are designed for specific snapshots rather than the entire evolution of data availability.

Conclusion: TrED is a well-posed and unsolved problem. It represents an important direction for future research because it better reflects real-world scenarios where data accumulates progressively. The classical transfer learning settings are recovered as regimes that a learner may pass through, rather than as separate problems that TrED concatenates.

Abstract: Transfer learning explores how to leverage knowledge from various tasks or domains (sources) to enhance predictive performance in related tasks or domains (targets). Typically, transfer learning research is segmented into several isolated sub-areas (such as domain generalisation, domain adaptation, or multi-domain learning), each making distinct assumptions about target data availability, namely how much data and how many labels are available at training time. However, in many real-world applications, data availability is not fixed but evolves over time, as instances and labels are progressively collected from a new domain. Each of the classical settings then describes only a snapshot of a trajectory that a deployed system must traverse in full. We formalise this trajectory as a transfer learning problem in its own right, Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains (TrED), specified by a data availability process fixed by the environment, a learning protocol that the method is free to choose, and an evaluation criterion that scores the whole trajectory of models rather than a single one. Within this formalism, the classical settings are recovered as regimes that a learner may pass through, rather than as separate problems that TrED concatenates. We then examine the transfer learning literature to identify mechanisms that are promising building blocks for a solution, and find that most methods are tailored to a single regime and that even the strongest existing candidates do not yet optimise the whole trajectory. We argue that TrED is a well-posed and unsolved problem, and an important direction for future research.

</details>


### [117] [Quantile-based Loss Filtering for Outlier-Robust Stochastic Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13040)
*Jamie Haddock,Anna Ma,Elizaveta Rebrova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Quantile-k-Loss SGD (Q(k)L-SGD), a loss-based filtering method for finite-sum optimization with corrupted component functions. It samples k component losses at each iteration and updates using an index from the lower empirical q-quantile, achieving linear convergence under standard convexity assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to handle finite-sum optimization problems where a subset of component functions have highly unreliable gradients (corrupted components). Existing methods like min-k-loss SGD and quantile-based methods for corrupted linear systems inspire this approach, but they have limitations - min-k-loss can stall by repeatedly selecting nearly solved components.

Method: Q(k)L-SGD samples k component losses at each iteration and selects an index uniformly from the lower empirical q-quantile of these losses for the update. The method has two analysis frameworks: (1) Large-sample analysis requiring sample size to scale with number of corruptions and a subset strong-convexity threshold; (2) Small-sample probabilistic analysis covering any sample size k.

Result: The paper proves linear convergence under standard convexity assumptions. Experiments on polynomial regression, regularized logistic regression, and regularized hinge loss show that intermediate quantiles outperform both standard SGD and min-k-loss SGD. Min-k often stalls by repeatedly selecting nearly solved components, while intermediate quantiles retain robustness and produce more informative updates.

Conclusion: The proposed Q(k)L-SGD family provides a robust approach for handling corrupted components in finite-sum optimization. The key insight is that using intermediate quantiles (rather than the minimum or standard sampling) provides better trade-off between robustness and informativeness, avoiding the stalling behavior of min-k-loss while maintaining advantages over standard SGD.

Abstract: We study loss-based filtering for finite-sum optimization with a subset of corrupted component functions whose gradients may be highly unreliable. Motivated by minimum-loss-based SGD (min-$k$-loss) and quantile-based methods for corrupted linear systems, we propose and analyze a general loss-filtering framework -- Quantile-\(k\)-Loss SGD (Q\(k\)L-SGD) -- that samples \(k\) component losses at each iteration and updates using an index chosen uniformly from the lower empirical \(q\)-quantile. We prove linear convergence of this family of methods under standard convexity assumptions, requiring the sample size to scale with the number of corruptions and a subset strong-convexity threshold. For the cases when large enough sampling is impossible or undesirable, we give a complementary small-sample probabilistic analysis that covers any sample size $k$ and the convergence behavior depends on the probability of selecting an outlier and on the curvature of the selected good step. Experiments on polynomial regression, regularized logistic regression, and regularized hinge loss show that intermediate quantiles often outperform both standard SGD and min-\(k\)-loss SGD. In particular, min-\(k\) often stalls by repeatedly selecting nearly solved components, while intermediate quantiles retain robustness and produce more informative updates.

</details>


### [118] [DynSHAP: Towards Explainable Dynamic Survival Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13042)
*Nastasya Anokhina,Jonas Jürß,Pietro Liò*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DynSHAP is a SHAP-based explainability framework designed for dynamic survival analysis (DSA) that handles longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs. It treats time-feature pairs as players in the Shapley game and includes Temporal DynSHAP which addresses temporal dependencies through conditional sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models for DSA achieve strong predictive performance but their black box nature limits clinical trust and adoption. Existing explainability methods cannot handle longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs simultaneously.

Method: Extended common marginal SHAP estimators to DSA by treating time-feature pairs as players in the Shapley game. Temporal DynSHAP learns linear dependencies in features over time and uses conditional sampling to address these dependencies in explanations.

Result: On synthetic data with known ground-truth attributions, Temporal DynSHAP recovers temporally dependent features more accurately than marginal estimators. On two real-world clinical datasets and two DSA architectures, DynSHAP produces attributions faithful to model learning.

Conclusion: DynSHAP allows medical experts to see which patient information drove the prediction and when, enabling clinical trust and adoption of DSA models.

Abstract: Deep learning models for dynamic survival analysis (DSA) achieve strong predictive performance by incorporating longitudinal patient data, but their black box nature limits clinical trust and adoption. Existing explainability methods cannot handle longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs simultaneously, which limits their usability in DSA. We propose DynSHAP, a SHAP framework suited specifically for dynamic survival analysis. It extends common marginal SHAP estimators to this setting by treating time--feature pairs as players in the Shapley game. We further introduce Temporal DynSHAP, which learns linear dependencies in features over time and uses conditional sampling to address them in explanations. When applied to synthetic data with known ground-truth attributions, Temporal DynSHAP recovers temporally dependent features more accurately than marginal estimators for a given state-of-the-art model. Applied to two real-world clinical datasets and two DSA architectures, DynSHAP produces attributions faithful to model learning, allowing medical experts to see which patient information drove the prediction and when.

</details>


### [119] [Robust Policy Optimization via Adversarial Importance Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13044)
*Amine Andam,Jamal Bentahar,Mustapha Hedabou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses robustness in Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) against input perturbations across three stages: algorithm design, implementation, and evaluation. The authors propose (1) Adversarial Importance Sampling (Advis), a method using importance sampling to estimate worst-case returns without additional environment interactions or auxiliary networks; (2) advrl, a PyTorch library providing modular implementations of robustness methods; and (3) a comprehensive evaluation approach using 6-14x more attacker configurations than prior work, demonstrating that optimal adversarial hyperparameters do not transfer across agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for safeguarding DRL policies against input perturbations have limitations: they either require additional environment interactions, need auxiliary networks, or fail to capture long-term robustness. Additionally, there lacks standardized tools for implementation and evaluation, and prior evaluation methods may overestimate robustness due to limited attacker configurations.

Method: The authors propose three contributions: (1) Adversarial Importance Sampling (Advis) - uses importance sampling over trajectories from standard training to estimate and optimize verifiable worst-case returns; (2) advrl - a modular PyTorch library providing clean, single-file implementations of robustness methods and adversarial attacks; (3) A comprehensive evaluation approach using 6-14x more attacker configurations than prior work across continuous control environments.

Result: Advis satisfies three desirable criteria jointly: no additional environment interactions, no auxiliary networks, and captures long-term robustness. The authors demonstrate that optimal adversarial hyperparameters do not transfer across agents, which can lead to overestimation of robustness when using limited attacker configurations. Their approach shows effectiveness relative to existing baselines on continuous control environments.

Conclusion: The proposed Advis method, together with the advrl library and comprehensive evaluation framework, provides a more complete and reliable approach to developing robust DRL policies. The findings highlight the importance of diverse evaluation configurations and transferable robustness criteria.

Abstract: Significant progress has been made in safeguarding deep reinforcement learning (DRL) policies against input perturbations. Developing robust DRL involves three main stages: algorithm design, implementation, and evaluation. In this work, we identify and address a key limitation at each stage. First, we introduce Adversarial Importance Sampling (Advis), a method that uses importance sampling over trajectories from standard training to estimate and optimize verifiable worst-case returns. Advis satisfies three desirable criteria not jointly achieved by prior work: it requires no additional environment interactions, no auxiliary networks, and captures long-term robustness. Second, we introduce advrl, a modular PyTorch library that provides clean, single-file implementations of existing robustness methods and adversarial attacks, facilitating rapid prototyping and enabling reproducible and traceable evaluations. Third, we revisit evaluation under learned adversaries and show that optimal adversarial hyperparameters do not transfer across agents, which can lead to an overestimation of robustness when using a limited set of attacker configurations. Accordingly, we evaluate policies against a large and diverse set of attackers, using 6-14x more configurations than prior work. Finally, we evaluate our approach on continuous control environments, demonstrating its effectiveness relative to existing baselines. The code is available at: https://github.com/AmineAndam04/advrl

</details>


### [120] [MCRL2: Multi-resource Cross-attention-based Representation Learning-augmented Reinforcement Learning for Cloud Microservice Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13048)
*Tiangang Li,Shi Ying,Xiangbo Tian,Chuan Shi,Ding Xiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MCRL2 is a novel reinforcement learning approach for microservice scheduling that uses multi-resource cross-attention-based representation learning to capture complex interdependencies among heterogeneous resources. It augments actor-critic architecture with maximum entropy objective to improve system state expressiveness and scheduling decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient microservice scheduling is crucial for load balance and QoS in data centers, but faces challenges from dynamic resource imbalance, nonlinear coupling across resource dimensions, and heterogeneous microservice demands. Existing RL approaches fail to capture complex interdependencies and neglect informative system representations.

Method: 1) Propose MCRL, a representation learning approach using multi-resource cross-attention to capture interactions among nodes, resources, and microservices. 2) Augment RL with MCRL-enhanced actor-critic architecture combined with maximum entropy objective. 3) Evaluate on real production cluster traces.

Result: MCRL2 significantly outperforms existing baselines in load balancing, scheduling success rate, and average completion time across diverse workload patterns.

Conclusion: Multi-resource cross-attention-based representation learning effectively enhances reinforcement learning for microservice scheduling, achieving better performance through improved state representation and stable scheduling decisions.

Abstract: Efficient microservice scheduling is crucial for maintaining load balance across nodes in data centers and ensuring high quality of service. However, achieving this in practice remains challenging due to dynamic resource imbalance under fluctuating workloads, nonlinear coupling across multiple resource dimensions, and the heterogeneity of microservice resource demands. While reinforcement learning-based approaches have shown promise, they struggle to capture the complex interdependencies among heterogeneous resources and neglect the importance of learning informative system representations. To address these limitations, we propose MCRL2, a novel reinforcement learning approach augmented with multi-resource cross-attention-based representation learning for microservice scheduling. Specifically, we first propose MCRL, a novel representation learning approach that captures structured and informative interactions among nodes, resources, and microservices via a multi-resource cross-attention mechanism. Then, MCRL2 augments reinforcement learning through MCRL-enhanced actor-critic architecture combined with a maximum entropy objective, improving system state expressiveness and leading to more stable and effective scheduling decisions. Extensive experiments on real production cluster traces demonstrate that MCRL2 significantly outperforms existing baselines in load balancing, scheduling success rate and average completion time across diverse workload patterns.

</details>


### [121] [A Unified and Constrained View of Regularization-Based Robust Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13050)
*Amine Andam,Jamal Bentahar,Mustapha Hedabou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Regularization-based methods have become a standard approach for training Deep Reinforcement Learning policies against adversarial input perturbations. In this paper, we unify these methods by deriving new upper bounds on the performance gap between the nominal and worst-case policies. Each upper bound is expressed as an existing regularization objective plus a KL-divergence penalty between the nominal and worst-case policies, which further explains why adding a KL penalty improves robustness in practice. Building on these bounds, we formulate robust training as a constrained optimization problem, showing that existing methods correspond to the special case of a fixed Lagrange multiplier. We instead update the multiplier jointly with the policy to automatically tune the regularization weight. Finally, we conduct extensive adversarial evaluations across several continuous control tasks to validate our theoretical analysis.

</details>


### [122] [Benign Loss Landscapes Can Coexist with Worst-Case Hardness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13057)
*Zach Furman,Stephan Wäldchen,Yangda Bei,Liam Hodgkinson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates why deep neural networks learn well in practice despite theoretical hardness results, using tree tensor networks (TTNs) as a model class. It shows TTNs can contain worst-case targets that cannot be learned by gradient descent in polynomial time, but surprisingly, bad local minima are not the source of difficulty. Instead, learning difficulty arises from high-order degenerate saddle points caused by rank-deficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand what non-generic structure of real-world targets enables efficient learning in neural networks, when theoretical results suggest they should be hard to learn. Existing surrogate models (deep linear networks, kernel methods) cannot properly pose this question because they either lack hard-to-learn targets or cannot evaluate them efficiently.

Method: Theoretical analysis of tree tensor networks (TTNs) - a model class generalizing deep linear networks and Tucker decompositions. The authors analyze the loss landscape geometry and prove properties about local minima and saddle points.

Result: TTNs can embed arbitrary read-once Boolean formulas, containing polynomial-size targets that gradient descent cannot learn in polynomial time. However, their loss landscapes are conditionally benign: every local minimum that is minimum-norm is global. Bad local minima are not what distinguishes typical from worst-case problems. Instead, difficulty arises from high-order degenerate saddle points caused by rank-deficiency. The parity function is used as a case study.

Conclusion: The source of computational hardness in TTNs (and potentially neural networks) is not bad local minima but rather high-order degenerate saddle points stemming from rank-deficiency. This provides a new perspective on the relationship between landscape geometry and computational hardness in deep learning.

Abstract: Deep neural networks are expressive enough to contain worst-case targets that can be evaluated in polynomial time but cannot be learned in polynomial time by gradient descent. For practical tasks they nonetheless learn well, raising the question of what non-generic structure of real-world targets enables this. Existing surrogate models cannot pose this question because they either lack hard-to-learn targets entirely (deep linear networks) or cannot evaluate such targets efficiently (kernel methods, infinite-width limits). We study tree tensor networks (TTNs), a model class that generalizes deep linear networks and Tucker decompositions. We show they embed arbitrary read-once Boolean formulas, and thus contain polynomial-size targets that cannot be learned by gradient descent in polynomial time under the same mechanism as neural networks. Despite this, we prove that their loss landscapes are conditionally benign for every realizable target: every local minimum that is minimum-norm is global. Thus, surprisingly, bad local minima are not what distinguishes between typical and worst-case problems in TTNs. Instead, learning difficulty in TTNs can arise from high-order degenerate saddle points, which we show are caused by rank-deficiency. This is explored through a case study of the parity function, illustrating the potential for TTNs to relate landscape geometry to computational hardness.

</details>


### [123] [CanvasAnneal: Curriculum Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13060)
*Blake Olson,Yuhang Song,Emmett McQuinn,Yuan Shangguan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CanvasAnneal is a curriculum-guided diffusion RL framework that addresses exploration bottlenecks in Diffusion Language Models by injecting teacher-generated reasoning traces initially, then gradually removing guidance to encourage independent reasoning. It improves over diffu-GRPO on mathematical reasoning and tool-use benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) offer parallel generation capabilities but lag behind autoregressive models in complex reasoning and tool-use tasks. Standard RL approaches suffer from an exploration bottleneck when applied to DLMs.

Method: CanvasAnneal uses a curriculum-guided approach: (1) Initial RL phase - warm-start exploration by injecting teacher-generated reasoning traces into the initial diffusion canvas; (2) Gradually remove guidance as training progresses, requiring the model to generate more of the reasoning trajectory independently.

Result: CanvasAnneal improves over standard diffu-GRPO on MATH500, Countdown, and Tau2 benchmarks, and substantially accelerates reward improvement on several tasks, though gains are task-dependent.

Conclusion: Structured training-time guidance can alleviate exploration bottlenecks in diffusion RL and speed up convergence on harder tasks.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) offer promising parallel generation capabilities but lag behind autoregressive models in complex reasoning and tool-use tasks. While Reinforcement Learning (RL) has recently been applied to enhance DLMs, standard RL approaches suffer from an exploration bottleneck. To address this, we inject reasoning priors from a stronger teacher model to guide RL exploration. In this paper, we introduce CanvasAnneal, a curriculum-guided diffusion RL framework. During the initial RL phase, we warm-start exploration by injecting teacher-generated reasoning traces into the initial diffusion canvas. As training progresses, we gradually remove this guidance and require the model to generate more of the reasoning trajectory independently. Across mathematical reasoning and tool-use benchmarks, CanvasAnneal improves over standard diffu-GRPO on MATH500, Countdown, and Tau2 and substantially accelerates reward improvement on several tasks, while gains are task-dependent. Our results suggest that structured training-time guidance can alleviate exploration bottlenecks in diffusion RL and speed up convergence on harder tasks.

</details>


### [124] [MAxBench: A Multinomial Concept Recovery Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13072)
*Divya Appapogu,Freya Behrens,Yonatan Belinkov,Aaron Mueller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces MAxBench, a geometry-agnostic evaluation framework for evaluating multinomial concept representations in language models. It compares 10 localization methods across 5 geometry types on 6 concepts and 4 models, finding that affine subspaces outperform other geometries mainly due to better offsets, and that no method consistently beats prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Binary concepts (like refusal) can be steered with a single direction, but multinomial concepts (like Animals, Countries) have many subcategories with much larger search spaces. It is unclear what geometries are most appropriate or effective for recovering these representations.

Method: The authors introduce MAxBench, a geometry-agnostic evaluation framework based on sampling from recovered concept representations. They compare 10 localization methods covering 5 geometry types across 6 concepts and 4 models.

Result: Key findings: (i) Affine subspaces steer more reliably and have greater recall than rank-one or linear subspaces; (ii) The advantage comes mainly from better non-zero offsets rather than basis choice; (iii) Manifold steering is competitive with the best methods when applicable; (iv) No method consistently outperforms prompting.

Conclusion: These findings underscore the importance of expanding interpretability research and meta-evaluation to concepts with more varied structure beyond binary concepts.

Abstract: Fine-grained control of language model behaviors (e.g., steering) is among the more actionable outcomes of interpretability research. For binary concepts such as refusal, a single direction in activation space often suffices for steering. However, many concepts are not binary: Animals and Countries contain many subcategories, each with multiple instances. For these concepts, the search space over possible representation geometries is far larger than for binary concepts; it is thus not clear what geometries are most appropriate, nor what methods are most effective at recovering them. In this work, we introduce MAxBench, a geometry-agnostic evaluation framework for multinomial concept representations based on sampling from the recovered concept representation. We use MAxBench to compare 10 localization methods (covering 5 geometry types) across 6 concepts and 4 models. Using this framework, we find that (i) affine subspaces steer more reliably and have greater recall than rank-one or linear subspaces; (ii) much of this advantage is due to better non-zero offsets rather than the choice of bases; (iii) manifold steering is competitive with the best methods when applicable; and (iv) no method consistently outperforms prompting, in alignment with prior findings on binary concepts. These findings underscore the importance of expanding the scope of interpretability research and meta-evaluation to concepts with more varied structure.

</details>


### [125] [RunningTensor: Generalizing Linear Attention to Higher-Order Recurrent States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12814)
*Luca Herranz-Celotti,Vincent Guigue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces RunningTensor, which generalizes the recurrent memory of linear attention and state-space models from a matrix (second-order) to a higher-order tensor (order-o), enabling greater working memory capacity while maintaining linear time complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear attention and state-space models are limited by their matrix-valued state (second-order tensor), which restricts the order of interactions that can be represented. This motivates the need for a higher-order recurrent state that can capture more complex relationships.

Method: The RunningTensor generalizes memory to an order-o tensor, updated via rank-1 outer product and read by contracting against o-1 vector queries. Order 3 is studied as a proof of concept, retaining both recurrent and parallel forms while maintaining linear complexity in sequence length T.

Result: RunningTensor improves working memory capacity from O(W²) to O(W^o). On synthetic multi-query associative recall, it outperforms linear-attention and SSM baselines. After pretraining, it also improves performance on language-understanding and non-synthetic retrieval tasks.

Conclusion: Higher-order recurrent state can provide useful additional memory capacity beyond matrix-valued state, suggesting a promising direction for sequence modeling.

Abstract: Linear attention and state-space models provide linear-time sequence modeling, but their recurrent memory remains a second-order tensor (a matrix), limiting the order of interactions that can be represented in the state. We introduce the RunningTensor, which generalizes this memory to an order-$o$ tensor, updated by a rank-1 outer product and read by contracting against $o-1$ vector queries. Order $2$ recovers linear attention; we study order $3$ as a proof of concept, retaining both recurrent and parallel forms while remaining linear in sequence length $T$ and improving working memory capacity from $\mathcal{O}(W^2)$ to $\mathcal{O}(W^o)$. On synthetic multi-query associative recall, RunningTensor outperforms linear-attention and SSM baselines. After pretraining, it also improves performance on language-understanding and non-synthetic retrieval tasks, suggesting that higher-order recurrent state can provide useful additional memory capacity beyond matrix-valued state.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

### [126] [Grid-Free Monte Carlo for Time-Dependent Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12306)
*Zihong Zhou,Rohan Sawhney,Eugene d'Eon,Wojciech Jarosz*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper presents a generalization of grid-free Monte Carlo methods (walk on spheres and walk on stars) to solve transient heat equations with time-dependent boundary conditions, initial conditions, and source terms, without requiring volumetric meshing or sequential time stepping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional transient PDE solvers face two major bottlenecks: (1) costly volumetric meshing for complex geometries, and (2) sequential time marching with step size selection constraints. Existing grid-free Monte Carlo methods like WoS and WoSt are largely limited to steady-state problems, creating a need for methods that can handle time-dependent diffusive systems efficiently.

Method: The authors generalize WoS and WoSt by equipping random walks with a finite time budget and sampling exit times at each spatial step. When exit time exceeds the remaining budget, the walk terminates by sampling an interior point and evaluating the initial condition. Otherwise, the walk continues with reduced budget, accumulating source and boundary contributions. Key technical contributions include low-bias tabulation-free exit time samplers and efficient rejection samplers for variance reduction.

Result: The method eliminates time step selection and temporal discretization bias entirely while retaining the parallel, progressive, and output-sensitive properties of WoS/WoSt. It enables direct estimation of solutions at any requested time without volumetric meshing or sequential time marching. Sharing walks across multiple target times allows efficient estimation at multiple time points.

Conclusion: This work successfully extends grid-free Monte Carlo solvers from steady-state to transient PDEs, providing a novel approach that avoids the traditional bottlenecks of meshing and time stepping while maintaining parallelism and output sensitivity.

Abstract: Many scientific applications require modeling how diffusive systems evolve over time, not merely their eventual steady states. While conventional steady-state analysis of partial differential equations (PDEs) on complex geometries is already hindered by costly volumetric meshing, transient analysis further requires sequential time stepping and careful step size selection. Grid-free Monte Carlo solvers such as walk on spheres (WoS) and walk on stars (WoSt) avoid this meshing bottleneck but remain largely limited to steady-state problems. We generalize WoS, for pure Dirichlet problems, and WoSt, for mixed Dirichlet--Neumann problems, to heat equations with initial conditions and time-dependent source and boundary data. We equip each random walk with a finite time budget and sample an exit time at every spatial step. If the exit time exceeds the remaining budget, the walk samples an interior point and evaluates the initial condition; otherwise, it continues with a reduced budget, accumulating source and boundary contributions. Our main technical contribution is a suite of kernel sampling and variance reduction techniques, including a low-bias, tabulation-free exit time sampler and efficient rejection samplers. Unlike grid-based transient solvers, our method directly estimates the solution at any requested time without volumetric meshing or sequential time marching. It also retains the parallel, progressive, and output-sensitive evaluation of WoS and WoSt while eliminating time step selection and temporal discretization bias entirely. Finally, we show how sharing walks enables efficient estimates at multiple target times.

</details>


### [127] [SNAP3D: Physically Grounded 3D Parts for Assembly from a Single Image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13146)
*Yu-Rou Tuan,Hao-Tang Tsui,Nicolas Ugrinovic,Kris Kitani,Xiaoxuan Ma*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: The paper presents a physics-guided framework to improve part-aware 3D asset generation from single images, addressing physical compatibility issues like interpenetration, invalid connections, and gravity-induced instability through parameterized connectors and simulation-based refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing part-aware 3D generation methods can create visually complete individual parts but fail to ensure they form valid physical assemblies, leading to interpenetration, invalid connections, and collapse under gravity.

Method: The framework resolves inter-part penetration, recovers contact graphs between neighboring parts, introduces parameterized connectors at contact surfaces, and uses physical simulation feedback to refine connector placement, orientation, and dimensions while preserving generated geometry.

Result: Substantial improvements in physical realizability and stability while maintaining geometric quality, validated through 3D printing and real-world assembly.

Conclusion: The physics-based evaluation protocol effectively complements conventional geometric metrics by directly testing assembly validity and stability under gravity, enabling practical applications in editing, articulation, simulation, and fabrication.

Abstract: Part-aware 3D asset generation enables applications such as editing, articulation, simulation, and fabrication, yet existing methods can generate visually complete individual parts without ensuring that they form a valid physical assembly. Consequently, generated neighboring parts may interpenetrate, lack valid connections, or collapse under gravity. We propose a physics-guided framework for improving single-image part-aware 3D generation with physically compatible geometry and stable connections. Our method resolves inter-part penetration, recovers a contact graph between neighboring parts, and introduces parameterized connectors at their contact surfaces. Using feedback from physical simulation, we refine connector placement, orientation, and dimensions to improve assembly stability while preserving the generated geometry. We further introduce a physics-based evaluation protocol that complements conventional geometric metrics by directly testing assembly validity and stability under gravity. Experiments comparing against multiple part-aware 3D generators show substantial improvements in physical realizability and stability while maintaining geometric quality. We additionally validate the resulting parts through 3D printing and real-world assembly.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [128] [Language Is an Insufficient Substrate for Quantitative Reasoning, and Consequential Domains Need Large Quantitative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12105)
*Reuben Vandeventer,David Imrem,David J. Wild*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that progress in Large Language Models (LLMs) will not translate to consequential quantitative decisions (e.g., pricing risk, medical triage, network security) because LLMs are trained on lossy human descriptions rather than quantitative records. This irreversible information loss cannot be recovered at any scale. The authors propose that consequential settings require three properties (reproducibility, lineage, and calibrated uncertainty) that LLMs cannot provide, defining a new model class called Large Quantitative Model (LQM).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prevailing assumption in applied machine learning is that advances in LLMs will solve consequential decision-making problems. This paper challenges that assumption by demonstrating that LLMs are fundamentally limited when applied to quantitative decisions due to their reliance on human descriptions rather than raw quantitative data.

Method: The authors formalize the limitation as a property of the representation (training data) rather than model capacity. They analyze what consequential settings require and identify three critical properties that a language substrate cannot provide by construction: reproducibility, lineage from outputs to source records, and calibrated uncertainty.

Result: The paper identifies three essential properties for consequential decisions that LLMs inherently cannot satisfy: (1) reproducibility - the ability to produce consistent results, (2) lineage - traceability from every output back to the source records that produced it, and (3) calibrated uncertainty - properly quantified confidence in outputs.

Conclusion: The authors argue that these properties define a distinct model class, termed Large Quantitative Model (LQM), which differs fundamentally from LLMs. LQMs would need to be trained on quantitative records directly rather than human descriptions, and would inherently provide reproducibility, lineage, and calibrated uncertainty by construction.

Abstract: The prevailing assumption in applied machine learning is that progress on consequential quantitative decisions such as pricing risk, allocating capital, triaging patients, or containing a network intrusion will follow from progress in large language models (LLMs). A language model is trained on a representation of the world that was produced by human description; description is a lossy encoding of the quantitative record, and the loss is irreversible: no downstream model, at any scale, can recover from a description what the description did not encode. We formalize this as a property of the representation on which a model is trained rather than of the model capacity, and we identify three further properties that consequential settings demand of a model and that a language substrate cannot supply by construction: reproducibility, lineage from every output back to the source records that produced. it, and calibrated uncertainty. We argue that these properties define a distinct model class, which we call the Large Quantitative Model (LQM).

</details>


### [129] [DU-NO: A Parameter-Efficient Double U-Shaped Neural Operator for Phase-Resolving Wave Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12115)
*Enrique Hernandez Noguera,Md Meftahul Ferdaus,Nathan Cooper,Elias Ioup,Mahdi Abdelguerfi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DU-NO (Double U-shaped Neural Operator) is a lightweight multiscale neural operator for phase-resolving wave modeling that achieves better accuracy than U-FNO with 10.8x fewer parameters by combining spectral operations with targeted convolutional branches at shallow encoder/decoder levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phase-resolving wave models like FUNWAVE-TVD are accurate but computationally expensive, making them unsuitable for ensembles, uncertainty quantification, and real-time forecasting. Existing neural operators achieving solver-level accuracy are too large (tens of millions of parameters). The need is for a compact yet accurate model.

Method: DU-NO is a multiscale U-shaped spectral operator that attaches lightweight convolutional U-Net branches only at its two shallowest encoder/decoder levels. This design follows a sampling argument: high-wavenumber content exists only on fine grids, so local full-band pathways are placed where that content lives, while coarse band-limited levels stay purely spectral. A depth-decaying mode schedule holds the model to 3.64M parameters.

Result: On the FUNWAVE-TVD benchmark, DU-NO achieves the best autoregressive rollout error among six identically trained architectures, improving on U-FNO by 14.9% with 10.8x fewer parameters. Frequency-band analysis shows gains across all bands, including the high-wavenumber band where truncated-spectral operators fail. Parameter-matched controls confirm the gain is architectural: rescaled to 3.6M budget, the best baseline still trails DU-NO by 28.6%. DU-NO also matches or exceeds baselines on 2D Navier-Stokes and PDEBench shallow-water rollouts.

Conclusion: DU-NO demonstrates that strategic placement of convolutional branches within a spectral operator architecture can achieve superior accuracy while maintaining computational efficiency. The architectural innovation (hybrid spectral-convolutional design with shallow-level local pathways) is confirmed by parameter-matched ablation studies. The approach generalizes beyond nearshore waves to other PDEs like Navier-Stokes and shallow-water equations.

Abstract: Phase-resolving wave models such as FUNWAVE-TVD are the accuracy standard for nearshore dynamics, resolving the shoaling, refraction, and breaking of individual waves, but their cost rules them out for the ensembles, uncertainty quantification, and real-time warning that operational forecasting demands. Neural operators promise solver-level accuracy at a fraction of that cost, yet on wave-dominated fields the accurate ones are large: hybrid spectral-convolutional operators such as U-FNO (the strongest baseline in our study after DU-NO) buy their fidelity with tens of millions of parameters. We introduce DU-NO (Double U-shaped Neural Operator), a multiscale U-shaped spectral operator that attaches lightweight convolutional U-Net branches only at its two shallowest encoder and decoder levels. The placement follows a sampling argument: high-wavenumber content exists only on fine grids, so the local, full-band pathways go where that content lives, while the coarse, band-limited levels stay purely spectral. A depth-decaying mode schedule holds the model to 3.64M parameters, an order of magnitude below U-FNO. On our publicly released FUNWAVE-TVD benchmark, DU-NO attains the best autoregressive rollout error of six identically trained architectures, improving on U-FNO by 14.9% with 10.8x fewer parameters, and a frequency-band analysis shows the gain holds across all bands, including the high-wavenumber band where truncated-spectral operators collapse. Parameter-matched controls confirm the gain is architectural: rescaled to the same 3.6M budget, the best baseline still trails DU-NO by 28.6%. The advantage carries beyond nearshore waves: DU-NO matches the strongest baselines on 2D Navier-Stokes and wins clearly on PDEBench shallow-water rollouts. Code, trained models, and evaluation artifacts are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/duno-code-5A7B/.

</details>


### [130] [What Drives Recovery in Agentic Text-to-Cypher? LAST-CQ: An LLM Agent Self-Refinement Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12746)
*Ioannis Prokopiou,Athanasios Aidinis,Panagiotis-Christos Kyrmpatsos,Pantelis Vikatos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates which components of agentic Text-to-Cypher pipelines contribute to performance gains, using LAST-CQ (a five-agent framework) as a testbed across 2,471 queries and six backbones. The key finding is that failure detection and retry routing matters most—not feedback sophistication or parallel sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic pipelines for structured-query generation are expanding, but it was unclear which parts of the loop actually produce gains. The researchers wanted to decompose the pipeline and identify the critical components.

Method: The authors used LAST-CQ—a five-agent, training-free, execution-grounded Text-to-Cypher framework—as an instrumented testbed. They ran three counterfactuals: (1) removing correction, (2) replacing LLM-synthesized feedback with raw database errors, and (3) using parallel sampling instead of serial retry. Experiments covered 2,471 live-database queries across six backbones spanning three vendor scale tiers.

Result: Correction recovery adds 3.1% aggregate execution-BLEU vs single-pass and 12.3% vs no-refinement (up to 80.7% for weakest backbone). Raw database errors perform nearly identically to LLM-synthesized feedback (~20% vs 19.9% naive exact match). Parallel sampling degrades quality by 10-11%. LAST-CQ recovers 91.7% of failed queries. N-gram overlap over-scores against set equivalence on 65.9% of results while under-scoring against judged semantics. The LLM judge is optimistic by 9 points vs human labels.

Conclusion: What works in agentic query generation is detecting failure and routing to retry—not feedback sophistication or sampling. The correction mechanism is the key value driver, while raw error feedback suffices and parallel sampling hurts. The study also reveals limitations in standard evaluation metrics (n-gram overlap and LLM judges).

Abstract: Agentic pipelines for structured-query generation are rapidly expanding, but it is unclear which part of the loop produces the gain. We use LAST-CQ -- a five-agent, training-free, execution-grounded Text-to-Cypher framework -- as an instrumented testbed, running three counterfactuals over 2,471 live-database queries and six backbones spanning three vendor scale tiers. Removing correction is worth between 3.1% aggregate execution-BLEU against the single-pass system and 12.3% against a no-refinement counterfactual (up to 80.7% for the weakest backbone). Replacing schema-grounded, LLM-synthesised feedback with raw database error strings costs almost nothing (20.9% vs. 19.9% naive exact match; <0.2% end-to-end; equivalent within $\pm 0.075$ set-F1 by two one-sided tests). Spending the same call budget on parallel sampling degrades quality by 10-11%. What works is detecting failure and routing it to a retry, not the feedback sophistication or number of samples. LAST-CQ itself recovers 91.7% of queries that fail under single-pass generation, while a query that succeeds first time still costs exactly one LLM call. We also show that n-gram overlap on serialised results is not a bound in either direction: it over-scores against set equivalence on 65.9% of results while under-scoring against judged semantics. Finally, we calibrate our LLM judge against blind human labels and find it optimistic by 9 points.

</details>


### [131] [Niching Agents in The Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12398)
*Gary B. Parker,Jim O'Connor,John Asaro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Core is a competitive co-evolution algorithm that evolves agents without fitness functions. This research investigates niching agents to specific environmental subsets, finding niched agents outperform those evolved in the full environment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While The Core has proven effective for evolving combat and navigation agents in Xpilot, this research aims to improve agent performance by niching them to specific subsets of the original environment rather than the entire system.

Method: Agents were niched to specific environmental subsets and compared against agents niched to the entire system and other sub-environments. Evolution occurs via local interactions through tournament selection, crossover, and mutation.

Result: Niched agents demonstrated superior performance compared to agents evolved in the complete environment and those niched to different sub-environments.

Conclusion: Niche specialization to specific environmental subsets enhances agent performance in The Core framework, suggesting that targeted evolution produces more capable autonomous controllers than broad-spectrum evolution.

Abstract: The Core is a unique competitive co-evolution algorithm that allows agents to evolve autonomous control without utilizing a traditional fitness function. The agents evolve via local interactions through tournament selection, crossover, and mutation, producing offspring by evolving better controllers. Previous works have shown The Core's ability to evolve agents capable of combat and navigation in the Xpilot video game. This research expands upon that premise by niching agents to specific subsets of the original environment The Core was tested in. Our results demonstrate the niched agents capacity for success over agents niched to the entire system and agents niched to different sub-environments.

</details>


### [132] [VRL-Bench: Benchmarking agents on computer control tasks under finite trial budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12404)
*Yu Bai,Yukai Miao,Dawei Wang,Li Chen,Yanyu Ren,Yuqian Shi,Dan Li,Ying Xiong,Chengqiu Tan,Run Zhou,Li Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VRL-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating trial-and-error learning methods for language agents on computer control tasks. It evaluates several verbal-memory methods (including Reflexion) across MiniWoB and WebShop, finding that while some methods improve success in certain settings, they can also reduce it in others. The paper proposes VEX², a verbal exploration-exploitation scheduler that achieves positive gains in all six evaluated settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve language agents on complex tasks like computer control through trial-and-error learning. Current verbal reinforcement learning methods like Reflexion show mixed results, and there was no fair evaluation benchmark for comparing these methods under finite trial budgets.

Method: The authors introduce VRL-Bench as an evaluation harness. They evaluate three models across MiniWoB and WebShop datasets, testing several verbal-memory methods (Reflexion and successors). They conduct replay experiments to analyze the trade-off between exploiting experience and continued exploration. They propose VEX², which uses a language model to jointly select policies and allocate remaining trial budgets.

Result: All evaluated verbal-memory methods improve observed success over memory-free retry in some settings but reduce it in others. Replay experiments reveal that using reflection can reduce success rates due to a trade-off between exploiting experience and continued exploration. VEX² is the only method to achieve positive observed success-rate gains over retry in all six settings.

Conclusion: VEX², a verbal exploration-exploitation scheduler, successfully addresses the trade-off between exploiting past experience and continued exploration. It is the only evaluated method that consistently outperforms memory-free retry across all settings, demonstrating the importance of intelligent policy selection and budget allocation in trial-and-error learning for language agents.

Abstract: Learning from trial and error is a promising way to improve language agents on complex tasks such as computer control. Reflexion introduced verbal reinforcement learning, which turns failed trials into text that guides later attempts without updating model parameters. We introduce VRL-Bench, a harness for fair evaluation of trial-and-error learning under finite trial budgets. Across three models on MiniWoB and WebShop, we evaluate updates from several prominent verbal-memory methods spanning Reflexion and later work: each improves observed success over memory-free retry in some settings but reduces it in others. Replay experiments show that using reflection can reduce success rates, revealing a trade-off between exploiting experience and continued exploration. We propose VEX$^2$, a verbal exploration--exploitation scheduler that uses a language model to jointly select policies and allocate the remaining trial budget. VEX$^2$ is the only evaluated update to achieve positive observed success-rate gains over retry in all six settings.

</details>


### [133] [SoK: Rethinking Jailbreaking in the Era of Agentic AI: Attacks, Defenses, and Practical Consideration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12413)
*Md Jueal Mia,Yanzhao Wu,Selcuk Uluagac,M. Hadi Amini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This SoK paper investigates whether traditional jailbreak security findings remain valid for modern LLMs and agentic AI systems. It develops unified taxonomies of attacks and defenses across the agentic execution pipeline (user interaction, planning, memory, tool use, inter-agent communication) and introduces a security-utility-efficiency evaluation framework. Empirical evaluation reveals three key gaps: native alignment doesn't guarantee jailbreak robustness, defense effectiveness varies significantly with costs in utility and latency, and intermediate agent components can be compromised even when final responses are filtered.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid evolution of LLMs from conversational assistants to agentic AI systems (with reasoning, planning, tool invocation, persistent memory, and inter-agent communication) combined with substantially stronger native safety alignment raises fundamental questions about the validity of established jailbreak-security findings from earlier LLM generations.

Method: Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) approach with: (1) unified taxonomies of attacks and defenses spanning all agentic execution pipeline components; (2) security-utility-efficiency evaluation framework distinguishing native harmful-prompt safety, adversarial jailbreak robustness, and agent-level security outcomes; (3) controlled empirical study of representative attacks and defenses within a common agentic framework.

Result: Three important gaps identified: (1) Strong native alignment does not imply robustness to adversarial jailbreaks; (2) Defense effectiveness is highly model-, attack-, and component-dependent, coming at substantial cost in over-refusal, utility, and latency; (3) Low final-response attack success can mask severe intermediate compromise—planning, memory, and tool interactions may remain unsafe even when final response is successfully filtered.

Conclusion: A paradigm shift is needed from response-centric jailbreak defense toward cross-layer, execution-aware security that protects agent state, component transitions, and external actions while preserving practical utility and efficiency.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly evolving from conversational assistants into agentic AI systems that reason, plan, invoke tools, maintain persistent memory, communicate with other agents, and execute multi-step tasks. At the same time, modern models exhibit substantially stronger native safety alignment than earlier generations on which many jailbreak attacks and defenses were originally studied. This shift raises a fundamental question: \textit{which established jailbreak-security findings remain valid in the era of modern LLMs and agentic AI?} We address this question through a Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) that reframes jailbreak security around the full agentic execution pipeline. We develop unified taxonomies of attacks and defenses spanning user interaction, planning and reasoning, memory, tool use, and inter-agent communication, and introduce a security--utility--efficiency evaluation framework that separates native harmful-prompt safety, adversarial jailbreak robustness, and agent-level security outcomes. We further conduct a controlled empirical study of representative attacks and defenses within a common agentic framework. Our results reveal three important gaps. First, strong native alignment does not imply robustness to adversarial jailbreaks. Second, defense effectiveness is highly model-, attack-, and component-dependent and can come at substantial cost in over-refusal, utility, and latency. Third, low final-response attack success can mask severe intermediate compromise: planning, memory, and tool interactions may remain unsafe even when the final response is successfully filtered. These findings motivate a shift from response-centric jailbreak defense toward cross-layer, execution-aware security that protects agent state, component transitions, and external actions while preserving practical utility and efficiency.

</details>


### [134] [Hierarchical Belief Modeling for Zero-Shot Opponent Adaptation in Partially Observable Multi-Agent Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12422)
*Kowei Shih,Lu Cheng,Zeyu Wang,Yeyun Xu,Kejian Tong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HORIZON is a hierarchical AI agent for Lux AI Season 3 that addresses partial observability, randomized dynamics, and best-of-five match structure through hierarchical control combining spatial perception, belief tracking, graph attention, exploration, and opponent-conditioned policy learning, trained with PPO in JAX.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lux AI Season 3 presents unique challenges including partial observability where agents cannot see the full game state, randomized episode-level dynamics that introduce uncertainty, and a best-of-five match structure that rewards both tactical execution and fast adaptation. Existing agents struggle with inferring hidden game parameters and adapting to opponent styles across matches.

Method: HORIZON employs a hierarchical architecture separating short-horizon control from cross-match meta reasoning. Key components include: (1) Symmetry aware spatial perception for pattern recognition, (2) Dual memory belief tracking for state estimation, (3) Relic centric graph attention for strategic reasoning, (4) Information gain driven exploration, and (5) Opponent conditioned policy mixture. Training uses PPO in a large-scale JAX simulator with auxiliary belief and world model objectives to stabilize learning.

Result: The agent explicitly infers hidden game parameters and opponent style. Experiments demonstrate consistent gains across multiple metrics: match win rate, episode win rate, adaptation gain, and league rating over strong recurrent and feed-forward baselines.

Conclusion: HORIZON successfully addresses the core challenges of Lux AI Season 3 through hierarchical reasoning and explicit inference of hidden information. The combination of belief tracking, graph attention, and opponent-adaptive policies enables superior performance in both tactical execution and strategic adaptation across match series.

Abstract: Lux AI Season 3 requires agents to act under partial observability, randomized episode level dynamics, and a best of five match structure that rewards both tactical execution and fast adaptation. We present HORIZON, a hierarchical agent that combines symmetry aware spatial perception, dual memory belief tracking, relic centric graph attention, information gain driven exploration, and an opponent conditioned policy mixture. HORIZON separates short horizon control from cross match meta reasoning, while auxiliary belief and world model objectives stabilize learning. Trained with PPO in a large scale JAX simulator, the resulting agent explicitly infers hidden game parameters and opponent style. Experiments show consistent gains in match win rate, episode win rate, adaptation gain, and league rating over strong recurrent and feed forward baselines.

</details>


### [135] [Do LLMs Trust the Accuser or the Accusation? Measuring Belief Shifts in Werewolf](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12446)
*Yu-Yu Yang,Ti-Rong Wu,Hung Guei,Hsing-Yu Chen,I-Chen Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a belief-shift evaluation benchmark for Werewolf that analyzes how LLM agents update their beliefs based on suspicion and accusation messages, revealing that larger models better distinguish wolves but still struggle to integrate accusation content with source trust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM agents in social-deduction games like Werewolf rely primarily on final game outcomes, which fail to capture the nuanced communication skills involved in belief updating and strategic reasoning.

Method: The authors annotate suspicion and accusation messages from LLM-played games, measure how an observing village-side model's beliefs change after each message, and evaluate 40 open-weight LLM configurations on 1,224 annotated messages.

Result: Larger models better distinguish true wolves from villagers based on game history, but accusations still strongly influence their beliefs. Models become more suspicious of the accused target and less suspicious of the accuser, especially when the accuser is trusted—even if the accuser is wolf-aligned. Larger models better resist accusations from accusers they already distrust.

Conclusion: Current open-weight LLMs up to 120B parameters still struggle to integrate accusation content with source trust in strategic communication, indicating a fundamental limitation in their reasoning capabilities for social deduction tasks.

Abstract: Social-deduction games such as Werewolf are increasingly used to evaluate LLM agents, but existing evaluations often rely on final game outcomes. We propose a belief-shift evaluation benchmark in Werewolf for analyzing communication skills through belief updating. Using LLM-played games, we annotate suspicion and accusation messages and measure how an observing village-side model's beliefs change after each message. We evaluate 40 open-weight LLM configurations on 1,224 annotated messages. Our results show that larger models better distinguish true wolves from villagers based on game history, but accusations still strongly influence their beliefs. Models become more suspicious of the accused target and less suspicious of the accuser, especially when the accuser is trusted, even if the accuser is wolf-aligned. Larger models better resist accusations from accusers they already distrust. Overall, our findings suggest that current open-weight LLMs up to 120B parameters still struggle to integrate accusation content with source trust in strategic communication. Our benchmark and code are available at https://rlg.iis.sinica.edu.tw/papers/werewolf-accusation-benchmark.

</details>


### [136] [EvoRS: On-Policy Self-Evolution of Reward Systems for Open-Ended Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12459)
*Weiyuan Li,Aili Chen,Xintao Wang,Yikai Zhang,Qingqing Dong,Jinghan Xu,Hongru Hou,Wenxuan Zhao,Chengkun Lang,Jun Gao,Yuanli Guo,Hongcheng Guo,Yanghua Xiao,Deqing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoRS introduces a self-evolving reinforcement learning framework that dynamically updates the reward system (Reward-DAG) from on-policy experience to maintain reliability in open-ended tasks, outperforming fixed reward systems in writing and roleplay benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended RL tasks rely on rubric-based rewards that become unreliable during training due to reward hacking and reduced response discriminability as the policy optimizes. Existing dynamic-rubric methods adapt evaluation criteria but don't address scoring mechanism or signal composition failures.

Method: EvoRS represents the reward system as an executable Reward-DAG and uses an agentic designer that updates this system from on-policy rollouts and reward traces to maintain train-time reliability throughout training.

Result: Across writing and roleplay tasks, EvoRS achieves the best quality under all three judges, outperforming the policy by 2.107 and 4.767 points respectively, while reducing reward hacking and coverage failures and preserving reward informativeness.

Conclusion: A comprehensive fixed reward system cannot remain reliable in open-ended tasks and must evolve throughout training. Ablations confirm that adaptation of the reward system is essential for maintaining evaluation reliability.

Abstract: Open-ended reinforcement learning often relies on rubric-based rewards for tasks without directly verifiable answers. Yet the policy and reward system form a dynamic feedback loop: as the policy optimizes the current reward, an initially useful reward system may become unreliable due to reward hacking or reduced response discriminability. The reward system should therefore evolve rather than remain fixed during training. Existing dynamic-rubric methods adapt evaluation criteria, but reward failures can also arise from scoring mechanisms or signal composition. We introduce EvoRS, a self-evolving RL framework that evolves the reward system from on-policy experience, representing it as an executable Reward-DAG. Specifically, an agentic designer updates this system from on-policy rollouts and reward traces to maintain train-time reliability. Across writing and roleplay, EvoRS achieves the best quality under all three judges, outperforming the policy by \(2.107\) and \(4.767\) points, respectively, while reducing reward hacking and coverage failures and preserving reward informativeness. Ablations confirm that a comprehensive fixed reward system cannot remain reliable in open-ended tasks and must evolve throughout training.

</details>


### [137] [Beyond Vector Similarity: Hierarchical Context-Aware Graph RAG vs Standard RAG in Enterprise Code Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12464)
*Nilesh Jaiswal,Aniket Agrawal,Arjit Shukla,Divya Malhotra,Saurabh Garg,Suchit Puri,Suddhasatwa Bhaumik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Hierarchical Context-Resident Graph (HCRG) methodology to improve automated code translation from monolithic systems to microservices using LLMs. It addresses the limitation of traditional vector-based RAG that fails to capture topological relationships, leading to high compilation failure rates. The solution uses tree-sitter for AST extraction, Google Cloud Spanner Property Graph for architectural edges, and Gemini Context Cache for topological code translation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional vector-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (Standard RAG) struggles to capture topological relationships in code. It fetches isolated chunks that sever inheritance chains, leading to high compilation failure rates. Traditional metrics like CodeBLEU (91% for both methods) mask these structural failures behind syntactically plausible but broken code.

Method: The HCRG pipeline uses: 1) tree-sitter for Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) extraction; 2) mapping architectural edges into a Google Cloud Spanner Property Graph; 3) serializing this structure into a Gemini Context Cache for topological, parent-first code translation. Evaluation uses a custom 7-metric Software Engineering framework instead of naive text-overlap metrics.

Result: Graph RAG significantly reduced: API hallucination rates (56.4% → 16.2%), improved Dependency Resolution Quality (34.8% → 65.9%), and Parent-Child Consistency (26.7% → 45.5%). However, trade-offs included reduced Cyclomatic Complexity Consistency (71.6% → 46.7%) and slightly degraded Docstring Preservation (67.0% → 61.0%). CodeBLEU scored 91% for both methods, demonstrating its limitation in detecting structural failures.

Conclusion: While trading code complexity for reduced hallucinations, Graph RAG provides a substantially more viable, architecturally sound path for automated enterprise codebase modernization. The traditional CodeBLEU metric effectively masked Standard RAG's structural failures, highlighting the need for domain-specific evaluation frameworks.

Abstract: As enterprises modernize legacy monolithic systems to microservices, Large Language Models (LLMs) are heavily utilized for automated code translation. However, traditional vector-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (Standard RAG) struggles to capture topological relationships. It fetches isolated chunks that sever inheritance chains, leading to high compilation failure rates. This paper introduces a Hierarchical Context-Resident Graph (HCRG) methodology to resolve these limitations. Our pipeline uses tree-sitter for Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) extraction, maps architectural edges into a Google Cloud Spanner Property Graph, and serializes this structure into a Gemini Context Cache for topological, parent-first code translation. We shift evaluation from naive text-overlap to a custom 7-metric Software Engineering framework. Traditional metrics like CodeBLEU (which scored 91% for both methods) effectively masked Standard RAG's structural failures behind syntactically plausible but broken code. Empirically, Graph RAG decisively mitigates dependency loss: API hallucination rates dropped from 56.4% to 16.2%, Dependency Resolution Quality improved from 34.8% to 65.9%, and Parent-Child Consistency rose from 26.7% to 45.5%. However, Graph RAG introduces specific trade-offs. The dense global context causes defensive over-engineering by the LLM, reducing Cyclomatic Complexity Consistency from 71.6% to 46.7%, and slightly degrades Docstring Preservation (67.0% to 61.0%). Ultimately, while trading code complexity for reduced hallucinations, Graph RAG provides a substantially more viable, architecturally sound path for automated enterprise codebase modernization.

</details>


### [138] [When Does AI Augment Work? A Workflow-Level Framework for Human-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12482)
*CIVIC-AI Collaboration,:,Jiaying Wu,Caleb Ziems,Raymond Chan,Nancy F. Chen,Corlyss Chua,Gerard Chung,Jungpil Hahn,Wee Sun Lee,Zhengyuan Liu,Jamie Ng,Desmond C. Ong,Jeryl Ong,Da Ren Soon,Tianqi Song,Zhi-Xuan Tan,Sixing Tao,Emily Yang,Yajing Yang,Stella Xin Yin,Min-Yen Kan,Diyi Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a framework to measure AI's value in the workplace beyond current automation metrics, focusing on human-AI collaboration and workflow augmentation. It defines AI augmentation through six conditions and demonstrates the framework using AI-mediated social surveys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current studies measure AI value mainly through automation capabilities and adoption rates, which fails to capture the transformative potential of human-agent collaboration in reshaping work. The authors argue this narrow focus misses the broader impacts on workflows and human development.

Method: The authors establish a precise definition of AI augmentation comprising six conditions: (1) durable net value, (2) meaningful human control, (3) accountability and recovery, and (4-6) long-term human development through learning, career pathways, and job purpose. They apply this framework in a case study of AI-mediated social surveys.

Result: The paper provides a framework with six conditions for evaluating AI augmentation and demonstrates its application through a concrete case study of AI-mediated social surveys.

Conclusion: The framework offers a tool for organizations, researchers, and government leaders to evaluate and make sense of AI's future impact on work, enabling more comprehensive assessment beyond narrow automation metrics.

Abstract: We aim to characterise the value of artificial intelligence in the workplace. Current studies largely measure this value in terms of the current automation capabilities and public adoption of AI. However, such metrics ignore the greater impacts of human--agent collaboration in transforming the nature of work. To account for this, we must expand the scope of our analysis beyond atomised tasks of today, and instead focus on how AI can augment entire workflows of the future. To ground this analysis, we establish a precise definition of AI augmentation comprising six conditions, spanning durable net value, meaningful human control, accountability and recovery, and long-term human development through learning, career pathways, and job purpose. We elaborate on these conditions and apply the framework in a case study of AI-mediated social surveys. We conclude by outlining how organisations, researchers, and government leaders can use this framework to make sense of the future of work.

</details>


### [139] [Confidence-Gated Transductive Test Generation for Code Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12489)
*Sungjae Lee,Youngsik Yoon,Seockbean Song,Siwei Wang,Wei Chen,Jungseul Ok*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoTT is a test case synthesis method for evaluating LLM-generated programs that uses confidence-based gating to adaptively allocate computation - applying efficient inductive generation by default and invoking more expensive transductive generation only when confidence is low.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test case synthesis is crucial for evaluating and ranking programs generated by LLMs, but constructing high-quality test cases is challenging because reliable expected outputs are difficult to obtain.

Method: Confidence-Gated Transductive Test Generation (CoTT) uses a two-stage approach: first applies an efficient inductive procedure, then invokes transductive generation only when inductive confidence is low. This adaptive design improves output reliability while allocating extra computation only when needed.

Result: CoTT outperforms prior baselines across reported metrics on code reranking benchmarks while reducing cost relative to applying transductive generation to every input.

Conclusion: Confidence-based allocation of test-time computation provides a favorable efficiency-effectiveness trade-off with a single efficient LLM.

Abstract: Test case synthesis is crucial for evaluating and ranking programs generated by large language models (LLMs). However, constructing high-quality test cases remains challenging because reliable expected outputs are often difficult to obtain. We propose Confidence-Gated Transductive Test Generation (CoTT), which first uses an efficient inductive procedure and invokes transductive generation only when inductive confidence is low. This adaptive design improves output reliability while allocating extra computation only when needed. On code reranking benchmarks, CoTT outperforms prior baselines across the reported metrics while reducing cost relative to applying transductive generation to every input. These results show that confidence-based allocation of test-time computation provides a favorable efficiency-effectiveness trade-off with a single efficient LLM.

</details>


### [140] [Information Specialization and Constrained Synthesis in Multi-Agent LLM Forecasting: A Prospective Live-Study of the 2026 FIFA World Cup](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12495)
