*Julian Varghese,Lucas Bickmann,Sarah Sandmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study evaluates whether multi-agent systems with specialized roles produce distinct forecasts and whether synthesizing these forecasts improves prediction utility. Using a frontier foundation model, two forecasting agents (quantitative specialist focusing on structured statistics vs. news specialist focusing on unstructured current information) were combined with critic and meta-agent stages to predict 2026 FIFA World Cup matches. The news specialist outperformed, and the meta-agent rarely added unique insights beyond the specialists' forecasts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether organizing large language models into multi-agent systems with specialized roles produces distinct forecasts and whether subsequent synthesis improves forecasting utility, particularly in information-dense sports prediction contexts.

Method: Live prospective evaluation over 56 matches of the 2026 FIFA World Cup using a constant frontier foundation model. Two primary forecasting agents with contrasting specialist roles (quantitative specialist using structured performance statistics vs. news specialist using current injuries, tactics, and press conference information). A separate critic reviewed forecasts before a meta-agent combined them, creating a sequential four-agent model. Betting market forecasts served as external benchmark.

Result: The news specialist achieved the highest mean probability-weighted Top-3 utility and matched the betting market in Top-3 exact-score hits. The two specialists agreed on at least two of three scorelines in 50 out of 56 matches. The meta-agent never generated more than one scoreline outside the specialists' forecast set.

Conclusion: Rapidly changing, unstructured information (such as news about injuries and tactics) can provide valuable forecasting signals alongside structured statistics. However, adding critic and meta-agent stages does not necessarily create complementary information or improve upon the strongest specialist's performance.

Abstract: Large language models are being organized into multi-agent systems with specialized roles, but whether such specialization produces distinct forecasts and whether subsequent synthesis improves utility remains unclear. In this study, we carried out a live, prospective evaluation over the final 56 matches of the information-dense 2026 FIFA World Cup, keeping a frontier foundation model constant while assigning two primary forecasting agents contrasting specialist roles: a quantitative specialist focusing on structured performance statistics and a news specialist focusing on current injuries, tactics and information from press conferences. Their forecasts were then reviewed by a separate critic before being combined by a meta-agent, resulting in a sequential four-agent model. Forecasts from the betting market served as an external benchmark. The news specialist obtained the highest mean probability-weighted Top-3 utility and matched the betting market in Top-3 exact-score hits. Nevertheless, the two specialist forecasters agreed on at least two of the three scorelines in 50 out of 56 matches, and the meta-agent never generated more than one scoreline outside the specialists' forecast set. These findings show that rapidly changing, unstructured information can provide a valuable forecasting signal alongside structured statistics, whereas adding critic and meta-agent stages does not necessarily create complementary information or improve on the strongest specialist.

</details>


### [141] [From Collaboration to Capability: Internalizing Routed LLM Experts into Compact Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12578)
*Frank Nie,Shuyao Wang,Ethan B. Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Rivet is a compact controller system that learns to internalize expertise from stronger experts through collaboration. It uses expert-augmented reinforcement learning and verified trajectory internalization to consolidate successful interactions. After expert removal, the trained controller can still generate reasoning, code, and interaction structures using local Python execution. RIVET-4B achieves 44.16% average accuracy on competition-mathematics benchmarks, with Stage II adding 6.49 points improvement after expert removal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses whether a compact controller can learn from both the decisions and the reasoning/code of stronger experts, and continue functioning effectively after those experts are removed from the system.

Method: Two-stage approach: (1) Expert-augmented reinforcement learning applies shared outcome signal to controller decisions and returned expert spans; (2) Verified trajectory internalization consolidates complete successful interactions through format-aware supervised training. The system uses local Python execution with no external LLM.

Result: RIVET-1.7B achieves 28.25% average accuracy; RIVET-4B achieves 44.16% average accuracy across seven competition-mathematics benchmarks. Stage II improves RIVET-4B's accuracy after expert removal by 6.49 points. GPQA-Diamond results demonstrate generalization to scientific reasoning.

Conclusion: Training on both the content and structure of verified expert collaborations, including format weighting, effectively enables a compact controller to internalize expert capabilities and maintain performance after expert removal.

Abstract: A compact controller can coordinate stronger experts by selecting whom to consult, formulating requests, and integrating their responses. We study whether learning from both the controller's decisions and the experts' reasoning and code improves its generation after expert removal. We introduce \textsc{Rivet} for \emph{collaboration internalization}: expert-augmented reinforcement learning applies a shared outcome signal to controller decisions and returned expert spans, and verified trajectory internalization consolidates complete successful interactions through format-aware supervised training. The deployed controller generates reasoning, code, and interaction structure with local Python execution and no external LLM. Across seven competition-mathematics benchmarks, RIVET-1.7B and RIVET-4B achieve average accuracies of $28.25\%$ and $44.16\%$; Stage~II improves RIVET-4B's accuracy after expert removal by $6.49$ points, and GPQA-Diamond results provide evidence of generalization to scientific reasoning. Ablations show gains from ordinary trajectory supervision and additional format weighting, supporting the effectiveness of training on the content and structure of verified collaborations.

</details>


### [142] [Beyond Generation and Accuracy: Diagnosing and Enhancing Visual Chain-of-Thought for Geometry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12606)
*Zhitong Dong,Jicai Pan,Yingguo Gao,Jingting Ding,Hao Chen,Jinjie Gu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GeoVAD-Bench is a diagnostic benchmark for evaluating Visual Chain-of-Thought (VCoT) in geometry problem solving, featuring a five-dimensional trajectory diagnosis and controlled intervention settings. The study reveals that while high-quality visual aids offer substantial gains, autonomous generation suffers from compounding errors. GeoWeave-8B, developed through a specialized data construction pipeline and progressive training framework, achieves +25.3% improvement in final geometric accuracy and +30.4% gain in process average across diagnostic dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations for multimodal reasoning in geometry problems assess visual generation quality and final answer accuracy in isolation, but fail to examine whether intermediate visual aids are geometrically valid, effectively utilized in subsequent reasoning, or causally responsible for task success. There was a need to bridge this gap by systematically isolating intermediate error modes, causal gains of visual aids, and the resulting autonomy gap.

Method: The researchers introduced GeoVAD-Bench with a fine-grained five-dimensional trajectory diagnosis covering perception, auxiliary quality, utilization, deductive reasoning, and final correctness, combined with controlled No-Aux, Auto-Aux, and GT-Aux intervention settings. They developed a specialized data construction pipeline encompassing geometric perception, diagram editing, and interleaved visual-textual reasoning trajectories, along with a progressive SFT and multimodal RL training framework.

Result: The findings reveal that while high-quality auxiliary aids offer substantial theoretical gains for geometric problem solving, autonomous generation is frequently hampered by compounding errors across geometric perception, faithful visual manipulation, visual-state grounding, and deductive reasoning. GeoWeave-8B outperforms the base model by +25.3% in final geometric accuracy and achieves a +30.4% gain in process average across the four intermediate diagnostic dimensions.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the importance of diagnostic evaluation in geometry problem solving and provides a comprehensive framework for understanding and improving visual reasoning in multimodal models. The GeoWeave-8B model shows significant improvements, suggesting that the proposed data construction pipeline and training framework effectively address the challenges in autonomous visual auxiliary generation for geometry reasoning.

Abstract: While multimodal reasoning has advanced rapidly, solving complex geometry problems critically hinges on active visual assistance, such as constructing auxiliary lines, spurring the rise of Visual Chain-of-Thought (VCoT). However, existing evaluations typically assess visual generation quality and final answer accuracy in isolation, failing to examine whether intermediate visual aids are geometrically valid, effectively utilized in subsequent reasoning, or causally responsible for task success. To bridge this gap, we introduce GeoVAD-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that pairs a fine-grained five-dimensional trajectory diagnosis covering perception, auxiliary quality, utilization, deductive reasoning, and final correctness with controlled No-Aux, Auto-Aux, and GT-Aux intervention settings to systematically isolate intermediate error modes, the causal gains of visual aids, and the resulting autonomy gap. Our findings reveal that while high-quality auxiliary aids offer substantial theoretical gains for geometric problem solving, autonomous generation is frequently hampered by compounding errors across geometric perception, faithful visual manipulation, visual-state grounding, and deductive reasoning. Guided by these diagnostic insights, we establish a specialized data construction pipeline encompassing geometric perception, diagram editing, and interleaved visual-textual reasoning trajectories, and develop a progressive SFT and multimodal RL training framework. The resulting model, GeoWeave-8B, outperforms the base model by +25.3% in final geometric accuracy and achieves a +30.4% gain in process average across the four intermediate diagnostic dimensions.

</details>


### [143] [SteerDuplex: Steerable Duplex Speech Dialogue Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12623)
*Utkarsh Tyagi,Ramaneswaran Selvakumar,Advait Gosai,Sonal Kumar,Nikhil Barhate,Isabell Sagar,Steven Li,Miheer Bavare,Daniel Quigley,Fabiola Tapia Carrillo,Jose M Patron E,Diego Macías Gutiérrez,Paul Song,Ramani Duraiswami,Dinesh Manocha,Yunzhong He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces SteerDuplex, a full-duplex speech model for spoken dialogue with steerable attributes (tone, persona, speaking rate, voice style). It proposes a taxonomy of text- and audio-based steerability, presents the SteerBench benchmark with 390 spoken prompts and 1,067 rubrics, and uses two-stage RL with hybrid rewards to improve turn-taking and response quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-duplex spoken dialogue models support low-latency turn taking, interruption handling, and backchanneling, but steerability—the ability to shift conversational behavior along attributes like tone, persona, speaking rate, and voice style in response to user instructions—remains underexplored. Existing models lack systematic capabilities for controllable spoken dialogue.

Method: The authors: (1) introduced a taxonomy of text- and audio-based steerability; (2) built SteerDuplex, a Moshi-based full-duplex speech model fine-tuned on natural and synthetic dialogues targeting instruction following, vocal delivery, reasoning, and duplex interaction; (3) applied two-stage RL with hybrid rewards combining verifiable interaction checks and judge-based semantic feedback; (4) created SteerBench benchmark with 390 spoken prompts and 1,067 human-authored binary audio/text rubrics across tone, persona, style/accent, and speed/length.

Result: Supervised training improved audio-steering average pass rate by 44.5 percentage points over the strongest open baseline. On Audio MultiChallenge, task average pass rate improved by 7 points. RL further raised source-clean interruption response from 72.5% to 82.5% and reduced synthetic pause barge-in from 26.5% to 9%. Reward probing revealed reward hacking through incomplete responses.

Conclusion: SteerDuplex and SteerBench enable systematic research on spoken steerability. The reward analysis demonstrates that timing gains must be evaluated alongside response completeness to avoid reward hacking. The model achieves substantial improvements in steering capabilities while maintaining or improving aggregate task scores.

Abstract: Full-duplex spoken dialogue models support low-latency turn taking, interruption handling, and backchanneling, yet a key capability remains underexplored: steerability, the ability to reliably shift conversational behavior along attributes such as tone, persona, speaking rate, and voice style in response to user instructions. We introduce a taxonomy of text- and audio-based steerability that identifies substantial gaps in current full-duplex models. To address this gap, we introduce SteerDuplex, a Moshi-based full-duplex speech model fine-tuned on natural conversations and synthetic dialogues targeting instruction following, vocal delivery, reasoning, and duplex interaction. We further apply two-stage reinforcement learning (RL) with hybrid rewards, combining verifiable interaction checks and judge-based semantic feedback to improve timing and response continuity. To evaluate full-duplex spoken steerability, we introduce SteerBench, a benchmark with 390 spoken prompts and 1,067 human-authored binary audio and text rubrics spanning tone, persona, style/accent, and speed/length. On SteerBench, supervised training improves audio-steering average pass rate by 44.5 percentage points over the strongest evaluated open baseline. On Audio MultiChallenge, task average pass rate improves by 7 points over its strongest evaluated open baseline. RL further raises source-clean interruption response from 72.5% to 82.5% and reduces synthetic pause barge-in from 26.5% to 9%. Steering and aggregate task scores remain comparable or higher, while reward probes reveal reward hacking through incomplete responses. Our model and benchmark support systematic research on spoken steerability, with reward analysis showing why timing gains must be evaluated alongside response completeness.

</details>


### [144] [Generative AI Use Cases In Real Estate Marketing: Adoption and Constraints in Germany](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12684)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Felicia Perpina,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study examines how generative AI (GenAI) is being used in real estate marketing through 11 semi-structured interviews with German real estate agents. It finds that GenAI is primarily used for marketing communication, especially writing exposé texts, with a human-in-the-loop approach where AI drafts and humans verify. Key constraints include integration issues, data availability, and compliance concerns rather than model capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While generative AI is transforming work organization, empirical evidence of GenAI adoption in real estate agents' daily practice remains limited. This study addresses this gap by investigating actual usage patterns in the real estate marketing sector.

Method: Empirical study conducted in Germany using eleven semi-structured interviews with real estate agents to explore GenAI adoption in their day-to-day practice.

Result: GenAI is already used across various activities, with marketing communication being the most prominent. Writing exposé texts is the only widely established use case. Usage is uneven and emergent. Interaction follows a human-in-the-loop pattern: GenAI handles drafting, structuring, and retrieval while agents curate, verify, and decide. Constraints primarily relate to integration with listings/documents, data availability, and compliance in sensitive tasks, rather than model capabilities.

Conclusion: The study provides a grounded map of existing and potential GenAI use cases in real estate marketing and identifies practical implications for adoption, suggesting that barriers are more about implementation and data issues than technological limitations.

Abstract: Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) is changing how work is organized and performed. Real estate marketing is a prime example of this, yet evidence of GenAI in real estate agents' day-to-day practice remains scarce. In this work, we report on our insights from a German-based empirical study with eleven semi-structured interviews. GenAI is already utilized across different activities, with marketing communication being the most prominent. Concrete use cases are emergent and unevenly adopted, with writing exposé texts being the only widely established one. Interaction is predominantly human-in-the-loop: GenAI drafts, structures, and retrieves, while real estate agents curate, verify, and decide. Constraints stem less from model capability than from integration with listings and documents, data availability, and compliance in sensitive tasks. The study contributes a grounded map of existing and potential use cases and identifies tentative practical implications for adoption.

</details>


### [145] [Residual Vector-based Reconstruction as Long-Context Recall Regardless of Context Window Size](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12686)
*MyungHoon Ryu,XinYu Piao,Jong-Kook Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A method to enable LLMs to recall long-context information (up to 2 million tokens) with near-constant GPU memory usage by reconstructing facts from parameter activations (residual vectors) in feed-forward layers, without requiring additional training or fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs face token-level memory usage that increases proportionally with input length, and existing methods (model optimization and lossy prompt compression) fail to solve the long-context recall problem beyond pretrained context windows.

Method: Reconstruct facts using parameter activations in the LLM's feed-forward layers, which store residual vectors representing facts from the source document. This allows deterministic reconstruction of query-relevant facts without referencing the original document.

Result: The proposed method enables answering single-fact questions in two-million-token story contexts where previous methods fail.

Conclusion: The method successfully achieves near-constant GPU memory usage as context length increases while preserving high fidelity, without any fine-tuning of model weights.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) process long contexts, including long documents and lengthy conversations, but face token-level memory usage that increases proportionally to input length. Although model optimization and lossy prompt compression are widely used, these methods still fail to solve the long-context recall problem beyond pretrained and size-constrained context windows. This paper proposes a long-context recall method that maintains near-constant GPU memory usage as context length increases, without additional training. The main idea is to reconstruct facts using parameter activations in the LLM's feed-forward layers, which store residual vectors representing facts from the source document. Utilizing residual vectors allows the LLM to deterministically reconstruct query relevant facts without referencing the original document, preserving high fidelity and reducing memory usage without fine-tuning weights. Experimental results show that the proposed method enables answering single-fact questions in two-million-token story contexts where previous methods fail.

</details>


### [146] [I Am AdMan: A Pipeline for Automatic Generation of Personalized Advertising Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12694)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Claudius Budcke,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes AdMan, a multi-agent pipeline that uses AI to transform customer data into personalized advertising images at scale. The system generates persona-based images conditioned on product references, with LLM-based quality control. Evaluation across 1745 advertisements shows feasibility but with limitations depending on product complexity and model configuration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While personalized marketing effectively matches products to customers, advertising visuals remain generic and weakly tailored to individuals. Existing research shows AI can improve personalized ad creation for text, but systematic translation of customer information into fully AI-generated personalized imagery at scale remains underexplored.

Method: AdMan pipeline with three components: (1) Customer data to persona transformation, (2) Personalized image generation conditioned on product reference images, (3) LLM-based judge agent for automated quality control. Implementation with two model configurations. Evaluation: 4 products, 6 celebrity personas (qualitative), 100 real customer profiles (quantitative), 1745 total advertisements. Combined qualitative expert focus group and quantitative artifact-rate assessment.

Result: Pipeline can generate photorealistic and personalized advertisements. However, performance varies substantially by product complexity and model configuration.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility and current limitations of fully automated image generation for personalized advertising. Extends AI-based personalized advertising literature by showing technical implementation and evaluation of systematic customer-to-image translation at scale.

Abstract: Personalized marketing can increase customer engagement, satisfaction, and conversion. While existing personalization approaches have become effective at matching the right product to the right customer, the visual representation of advertisements remains generic and only weakly tailored to the individual. Prior research shows that generative artificial intelligence can improve the creation of personalized advertisements, particularly for text, and that image generation models can support scalable advertisement production. However, little research has examined how detailed customer information can be systematically translated into fully AI-generated, personalized advertising imagery at scale on a technical level. To address this gap, we propose AdMan, a multi-agent pipeline that transforms customer data into personas, generates personalized advertisement images conditioned on product reference images, and applies an LLM-based judge agent for automated quality control. We implement the pipeline with two different model configurations and evaluate it across four products, using six celebrity personas for qualitative inspection, and 100 real customer profiles, producing 1745 advertisements. The evaluation combines a qualitative expert focus group and a quantitative artifact-rate assessment. The results show that the pipeline can generate photorealistic and personalized advertisements. At the same time, performance varies substantially by product complexity and model configuration. Our findings extend the literature on AI-based personalized advertising by demonstrating the feasibility and current limitations of fully automated image generation for advertising.

</details>


### [147] [Enabling and Understanding Personalization in AI-Generated Advertising Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12697)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Claudius Budcke,Claas Christian Germelmann,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Personalized marketing traditionally matches static products to customers, while dynamic creative optimization focuses mainly on AI-driven text personalization or basic product image modifications. We address this gap by developing and implementing an AI-based framework that generates personalized advertising imagery directly from customer data. We evaluate this framework in a two-stage within-subject study with N=100 participants across four products and three levels of personalization, varied by the amount and specificity of customer data used. Participants rated each image on attitude toward the advertisement, attitude toward the product, and purchase intention. Results show that participants perceive differences across personalization levels and evaluate AI-generated advertising imagery most positively at a moderate level of personalization. High personalization increases perceived personalization, which is positively associated with all three outcome measures, but also increases perceived creepiness, which is negatively associated with the outcomes and dominates the total effect.

</details>


### [148] [Implicit Personality Representations in Humans and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12704)
*Yilin Geng,Omri Abend,Eduard Hovy,Lea Frermann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study tests whether a large language model (Qwen 2.5-7B-Instruct) reproduces the consistent relational structure of personality traits that humans share across cultures. By comparing a human implicit-personality matrix (from millions of crowd-sourced ratings) with a model-based matrix (from contrastive activations), the researchers found strong alignment (Mantel r = 0.77). The model's trait representations recover the two dominant dimensions of human personality impressions: social warmth and intellectual competence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A century of psychology research has shown that while trait words vary across languages and cultures, the underlying relational structure among traits is remarkably consistent. This study investigates whether large language models capture this shared human structure in their internal representations, which would have implications for understanding AI personality understanding and human-AI alignment.

Method: The researchers built two matrices: (1) a human implicit-personality matrix from millions of crowd-sourced personality ratings of fictional characters across hundreds of traits, and (2) a matching matrix from contrastive model activations over the same traits. They compared these structures using Mantel correlation and validated on held-out dialogue by projecting model activations onto the recovered personality dimensions.

Result: The two relational structures align strongly with a Mantel r = 0.77. The agreement holds both trait-by-trait and in aggregate. Two dominant axes of the model's trait representations successfully recover the social warmth and intellectual competence dimensions that are known to organize human personality impressions. On held-out dialogue, projecting model activations onto these directions yields personality profiles that agree with human ratings.

Conclusion: This work establishes a framework for comprehensive, human-grounded comparison between internal model trait geometry and the shared structure of human personality impressions. It demonstrates that LLMs can internalize the same relational structure of personality traits that humans share across cultures, opening new avenues for studying AI personality understanding and human-AI alignment.

Abstract: A century of psychology has found that the trait words people use to describe one another vary, but the relational structure among those traits, which ones go together and which oppose, is strikingly consistent across raters and cultures. We test whether the LLM (Qwen 2.5-7B-Instruct) reproduces this structure in its internal trait representations. From millions of crowd-sourced personality ratings of fictional characters, we build a human implicit-personality matrix over hundreds of traits; from contrastive model activations, we build a matching matrix over the same traits. The two relational structures align strongly (Mantel r = 0.77), and the agreement holds trait by trait as well as in aggregate. Two dominant axes of the model's trait representations recover the social and intellectual dimensions long known to organize human personality impressions, social warmth and intellectual competence. On held-out dialogue, projecting model activations onto these directions yields personality profiles that agree with human ratings. This work establishes a framework that enables comprehensive, human-grounded comparison between internal model trait geometry and the shared structure of human personality impressions.

</details>


### [149] [When Rubrics Fail: Hallucinations Reveal Blind Spots in Medical AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12718)
*Griffin Farrow,Lily Sijia Li,Jack Johnson,Tingyan Wang,Philip Torr,William Bolton,Fabio J. Fehr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether rubric-based evaluation methods adequately capture clinically relevant hallucination errors in medical LLMs. The authors find that current rubrics often miss medically significant errors, leaving scores unchanged even when dangerous hallucinations are present.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucinations in medical LLMs can undermine clinician trust, but it was unclear whether the leading evaluation approach (rubric-based assessment) actually captures clinically relevant errors that would matter in practice.

Method: The authors (1) studied rubric specificity in a controlled setting using MedHallu, (2) developed a taxonomy of medical hallucination types with a clinician-validated error-injection pipeline to create matched correct and error-injected responses, (3) tested across three major medical LLM benchmarks (HealthBench, HealthBench Professional, and LiveMedBench), and (4) explored a retrieval-based factuality check as a complementary evaluation approach.

Result: More specific rubrics better distinguish correct from hallucinated responses. Clinically relevant hallucinations are frequently missed by rubrics, often leaving scores unchanged. Rubrics work best when explicitly checking facts but fail for unexpected errors they don't anticipate. A preliminary retrieval-based factuality check recovered some rubric-blind errors.

Conclusion: Rubric scores alone are insufficient to establish clinical reliability for medical LLMs. Current evaluation methods have systematic blind spots that could undermine clinician trust and pose risks for clinical deployment.

Abstract: Hallucinations can undermine clinician trust in LLMs, making it important that evaluation methods capture clinically relevant errors. Rubric-based evaluation has become the leading approach for assessing LLMs in medicine, but it is unclear whether rubric scores reflect such errors. We first study this in a controlled setting using MedHallu, finding that more specific rubrics better distinguish correct from hallucinated responses. To test this systematically, we develop a taxonomy of medical hallucination types and a clinician-validated error-injection pipeline that creates matched correct and error-injected responses. Across HealthBench, HealthBench Professional, and LiveMedBench, our clinically relevant hallucinations are missed by rubrics, often leaving scores unchanged. We find that rubrics are most effective when explicitly checking facts, and are less effective for additional or unexpected errors they do not anticipate. A preliminary retrieval-based factuality check recovers some of the rubric-blind errors, suggesting a complementary approach. These findings reveal systematic blind spots in current medical evaluation of LLMs and suggest that rubric scores alone are insufficient to establish clinical reliability, potentially undermining clinician trust and confidence in clinical deployment.

</details>


### [150] [Skill Issue: Lessons from Optimizing Repository SKILLs for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12742)
*Mykhailo Kozyrev,Andrei Kozyrev,Anton Podkopaev*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses how to automatically generate effective documentation (SKILLs) for coding agents in repositories that lack benchmarks. The authors propose GEPA (Gradient-Evaluated Prompt Augmentation), which mines harder tasks from reverted merged pull requests and scores documents based on agent performance improvement. On three Kotlin repositories, GEPA achieves 4.9pp improvement while prior method SkillOpt only achieves 0.1pp.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on synthesizing documentation for coding agents relies on benchmarks that don't exist for bare repositories. Additionally, prior synthetic tasks are too small - capable agents can solve them without any document. This work aims to create harder, more realistic tasks and better document generation methods.

Method: The authors propose: (1) Mining harder tasks from merged pull requests reverted at a single frozen base commit, rather than using small synthetic tasks. (2) Scoring candidate documents by comparing agent performance WITH the document versus WITHOUT it. They compare their GEPA method against SkillOpt and a seed baseline on three Kotlin repositories.

Result: GEPA documents raise the score by 4.9 percentage points on average, while SkillOpt documents only improve by 0.1pp above the seed. The GEPA gain matches what prior work reports with the same optimizer. However, at the dataset size from a single repository, the gain cannot be separated from agent run-to-run variance - settling that would require more tasks than one repository's history yields. Subjectively, a maintainer found valuable project-specific knowledge in the GEPA-generated documents.

Conclusion: GEPA effectively generates useful documentation for coding agents by using harder tasks from actual repository history and measuring actual performance improvement. The 4.9pp gain is meaningful and matches prior work, though the dataset size limitation makes it hard to distinguish from variance. The documents are readable and contain project-specific knowledge that only comes from working in the project.

Abstract: Coding agents increasingly read repository knowledge from SKILLs --- plain \texttt{.md} files versioned alongside the code. Recent work synthesizes these files automatically, by optimizing the document against a benchmark. A bare repository comes with no benchmark, and the synthetic tasks prior work builds are small enough that a capable agent saturates them with no document at all. We mine harder tasks --- merged pull requests of the repository, reverted at a single frozen base commit; and score a candidate document by whether the same agent does better with it than without it. On three Kotlin repositories, the documents GEPA finds raise this score by $4.9$pp on average, and the ones SkillOpt finds leave it where it started, $0.1$pp above the seed. The GEPA gain matches what prior work reports with the same optimizer, and at the dataset size a single repository supplies it cannot be separated from the agent's run-to-run variance; settling that would take more tasks than one repository's history yields. The documents themselves read better than the score: a maintainer of one repository found in them knowledge one only gets by working in the project.

</details>


### [151] [Assisted Spatial Cognition Through Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12747)
*H. Riaz,J. B. Fernandez,I. Mills,D. Hickey,F. Cleary,M. I. Ali*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces an end-to-end assistive navigation framework combining LLMs, VLMs, and digital twin technologies to help visually impaired and neuro-divergent users navigate indoor/outdoor environments by generating dense 3D point clouds from monocular video and interpreting spatial contexts through AI models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the navigation challenges faced by 43+ million visually impaired and neuro-divergent individuals who lack comprehensive 3D scene understanding from existing navigation aids that rely on constrained route-based strategies.

Method: The system uses mobile phone cameras to capture video input, applies SLAM3R for real-time dense 3D point cloud generation from monocular RGB sequences, employs custom post-processing for multi-viewpoint alignment without predefined reference points, utilizes SpatialLM for structured 3D representations (architectural elements, oriented object bounding boxes), and processes enriched spatial data through a locally deployed LLM for scene descriptions and distance measurements.

Result: Evaluation across diverse video scenarios with various perspectives, looped walking views, and multiple environments demonstrates consistent accuracy in 3D scene interpretation and object localization.

Conclusion: The proposed system shows potential as a transformative assistive navigation solution combining advanced visual perception with spatial reasoning, though real-world deployment and user testing would further validate its practical utility.

Abstract: Multimodal AI, powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs), is transforming assistive technologies by enabling simultaneous processing of visual and textual data. This advancement holds significant promise for over 43 million visually impaired and neuro-divergent individuals worldwide who face persistent challenges in navigating indoor and outdoor environments due to limited spatial awareness and insufficient environmental cues. Existing navigation aids often lack comprehensive 3D scene understanding, relying on constrained route-based strategies that hinder user autonomy. In this paper, we introduce a novel end-to-end framework that integrates LLMs, VLMs and digital twin technologies to deliver a spatially cognitive navigation support for visually impaired and neuro-divergent users. Our system captures video input via standard mobile phone cameras, and employs SLAM3R to generate dense 3D point clouds from monocular RGB sequences in real-time. Our custom post-processing algorithm ensures accurate point cloud alignment across multiple viewpoints without requiring predefined reference points. This enhances the capabilities of SpatialLM to produce structured 3D representations, including architectural elements and oriented object bounding boxes. The enriched spatial data is then processed by a locally deployed LLM, which interprets 3D contexts to generate detailed scene descriptions and precise distance measurements between users and surrounding objects. We evaluated our approach across diverse video scenarios featuring various perspectives, looped walking views and captured in multiple environments. The evaluation results demonstrate consistent accuracy in 3D scene interpretation and object localisation, underscoring the potential of our system as a transformative assistive navigation solution that combines advanced visual perception with spatial reasoning

</details>


### [152] [SCQ: Stabilizing Conservative Q-Learning with Sigmoid-Bounded Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12749)
*Xiefeng Wu,Shu Zhang,Zhaojie Chu,Mingyu Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCQ introduces a sigmoid-bounded entropy term to address value estimation instability in offline-to-online reinforcement learning, where the standard log-entropy can become negative and destabilize policy updates. It retains conservative Q regularization and lower-bound calibration while ensuring the entropy term stays strictly positive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline-to-online RL reduces interaction costs for real-world robot learning but suffers from persistent value estimation instability. While existing methods address this through pessimistic regularization, lower-bound calibration, and architectural normalization, the entropy formulation itself has been overlooked as an instability source—the standard log-entropy term can become negative, destabilizing policy updates.

Method: SCQ replaces the standard log-entropy term with a sigmoid-bounded formulation that stays strictly positive. It retains conservative Q regularization and return-based lower-bound calibration to stabilize policy optimization without sacrificing exploration. Additionally, a direct clipping intervention removes negative log-probability contributions as a control.

Result: SCQ matches or exceeds baseline performance on D4RL benchmarks under both single-demonstration and standard dataset settings, exhibits more stable training dynamics across state-based and visual benchmarks, and successfully transfers to four real-robot platforms including manipulation, wheeled, quadruped, and humanoid systems. The positivity of the entropy term, rather than a particular score shape, drives much of the improvement.

Conclusion: The entropy formulation is an overlooked source of instability in offline-to-online RL. A simple sigmoid-bounded entropy term that remains strictly positive can significantly stabilize training while maintaining or improving performance across simulation, visual, and real-world robotic tasks. The core insight is that positivity rather than specific score shape drives the improvement.

Abstract: Offline-to-online reinforcement learning reduces interaction cost for real-world robot learning but suffers from persistent value estimation instability. Existing methods address this through pessimistic regularization, lower-bound calibration, and architectural normalization, but an overlooked source of instability lies in the entropy formulation: the standard log-entropy term can become negative, destabilizing policy updates. We introduce SCQ (Sigmoid-Bounded Conservative Q-Learning), which replaces this term with a sigmoid-bounded formulation that stays strictly positive. SCQ retains conservative Q regularization and return-based lower-bound calibration, stabilizing policy optimization without sacrificing exploration. We evaluate SCQ on D4RL (Minari) benchmarks under both single-demonstration and standard dataset settings, as well as on simulation and real-world visual tasks. SCQ matches or exceeds baseline performance while exhibiting more stable training dynamics across state-based and visual benchmarks, and transfers to four real-robot platforms including manipulation, wheeled, quadruped, and humanoid systems. A direct clipping intervention that removes negative log-probability contributions, together with gradient-matched positive-score controls, indicates that positivity rather than a particular score shape alone drives much of the improvement. Project website: https://scq-rl.github.io.

</details>


### [153] [Unified Agentic Video Editing Across Levels of Complexity and Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12769)
*Surabhi S. Nath,Kim Ferres,Milan Petrović,Lion Schulz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents agentic tooling for automated video editing across three tasks of increasing complexity: scene previews, video summaries, and cinematic trailers. The research evaluates these automated outputs and discusses implications for the future of automation and agency in video production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video editing is a core component of production requiring creative planning and decision-making under multiple constraints. The motivation is to explore how agentic AI tools can automate video editing across different editorial goals, complexity levels, and creativity requirements.

Method: The researchers developed methods for automated video editing agents and evaluated them across three distinct tasks: scene previews (simple), video summaries (moderate), and cinematic trailers (complex/creative). They assessed the outputs and analyzed implications for automation and agency.

Result: The paper reports methods for agentic tooling for automated video editing across the three tasks with varying editorial goals, complexity, and creativity. The outputs were evaluated, and implications for automation and agency in video production were discussed.

Conclusion: The research demonstrates the potential for agentic AI systems in automated video editing across different levels of complexity and creativity. It contributes to understanding the implications of automation in creative production workflows, raising questions about the role of human agency versus machine autonomy in creative decision-making.

Abstract: Editing is a core component of video production, requiring creative planning and decisions under multiple constraints. Here, we report methods for agentic tooling for automated video editing across three tasks varying in editorial goal, complexity and creativity, namely scene previews, video summaries and cinematic trailers. We evaluate the outputs and discuss implications for automation and agency.

</details>


### [154] [MPT: Missing Prototype Tracking via Barycentric Reconstruction in Vehicular Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12771)
*Hanju Jang,Gyeongmin Han,Sungmin Lee,Kichang Lee,Chunghan Lee,JeongGil Ko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MPT is a cross-vehicle federated learning framework that maintains rare-class recognition when vehicles depart before training converges, using privacy-preserving class-level statistics without requiring raw data or per-sample features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the problem of rare-class recognition degradation in cross-vehicle FL when departing vehicles hold most samples of a target class, making recovery difficult due to noisy prototype estimates and FL privacy constraints preventing centralized data access.

Method: MPT combines three components: (1) barycentric decomposition tracking drift shared with remaining-class prototypes, (2) covariance-based residual prediction estimating out-of-span drift, and (3) adaptive calibration weighing remaining rare-class samples by reliability.

Result: MPT outperforms all baselines in rare class F1, achieving 0.516 on nuImages dataset with only 1% of rare-class samples remaining, without raw data, per-sample features, or retraining.

Conclusion: MPT successfully maintains rare-class recognition in cross-vehicle FL by reconstructing class prototypes from privacy-preserving statistics, effectively handling vehicle departure scenarios while preserving data privacy.

Abstract: Cross-vehicle federated learning enables vehicles to collaboratively improve perception models while keeping locally collected driving data private. However, vehicle participation is transient, and a vehicle may depart before training converges while permanently taking its local data. When this departing vehicle holds most samples of a target class, the class becomes rare in the remaining FL network, and its recognition can silently degrade as the shared backbone continues to evolve. Recovering the class is difficult since the few remaining samples provide a noisy prototype estimate, while FL privacy constraints prevent centralized access to raw data or per-sample features. This paper presents MPT, a cross-vehicle FL framework that maintains rare-class recognition by reconstructing its prototype at every round from privacy-preserving class-level statistics. MPT combines a barycentric decomposition that tracks drift shared with remaining-class prototypes, a covariance-based residual prediction that estimates out-of-span drift, and an adaptive calibration that weighs the remaining rare-class samples according to their reliability. We evaluate MPT on three vehicle classification tasks and four backbones against representative calibration and drift-compensation baselines. MPT outperforms all baselines in rare class F1, reaching 0.516 on the nuImages dataset with only 1\% of rare-class samples remaining, without raw data, per-sample features, or retraining.

</details>


### [155] [Interpreting the predictions of neural network classification based on a Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12801)
*Markus Klute,Artur Monsch,Lars Sowa,Roger Wolf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA), a method to characterize how input features influence neural network predictions for event classification, using Taylor expansion. The approach is demonstrated on simple examples and a CERN LHC experiment application, with conclusion that second-order terms are sufficient for typical particle physics classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop a rigorous and comprehensive method for neural network introspection—specifically, to understand what properties of input features X lead to specific predictions ŷ in event classification tasks, particularly for high-energy physics experiments like CERN LHC.

Method: The method uses Taylor expansion of the neural network's prediction ŷ in terms of the input features X. This allows decomposition of the network's behavior into contributions from different orders (first-order, second-order, etc.) of feature interactions. The paper presents different levels of introspection using this approach.

Result: The paper demonstrates TCA on two simple benchmark tasks to build intuition, then applies it to a complex CERN LHC classification task. The key result is that properties of X most relevant for tasks of typical CERN LHC complexity are captured adequately by TCA up to second order.

Conclusion: The conclusion supports the assumption that second-order Taylor terms are sufficient to capture the most relevant properties of input features for particle physics classification tasks, making TCA a practical and interpretable tool for neural network analysis in high-energy physics.

Abstract: We introduce a rigid and comprehensive taxonomy and paradigm for characterizing the influence of the input feature space $X$ on the predictions $\hat{y}$ of a neural network (NN) used for event classification, based on a Taylor expansion of $\hat{y}$ in $X$. The complete process of introspection we refer to as Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA). Based on two simplistic example tasks, which can be easily understood and bencmarked, we illustrate the power of the TCA when it comes to revealing, what properties of $X$ have led to what value of $\hat{y}$, of a given NN model, building up intuition for the method. A more complex application is meant to represent $X$ of a typical classification task at a CERN LHC experiment. Based on this application, we play through the different levels of introspection that the TCA offers and discuss a number of practical aspects for a TCA application typical for the analysis of CERN LHC data. We conclude with a study to support the assumption that those properties of $X$ most relevant for tasks of the complexity typical for a CERN LHC experiment, are usually caught by a TCA up to the second order.

</details>


### [156] [K-Bench: A Benchmark for LLM Unlearning in Agentic Deployments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12808)
*Guangsheng Yu,Yanna Jiang,Qin Wang,Baihe Ma,Xu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: K-Bench is a benchmark that evaluates LLM unlearning under agentic deployment, revealing that traditional benchmarks (TOFU, MUSE) fail to detect knowledge leakage when models are deployed as ReAct agents. The benchmark inspects six channels (CoT, tool calls, tool observations, summary) and shows that secrets can leak through channels other than the final answer, making current unlearning methods ineffective in real-world deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current unlearning benchmarks only certify forgetting by checking the model's final answer, but this certificate doesn't transfer when the model is deployed as an agent. This creates a false sense of security about knowledge removal.

Method: K-Bench places secrets in one of three sources (weights, prompt, or retrieval store) and evaluates a ReAct agent across six channels. A query counts as leaked if the secret appears in any channel. The K-Score is computed separately for each source and only credits forgetting when the agent remains usable.

Result: 1) Clearing the answer channel doesn't prevent secret recovery. 2) On structured retrieval, secrets stay verbatim in tool-observation channel with unchanged leak rate. 3) When secrets are in prompt or retrieval store, TOFU/MUSE show no leakage but agent leaks 22-86% of queries. 4) None of 20 evaluated methods remove weight-based secrets. 5) Only input-corruption achieves selective forgetting. 6) Top-ranked method varies across base models. 7) Refusal-tuning resists extraction without verified knowledge removal.

Conclusion: Current machine unlearning methods are insufficient for agentic deployment settings. The community needs new benchmarks and methods that account for the multiple channels agents expose. A refusal-tuning method may resist extraction without actually removing knowledge, highlighting the need for verification.

Abstract: Unlearning benchmarks such as TOFU and MUSE certify forgetting by reading the model's final answer, where a model that refuses to answer already counts as having forgotten. We show that this model-level certificate does not transfer once the model is deployed as an agent. We introduce K-Bench, a benchmark that scores LLM unlearning under agentic deployment. K-Bench inspects all six channels a ReAct agent exposes, including its chain-of-thought (CoT), tool calls and tool observations, and elicited summary. A query counts as leaked if the secret appears in any of them. Each experiment places the secret in exactly one of the agent's three sources (the weights, the prompt, or the retrieval store). The K-Score is computed separately for each source and credits forgetting only when the agent remains usable. Clearing the answer channel does not make the secret unrecoverable. On structured retrieval, the secret stays verbatim in the tool-observation channel and the aggregate leak rate is unchanged. When the secret lives in the prompt or the retrieval store, TOFU and MUSE report no leakage, while the deployed agent still leaks it on 22--86\% of queries. When the secret is in the weights, none of the twenty evaluated published methods demonstrably removes it, and only an input-corruption intervention reaches selective forgetting under the evaluated observer. The top-ranked method changes across base models. A refusal-tuning method resists the evaluated extraction without verified knowledge removal.

</details>


### [157] [Scaling Clinical Judgment to Evaluate Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12822)
*Thomas A. Buckley,Zahir Kanjee,Peter G. Brodeur,Byron Crowe,Anthony M. Pettinato,Aashna P. Shah,Adrian D. Haimovich,Liam G. McCoy,Daniel Restrepo,Ethan Goh,Jonathan H. Chen,Laura Zwaan,Katherine E. Goodman,Daniel J. Morgan,Raja-Elie E. Abdulnour,Adam Rodman,Arjun K. Manrai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces PrecepTron, an LLM fine-tuned using LoRA for physician-level evaluation of clinical reasoning, along with GRAND-ROUNDS, a large-scale benchmark of 9,217 physician-annotated scores. The study addresses the scalability problem of human physician evaluation in assessing LLM clinical reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blinded physician evaluation is the gold standard for assessing clinical reasoning in LLMs but is difficult to scale. Prior studies rely on small physician panels from single institutions/specialties, limiting scientific questions and reproducibility. The authors seek a more rigorous and scalable method to study clinical reasoning in AI models.

Method: The authors trained PrecepTron using low-rank adaptation (LoRA) of a 32-billion-parameter model on a small number of physician examples. They also created GRAND-ROUNDS, a benchmark with 9,217 scores by 11 physicians across seven studies. They compared PrecepTron's evaluations with frontier LLMs using standard "LLM-as-a-judge" approaches and physician panels.

Result: Frontier LLMs in typical "LLM-as-a-judge" approaches often disagree with physicians and with each other. Fine-tuning PrecepTron on a small number of cases enables physician-level consistent scoring across tasks. PrecepTron successfully reproduced headline findings from five influential studies in JAMA, Science, and Nature Medicine without new human grading. The method also enables new questions about LLM reasoning (e.g., measuring diagnostic accuracy when clinical cases are provided piecemeal) that would be infeasible with human grading alone.

Conclusion: PrecepTron and GRAND-ROUNDS provide a foundation for reproducible, large-scale study of how LLMs reason in medicine. The approach addresses limitations of prior small-scale physician evaluations and enables both replication of existing findings and exploration of new research questions about LLM clinical reasoning.

Abstract: Blinded physician evaluation has been considered by many to be the gold standard for assessing clinical reasoning in large language models (LLMs). This is difficult to scale; thus, prior studies typically rely on small physician panels, often from a single institution or specialty, which both limits the scientific questions investigated and makes it unclear whether findings would be reproduced with a different set of evaluators. To more rigorously and scalably study clinical reasoning in AI models, here we introduce PrecepTron, an LLM fine-tuned for physician-level evaluation of open-ended responses. PrecepTron was trained using low-rank adaptation (LoRA) of a 32-billion-parameter model on a small number of physician examples. We also release GRAND-ROUNDS, a new large-scale physician-annotated benchmark of 9,217 scores by 11 physicians across seven studies. We show that frontier LLMs in typical "LLM-as-a-judge" approaches often disagree with physicians and with each other, but fine-tuning PrecepTron on a small number of cases enables physician-level consistent scoring across tasks. We use PrecepTron to reproduce headline findings from five influential studies assessing LLMs for clinical care in JAMA, Science, and Nature Medicine without new human grading. Using PrecepTron, we then pose new questions about how LLMs reason in medicine that would have been infeasible with human grading alone, including measuring the diagnostic accuracy of frontier LLMs when clinical cases are provided piecemeal, even token by token. Together, PrecepTron and GRAND-ROUNDS provide a foundation for reproducible, large-scale study of how LLMs reason in medicine. All code, data, and labels are made freely available for researchers.

</details>


### [158] [MedRoundsQA: A Persona and Difficulty Aware Evaluation for Multi-Turn Medical Consultations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12851)
*Youssef Mohamed,Ahmed Heakl,Qinrong Cui,Junhong Liang,Rafiq Ali,Bdour Babillie,Nazira Dunbayeva,Lang Gao,Omar Hussein,Ahmed Nada,Ahmed Mohamed Magdy Mohamed,Jinghui Liu,Salman Khan,Imran Razzak,Yuxia Wang,Xiuying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedRoundsQA is a multi-turn diagnostic benchmark with 1,387 board-exam cases across 17 specialties, converting cases into structured clinical records and doctor-patient dialogues under varying patient personas. Evaluation of 15 LLM agents reveals significant performance gaps between single-turn and multi-turn settings, diminishing returns in diagnostic accuracy after 6-12 turns, and equity risks from patient persona differences affecting diagnosis accuracy by 7-8 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical benchmarks are dominated by single-turn, multiple-choice clinical cases that poorly reflect real consultations. In practice, clinicians elicit evidence interactively and patient communication varies widely, but existing benchmarks fail to capture these dynamics.

Method: 1,387 board-exam cases across 17 specialties were converted into structured 24-slot clinical records, then instantiated as controlled doctor-patient dual-agent dialogues under varying patient personas while holding clinical content fixed. Cases were classified by difficulty using model-based uncertainty. Fifteen LLM doctor agents were evaluated.

Result: (i) Moving from single-turn diagnosis on standardized records to multi-turn consultations causes large performance degradations of roughly 13-39 points; (ii) more turns reliably improves question relevance, but diagnostic accuracy exhibits diminishing returns and typically plateaus after 6-12 turns; (iii) patient persona differences can shift diagnosis accuracy by about 7-8 points (lowest to highest education), highlighting equity risks that single-turn benchmarks miss.

Conclusion: Multi-turn clinical consultations are substantially harder than single-turn benchmarks for LLM doctors. Current benchmarks overestimate real-world performance. Patient communication variations (e.g., education level) create equity risks in diagnostic accuracy, which single-turn benchmarks fail to capture. The study calls for more realistic, multi-turn benchmark design to better evaluate clinical AI systems.

Abstract: Medical benchmarks are dominated by single-turn, multiple-choice clinical cases that poorly reflect real consultations. Practically, clinicians elicit evidence interactively and patient communication varies widely. We introduce MedRoundsQA, a multi-turn diagnostic benchmark derived from 1,387 board-exam cases across 17 specialties. Each case is converted into a structured 24-slot clinical record, and then instantiated as controlled doctor-patient dual-agent dialogues under varying patient personas, with the underlying clinical content held fixed. We further classify cases by difficulty using model-based uncertainty to enable easy-to-hard analysis. Evaluations of fifteen LLM doctor agents show that (i) moving from a single-turn diagnosis on the standardized records to multi-turn consultations causes large degradations of roughly 13-39 points; (ii) more turns reliably improves question relevance, but diagnostic accuracy exhibits diminishing returns and typically plateaus after 6-12 turns; and (iii) patient persona differences can shift diagnosis accuracy by about 7-8 points (lowest to highest education), highlighting equity risks that single-turn benchmarks miss.

</details>


### [159] [Tracing and Coordinating Cross-Layer Influence for Multimodal Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12897)
*Pengyang Zhou,Xiaobin Tu,Zhengxi Liu,Rongkun Xue,Haochen Li,Miancan Liu,Ziyuan Chen,Yinggui Wang,Jinkui Ren,Xiantao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TAC-Merge is a method for multimodal model merging that consolidates task experts into a single model while retaining their complementary capabilities. It addresses the challenge of cross-layer influence when visual and language expert updates are combined, where earlier updates affect later ones through the representation pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to solve two key challenges in multimodal model merging: (1) characterizing how individual expert updates influence multimodal representations across depth, and (2) jointly combining expert updates based on their multimodal influence. Existing methods combine expert updates within individual layers but fail to account for the cascading effects where later updates operate on inputs already modified by earlier ones.

Method: TAC-Merge consists of two modules: (1) Multimodal Influence Mapping (MIM) - constructs graphs of update effects and uses Ricci curvature together with expert predictions to define a shared fusion objective; (2) Coupled Merge Control (CMC) - models interactions among coefficient adjustments and jointly optimizes regional weights to synthesize one shared model.

Result: Experiments across diverse multimodal tasks demonstrate TAC-Merge's effectiveness in consolidating complementary expert capabilities and supporting generalization to unseen tasks.

Conclusion: TAC-Merge provides a principled approach for tracing and coordinating cross-layer influence in multimodal model merging, enabling successful consolidation of visual and language expert updates while maintaining performance across various tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal model merging aims to consolidate task experts into a single model that retains their complementary capabilities. Most unimodal model merging methods combine expert updates within individual layers, and multimodal approaches largely follow this design. However, an expert update changes the representations passed to subsequent layers, allowing its influence to propagate across depth and affect how visual and textual information interact. When visual and language updates are combined, later updates act on inputs already modified by earlier ones, coupling their effects. This poses two challenges: (1) how to characterize the multimodal influence of individual expert updates across depth, and (2) how to jointly combine expert updates based on their multimodal influence. To address these challenges, we propose TAC-Merge for tracing and coordinating cross-layer influence in multimodal model merging. It contains two modules, i.e., multimodal influence mapping (MIM) and coupled merge control (CMC). MIM constructs graphs of update effects and uses Ricci curvature together with expert predictions to define a shared fusion objective. CMC models interactions among coefficient adjustments and jointly optimizes regional weights to synthesize one shared model. Experiments across diverse multimodal tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of TAC-Merge in consolidating complementary expert capabilities and supporting generalization to unseen tasks.

</details>


### [160] [EduFair-Bench: Evaluating Pedagogical Fairness of LLM Tutors Across Student Demographics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12949)
*Jiaxu Zhao,Bahar Radmehr,Fares Fawzi,Tanya Nazaretsky,Tanja Käser*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EduFair-Bench is a benchmark designed to audit pedagogical fairness of LLM tutors by testing whether tutoring quality varies systematically with student demographics (gender, immigration background, first language, SES). The study finds that model capability and demographic fairness are largely orthogonal, with smaller models being more consistent and more capable models showing wider demographic gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are increasingly deployed as tutors, but it is unclear whether they support all students equally well. There is a need to audit whether tutoring quality varies systematically with student demographics (pedagogical fairness).

Method: EduFair-Bench pairs a multi-domain question bank (mathematics, physics, chemistry) with a controlled simulation where a fixed LLM student interacts with each tutor across nine demographic levels spanning four dimensions (gender, immigration background, first language, SES). Tutoring quality is scored on five turn-level pedagogical metrics and four conversation-level dimensions using an LLM judge validated against three-annotator consensus on 180 tutor turns. Bias is measured via paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests and bootstrap effect-size confidence intervals. Two ablations are conducted: demographic cues conveyed through names and conflicting demographic information between tutor and student.

Result: Across five tutors: (1) model capability and demographic fairness are largely orthogonal - the smallest model is the most consistent while the four more capable tutors all exhibit wide demographic gaps with no clear capability-to-fairness ordering; (2) pedagogy-specific RL training redistributes rather than removes bias; (3) language- and immigration-related cues produce larger gaps than gender- and SES-related cues.

Conclusion: The study reveals that current LLM tutors exhibit demographic bias in pedagogical quality, and improving model capability does not necessarily improve fairness. This highlights the need for targeted fairness interventions in LLM tutor deployment.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as tutors, but it is unclear whether they support all students equally well. We introduce \textbf{EduFair-Bench}, a benchmark for auditing the pedagogical fairness of LLM tutors---whether tutoring quality varies systematically with student demographics. EduFair-Bench pairs a multi-domain question bank (mathematics, physics, chemistry) with a controlled simulation in which a fixed LLM student interacts with each tutor across nine demographic levels spanning four dimensions: gender, immigration background, first language, and socioeconomic status (SES). Tutoring quality is scored on five turn-level pedagogical metrics and four conversation-level dimensions, using an LLM judge validated against three-annotator consensus on 180 tutor turns. Bias is measured via paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests and bootstrap effect-size confidence intervals. Two ablations (demographic cues conveyed through names; conflicting demographic information between tutor and student) disentangle tutor-driven from student-driven bias. Across five tutors, we find that model capability and demographic fairness are largely orthogonal: the smallest model is the most consistent while the four more capable tutors all exhibit wide demographic gaps with no clear capability-to-fairness ordering, pedagogy-specific RL training redistributes rather than removes bias, and language- and immigration-related cues produce larger gaps than gender- and SES-related cues.

</details>


### [161] [How Good Are Frontier Models at Physics? Expert Re-Grading Reveals Broken Evaluations and Near-Saturation of Leading Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13009)
*Ali Ansari,Haoran Sun,Andy Zeyi Liu,Mark Jabbour,Yongshan Ding,Steven Girvin,Yu He,Sohrab Ismail-Beigi,Aleksander Kubica,Owen D. Miller,Corey O'Hern,Vidvuds Ozolins,David Poland,A. Douglas Stone,Frank C. van den Bosch,Logan Wright,Navid Akbari,Santanu Antu,Kangle Cai,Andrew Calabrese-Day,Mateo Cárdenes Wuttig,Meng Cheng,Barry T. Chiang,Ali Ghorashi,Shouzhen Gu,Haoyang Huang,Zhibo Kang,Lukas Kienesberger,Hantian Liu,Charles Lomba,Zhongling Lu,Wenchao Ma,Rohin E. McIntosh,Evan McKinney,Ivan Rojkov,Xulei Sun,Yarone Meir Tokayer,Naveen Balaji Umasankar,Mira Varma,Leda Wang,Qimin Wang,Tyler Wang,Haoyu Wei,Jinming Yang,Jinchen Zhao,Sherlock Tingrui Zhao,Qinyuan Zheng,Jay S. Zou,Lucas Baker,Arman Cohan,John Sous*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reveals that frontier language models perform significantly better on physics benchmarks than originally reported, after expert auditing identifies benchmarking issues (grader errors, incorrect reference solutions, ambiguous questions) rather than genuine model errors. GPT-5.6-Sol's scores substantially improve after correction (e.g., 47.3%→78.7% on HLE-Physics), suggesting current benchmarks understate model capabilities and need expert validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The reported low scores on physics benchmarks conflict with domain experts' practical experiences, suggesting potential issues with benchmark quality rather than model limitations.

Method: Evaluated frontier models on six physics benchmarks; experts (faculty and graduate researchers) reviewed problem statements, reference solutions, and model responses to distinguish genuine model errors from benchmarking issues (grader errors, incorrect reference solutions, ambiguous questions).

Result: After expert correction: GPT-5.6-Sol's mean@4 rises from 47.3% to 78.7% on HLE-Physics, 61.0% to 87.2% on CMT-Benchmark, and reaches 94.4% on 54 retained CritPt challenges. Scores on UGPhysics, PRISM-Physics, and PHYBench also increase substantially.

Conclusion: Current physics benchmarks substantially understate frontier models' ability to solve well-posed physics problems. Near-saturation on closed-ended tasks highlights the need for more demanding, expert-validated evaluations.

Abstract: Low reported scores on leading physics benchmarks, including those featured in the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index (2026), suggest that frontier language models still struggle with advanced physics, a demanding test of their scientific reasoning and quantitative problem-solving abilities. Yet this impression does not always align with domain experts' experiences using these models in their work. We revisit these reported findings by evaluating frontier models on six widely used physics benchmarks and auditing them with experts, focusing on text-only problems with verifiable final answers. For each subfield of physics, faculty and graduate researchers with relevant expertise carefully review problem statements, reference solutions, and model responses to distinguish genuine model errors from grader errors, incorrect reference solutions, and ambiguous or underspecified questions. Most audited cases initially evaluated as incorrect reflect these benchmarking issues rather than errors in the models' physics reasoning. We then ask experts to address these benchmarking issues by correcting erroneous reference solutions and repairing or excluding flawed questions. We find that GPT-5.6-Sol's measured mean@4 rises from 47.3% to 78.7% on HLE-Physics and from 61.0% to 87.2% on CMT-Benchmark, while its corrected pass@4 reaches 94.4% on the 54 retained CritPt challenges. Corrected scores are computed on the retained evaluation subsets following expert review. Scores on the audited subsets of UGPhysics, PRISM-Physics, and PHYBench also rise substantially after correction. These findings suggest that current benchmarks substantially understate frontier models' ability to solve well-posed physics problems. Near-saturation on these closed-ended tasks highlights the need for more demanding, expert-validated evaluations.

</details>


### [162] [Diffusion Models and Concept Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13047)
*Zekun Wang,Karthik Singaravadivelan,Christopher J. MacLellan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that diffusion models (designed for image synthesis) implicitly perform the same cognitive computation as Cobweb (a classic cognitive model of concept formation) - both organize knowledge into a taxonomy with a "basic level" at which categorization requires least cognitive effort. The authors test this correspondence on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To connect two seemingly unrelated fields - cognitive science (Cobweb) and deep learning (diffusion models) - by demonstrating they perform the same underlying computation of concept formation with a basic level emergence.

Method: Theoretical analysis comparing the two models' properties, followed by empirical validation on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST by recovering the diffusion hierarchy through mode-finding and comparing basic levels.

Result: The authors find correspondence in four respects: both are hierarchical density models, both are hierarchical-Bayesian with Gaussian prototypes, both treat categorization as score-following reducing uncertainty, and a basic level emerges in both. The basic level in diffusion models is located at an intermediate noise level.

Conclusion: Diffusion models can be reframed as cognitive models of concept formation, while Cobweb gains a continuous, scalable instantiation through the diffusion framework. This bridges cognitive science and deep learning.

Abstract: Humans organize knowledge into a taxonomy of concepts with nested levels of abstraction and a \emph{basic level} at which people recognize and name objects with the least cognitive effort. Cobweb is a classic cognitive account of this ability, an incremental learner that builds a probabilistic concept hierarchy by maximizing category utility. We argue that diffusion models, although designed for image synthesis, implicitly perform the same computation. The noisy marginals of a diffusion model are Gaussian smoothings of the data distribution, and the modes of these marginals form a hierarchy that corresponds to a Cobweb tree of probabilistic prototypes in four respects. Both are hierarchical density models, both are hierarchical-Bayesian models with Gaussian prototypes, both treat categorization as score-following that reduces uncertainty, and in both a basic level emerges. We locate this basic level for a diffusion model at an intermediate noise level, where recent analyses show that the reverse process commits to the class identity of a sample. The two models differ mainly in how they represent and learn the taxonomy. Cobweb learns a discrete tree incrementally, whereas a diffusion model encodes a continuous, interpolable hierarchy in a single learned score field fit to the data distribution. We test the correspondence on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST by recovering the diffusion hierarchy through mode-finding and comparing the basic levels of the two models. This reframes diffusion as a cognitive model of concept formation and offers Cobweb a continuous, scalable instantiation.

</details>


### [163] [Anchoring Clinical Events in Time: UID-Preserving Multimodal Reconstruction and Source-Grounded Adjudication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13062)
*Sayantan Kumar,Nicolas Grimaldi,Jack Cummins,Jeremy C. Weiss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a UID-preserving framework for clinical timeline extraction that links narrative events to source spans and maintains identity through multiple processing stages. The system includes GAVEL, an LLM-based judge for evaluating timeline accuracy. Testing across 6 open-weight models on 40 critical care summaries showed GLM 5.2 multimodal revision achieved 43% improved event recovery while maintaining temporal agreement, performing competitively with clinician annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical timelines are essential for treatment-window analysis and modeling, but discharge summaries often obscure chronological information while structured EHR tables capture only partial patient courses. Existing methods lack proper event provenance and temporal grounding.

Method: The framework preserves UID (Unique Identifier) through text-only estimation, structured-evidence retrieval, timestamped source-row grounding, and joint revision. GAVEL serves as an LLM judge comparing UID-aligned timelines against narrative and structured records. Evaluation used 6 open-weight models on 40 mixed-critical-care summaries, with ablations and human review validation.
